### [1] [Derivative-Informed Dimension Reduction of Stochastic Differential Equations: A Path-Space Sensitivity Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33210)
*Joanna Zou,Han Cheng Lie,Youssef Marzouk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a derivative-informed method to find low-dimensional structure in the drift-to-observable map of SDEs, generalizing active subspaces to infinite-dimensional drift perturbations and path-space observables, with certified error bounds and a martingale control variate for efficient estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertainty in the SDE drift propagates to uncertainty in path-space observables (expected functionals), making uncertainty quantification costly. Existing dimension-reduction tools like active subspaces do not directly handle infinite-dimensional drift perturbations or path-space observables.

Method: Drift uncertainty is modeled as random perturbations in a Hilbert space. A path-space sensitivity operator is built from first-order Fréchet derivatives of the observable w.r.t. the perturbation, and its leading eigenfunctions are shown (under stated conditions) to give directions of maximal mean-squared sensitivity. A martingale control variate of the Fréchet derivative reduces estimator error, and a Galerkin scheme is used for discretization.

Result: Certified error bounds are established for the low-dimensional approximation, and the derivative-informed basis yields more accurate approximations of mean first hitting times than a Karhunen-Loève expansion of the same truncation order.

Conclusion: The derivative-informed, active-subspace-style reduction provides a principled and practically accurate way to lower the effective dimension of drift uncertainty for path-space observables, improving tractability of UQ for SDEs.

Abstract: Uncertainty in the drift of a stochastic differential equation (SDE) induces uncertainty in statistical observables defined as expected functionals on path space. We propose a derivative-informed method for identifying low-dimensional structure in the drift-to-observable map, with the aim of improving the tractability of uncertainty quantification. This approach can be viewed as a generalization of the active subspace method to infinite-dimensional input spaces and to path-space observables of SDEs. Representing uncertainty in the drift by random perturbations in a Hilbert space, we construct a path-space sensitivity operator using first-order Fréchet derivatives of the observable with respect to the perturbation. We establish conditions under which the leading eigenfunctions of this operator correspond to directions of maximum mean squared sensitivity of the observable, thus enabling low-dimensional approximation with certified error bounds. We further introduce a martingale control variate of the Fréchet derivative that reduces error in sample-based estimators of the operator. Using a Galerkin scheme for numerical discretization, we show that reduced representations of the drift from the derivative-informed basis yield more accurate approximations of mean first hitting times of SDEs than the Karhunen-Loève expansion of the same truncation order.

</details>


### [2] [Hybridizable Discontinuous Galerkin methods for the Keller-Segel model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33260)
*Silvia Bertoluzza,Gabriella Bretti,Micol Pennacchio,Christophe Prud'homme*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The authors propose and analyze a hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) finite element method for the time-dependent Keller-Segel chemotaxis drift-diffusion model, proving convergence of the scheme and confirming theoretical results with numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The time-dependent Keller-Segel model, a drift-diffusion system describing chemotactic cell movement, requires accurate and efficient numerical discretization; HDG methods offer attractive properties (reduced degrees of freedom, static condensation) that motivate their application here.

Method: Develop a hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) finite element scheme for the time-dependent Keller-Segel drift-diffusion system, and carry out a theoretical analysis establishing convergence of the proposed discretization.

Result: Theoretical convergence results are established for the HDG discretization, and numerical experiments are presented that confirm the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The HDG method is a valid and theoretically justified approach for approximating solutions of the time-dependent Keller-Segel model, with convergence supported by both analysis and numerical evidence.

Abstract: We propose a hybridizable discontinuous Galerkin (HDG) finite element method to approximate the solution of the time-dependent Keller-Segel model, a drift-diffusion system describing chemotactic cell movement. We develop and analyze the corresponding HDG scheme, and establish theoretical convergence results for the proposed discretization. Finally, we present numerical experiments that confirm the theoretical findings.

</details>


### [3] [Robust augmented Block Preconditioning for two-phase Incompressible Inductionless MHD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33385)
*Bijie Song,Lingxiao Li,Jie Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a robust augmented block preconditioner for a high-order monolithic ALE-FEM discretization of 3D two-phase incompressible inductionless MHD equations, solving the resulting large dual saddle-point system with preconditioned FGMRES.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fully coupled monolithic ALE-FEM discretization of two-phase inductionless MHD yields a large-scale, ill-conditioned, nonsymmetric, indefinite dual saddle-point system that is very hard to solve efficiently, especially with discontinuous physical parameters and varying mesh sizes.

Method: A high-order charge-conservative ALE finite element method (variables: current density, velocity, electric potential, pressure) with grad-div stabilization and backward Euler time discretization. A constraint-preconditioning-framework augmented block preconditioner combines augmented Lagrangian techniques with approximate block factorization for the current-density and fluid sub-systems; the coupled four-field system is solved with preconditioned FGMRES.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm high-order accuracy and discrete charge conservation of the ALE-FEM. The heavy-fluid-drop-in-light-fluid benchmark demonstrates the preconditioner's robustness with respect to discontinuities in physical parameters and mesh size.

Conclusion: The proposed augmented block preconditioner is robust and effective for the fully coupled four-field two-phase inductionless MHD system, handling parameter jumps and mesh refinement well.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop a robust augmented block preconditioner for three-dimensional, two-phase, incompressible inductionless MHD equations. We employ a high-order monolithic and charge-conservative arbitrary Lagrangian--Eulerian (ALE) finite element method as base scheme, for which the solution variables are current density, velocity, electric potential and pressure. Grad-div stabilization is added in both the current density equation and discrete momentum equation. Backward Euler method is used for the time discretization. At each time step, fully coupled solution strategy is adopted. This leads to large-scale, ill-conditioned, nonsymmetric, and indefinite dual saddle-point system. To solve this system efficiently, we construct an augmented block preconditioner within the constraint preconditioning framework. The ultimate preconditioner combines the augmented Lagrangian technique with approximate block factorization for current density sub-system and fluid sub-system. The fully coupled four-field system is then solved using preconditioned FGMRES iteration. Numerical experiments confirm the high-order accuracy and discrete charge conservation of the ALE-FEM. Drop of heavy fluid in light fluid is taken as benchmark to demonstrate the robustness of the preconditioner with respect to the discontinuity of physical parameters and the mesh size.

</details>


### [4] [Optimization-based Cell Average Limiters as Metric Projections: Constrained Evolution and Convergence Analysis for Convection-diffusion Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33449)
*Seungmin Lee,Sanghyun Lee,Chen Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes convergence of optimization-based cell average limiting in DG discretizations, showing the limiter is a backward Euler proximal step in a Lie splitting, proving nonexpansiveness/stability in L2 and non-increase of total variation, yielding O(τ+h^{k+1}) and O(τ+h^k) error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The optimization-based cell average limiter is widely used (phase-field models, compressible Navier-Stokes) to enforce global conservation and bounds, yet its convergence behavior has lacked a rigorous theoretical grounding. The authors aim to analyze convergence of the resulting scheme.

Method: Using heat and convection-diffusion equations as prototypes, they encode conservation and admissibility in a differential inclusion, identifying the limiter as a backward Euler proximal step within a Lie splitting of the constrained evolution. This step is the metric projection onto the convex admissible set; they prove it is nonexpansive and L2-stable and does not increase the TV of cell averages, then combine with standard error analysis.

Result: Under a reverse CFL condition, nonexpansiveness yields an O(τ+h^{k+1}) estimate for implicit Euler with degree-k DG, plus an unconditional O(τ+h^{k}) estimate. Numerical experiments confirm predicted convergence rates and bound enforcement.

Conclusion: The limiting scheme is provably convergent with established rates and preserves the structural properties (conservation, bounds, stability, non-increasing total variation) underpinning the optimization-based limiter.

Abstract: The optimization-based cell average limiter enforces global conservation and bounds, and has been successfully applied to discontinuous Galerkin (DG) discretizations of a wide range of nonlinear systems, from phase-field models to the compressible Navier-Stokes equations. In this paper, taking the heat and convection-diffusion equations as prototypes, we analyze the convergence of the scheme resulting from cell average limiting. Encoding conservation and admissibility in a differential inclusion identifies the limiter as a backward Euler proximal step in a Lie splitting of the constrained evolution. This proximal step is the metric projection onto the convex admissible set, and we prove that it is nonexpansive and stable in the $L^2$ norm, and that it does not increase the total variation of the cell averages. Combined with the standard error analysis, nonexpansiveness yields an $O(τ+h^{k+1})$ estimate for implicit Euler time stepping with DG discretization of polynomial degree $k$ under a reverse CFL condition, together with an unconditional $O(τ+h^{k})$ estimate. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rates and the enforcement of the bounds.

</details>


### [5] [An improved fast iterative moment method for the simulation of near-continuum gas flows with Shakhov model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33537)
*Zhenning Cai,Zhicheng Hu,Guanghan Li,Chunwu Wang,Xiaowen Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends the fast iterative moment (FIM) solver to the Shakhov model for near-continuum gas flows, improving spatial discretization and hydrodynamic correction to enhance efficiency and robustness at small Knudsen numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original FIM method achieves fast convergence for Boltzmann-BGK flows via alternating iterations, but needs extension to the Shakhov model and better handling of slow hydrodynamic convergence, especially at small Knudsen numbers and on refined grids.

Method: A finite volume method with linear reconstruction is used for both systems. The high-order moment system uses an implicit-explicit scheme treating the Shakhov collision term implicitly. A local Fourier analysis motivates a Newton-multigrid method with a new regularization strategy for the hydrodynamic equations, providing effective correction in one step per alternating iteration.

Result: Numerical experiments on planar Couette flow, lid-driven cavity flow, and bottom-heated cavity heat transfer show improved efficiency and robustness over the original FIM method, particularly at small Knudsen numbers, with good performance for both first- and second-order discretizations.

Conclusion: The improved FIM solver extends FIM to the Shakhov model and enhances hydrodynamic correction via Newton-multigrid, reducing empirical tuning and improving convergence for near-continuum flows.

Abstract: The fast iterative moment (FIM) method developed in [G. Li, C. Wang, and Z. Hu, Comput. Fluids, 311 (2026), 107031] achieves fast convergence for near-continuum gas flows modeled by the Boltzmann-BGK equation through alternating iterations between the high-order moment system and the consistent hydrodynamic equations. In this paper, we extend the FIM solver to the Shakhov model and improve it in the following aspects. A finite volume method with linear reconstruction is employed for high-resolution spatial discretization of both systems. Following the original FIM method, an implicit-explicit scheme treating the Shakhov collision term implicitly is adopted for the high-order moment system. A local Fourier analysis of a linearized one-dimensional BGK model reveals the need for more hydrodynamic iterations at smaller Knudsen numbers and their slower convergence on refined grids. Motivated by these findings, a Newton-multigrid method with a new regularization strategy is introduced for the hydrodynamic equations, generally providing an effective hydrodynamic correction with one step per alternating iteration, thereby reducing the need for empirical tuning. Numerical experiments for planar Couette flow, lid-driven cavity flow, and heat transfer in a bottom-heated cavity are carried out to investigate the performance of the improved FIM solver. The results demonstrate improved efficiency and robustness over the original FIM method, particularly at small Knudsen numbers, and favorable performance with both first- and second-order spatial discretizations.

</details>


### [6] [Inverse problems with fractional operators: an analytical study and its numerical linear algebra treatment via the GLT theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33763)
*Asim Ilyas*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This thesis leverages Generalized Locally Toeplitz (GLT) theory to analyze matrix sequences arising from regularized inverse problems for fractional differential equations, construct structure-preserving preconditioners, and extend the GLT algebra to nonlinear operations—proving maximal distribution results for geometric means of GLT sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional differential equations model memory and nonlocal effects but their inverse problems (recovering sources, coefficients, or initial conditions from noisy indirect data) are ill-posed in the Hadamard sense and require regularization. Discretization yields large linear systems with nonlocal structure whose asymptotic spectral properties are poorly understood, hindering efficient solvers and preconditioners.

Method: Uses GLT theory as a unified framework for the spectral distribution of large structured matrices. Applies quasi-boundary value regularization for stable reconstruction, analyzes matrix sequences from discretized inverse problems, constructs structure-preserving preconditioners, and studies nonlinear operations in the GLT algebra. Proves maximal distribution results for geometric means of GLT sequences, handling both commuting (symbol invertibility not required) and noncommuting degenerate cases.

Result: Establishes maximal distribution results for geometric means of GLT sequences: when symbols commute, the geometric mean sequence remains GLT without requiring symbol invertibility, resolving a long-standing conjecture; maximality is also shown in the noncommuting degenerate case.

Conclusion: The thesis integrates regularization, spectral analysis, and preconditioning into a coherent computational framework for inverse problems in nonlocal fractional dynamics, while also enriching structured matrix analysis through new GLT algebraic results.

Abstract: Fractional derivatives model memory effects and nonlocal interactions beyond classical integer-order models. Fractional differential equations (FDEs) thus describe anomalous diffusion, transport in heterogeneous media, and hereditary dynamics. Their nonlocality, however, makes inverse problems challenging: unknown sources, coefficients, or initial conditions must be recovered from indirect and noisy data. Inverse problems for FDEs are usually ill-posed in the Hadamard sense, so regularization, such as the quasi-boundary value method, is required for stable reconstruction. Discretization produces large linear systems whose matrices inherit the nonlocal structure of fractional operators. Understanding the asymptotic spectral properties of these matrix sequences is essential for efficient solvers and preconditioners. Generalized Locally Toeplitz (GLT) theory provides a unified framework for the spectral distribution of large structured matrices and for preconditioner design. This thesis uses GLT theory to analyze matrix sequences from inverse problems for FDEs, construct structure-preserving preconditioners, and study nonlinear operations in the GLT algebra. In particular, it proves maximal distribution results for geometric means of GLT sequences: when the symbols commute, the geometric mean sequence remains GLT without symbol invertibility. This resolves a long-standing conjecture and shows maximality in the noncommuting degenerate case. Overall, the thesis integrates regularization, spectral analysis, and preconditioning into a coherent computational framework for inverse problems in nonlocal fractional dynamics, while enriching structured matrix analysis.

</details>


### [7] [On the stability of second-order methods for DDEs and PDDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33780)
*Javier Rodríguez-Gutiérrez,Jesús Martín-Vaquero*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A one-parameter family of second-order IMEX multistep methods is studied for delay differential equations, with stability analyzed via field-of-values arguments for constant grid-aligned delays and via Lyapunov/slow-variation conditions for variable delays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Delay differential equations require stable numerical integrators; the paper aims to establish unconditional stability conditions for this IMEX multistep family, including handling non-grid-aligned and genuinely variable delays.

Method: Analysis of the matrix problem y'=Ay+B y(t-τ(t)) with A Hermitian negative definite and B arbitrary. For constant grid-aligned delays, a field-of-values argument gives unconditional stability when a generalized field of values F_p(A,B) lies in the open unit disk. Quadratic interpolation is examined for constant non-grid-aligned delays. For variable delays, the method is recast as a nonautonomous augmented system, with product-level sufficient conditions from a common quadratic Lyapunov inequality and a quantitative slow-variation hypothesis.

Result: Unconditional stability holds under the F_p(A,B) condition for grid-aligned delays; quadratic interpolation does not shrink the stability disk for non-grid-aligned delays; sufficient conditions are derived for variable delays. Examples include DDEs and semidiscrete parabolic partial delay differential equations with diffusion.

Conclusion: The developed theory provides stability results for second-order IMEX multistep methods applied to DDEs and illustrates their applicability to delay partial differential equations.

Abstract: In this paper, a one-parameter family of second-order implicit--explicit (IMEX) multistep methods is studied for delay differential equations (DDEs). The analysis is based on the matrix problem $y'(t)=A y(t) + B y(t-τ(t))$, where $A$ is Hermitian negative definite but $B$ is any matrix. For constant, grid-aligned delays, a field-of-values argument yields unconditional stability whenever a suitable generalized field of values $F_p(A,B)$ is contained in the open unit disk. For a constant non-grid-aligned delay, we show that quadratic interpolation does not reduce the corresponding stability disk. For genuinely variable delays, the method is formulated as a nonautonomous augmented system, and product-level sufficient conditions are obtained from a common quadratic Lyapunov inequality and from a quantitative slow-variation hypothesis.
  The approach developed herein is illustrated through several examples in which the discussed theory is applied not only to DDEs, but also to semidiscrete parabolic problems given by partial delay differential equations with a diffusion term.

</details>


### [8] [The Exact Dihedral Angle Sum Range for Circumcentral-Cevian Jaw Tetrahedra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33783)
*Sergey Korotov*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper determines the exact range of the dihedral-angle sum over the "special jaw" component of tetrahedra whose cevians to opposite-face circumcenters concur; the minimum is uniquely the regular tetrahedron and the maximum is only approached in a degenerate limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hajja, Hammoudeh, Hayajneh and Martini proved that such a concurrence condition forces the tetrahedron to be either a kite or a special jaw, giving an abstract structural classification. However, a quantitative geometric characterization — specifically how the dihedral-angle sum varies within the jaw family and where its extremes lie — was left open. The paper fills this gap by pinning down the exact range of this invariant.

Method: The family of jaw tetrahedra is reduced to a single scale-invariant parameter, capturing the whole shape up to similarity. The dihedral-angle sum is then expressed as a function of this parameter, and an explicit monotonicity argument is used to locate and identify its endpoints.

Result: The exact dihedral-angle-sum range on the jaw component is obtained. The lower endpoint is attained if and only if the tetrahedron is regular; the upper endpoint is never attained in a non-degenerate tetrahedron but is approached only in the degenerate limit.

Conclusion: The concurrence condition's jaw family admits a clean one-parameter description with a strictly monotone dihedral-angle sum, yielding sharp range bounds: a unique regular minimizer and a supremum realized only degenely. This completes the earlier classification with an explicit quantitative picture.

Abstract: Hajja, Hammoudeh, Hayajneh and Martini showed that a tetrahedron whose cevians to the circumcenters of the opposite faces are concurrent is either a kite or a special jaw. We determine the exact dihedral-angle-sum range on the jaw component. The lower endpoint is attained only by the regular tetrahedron, while the upper endpoint is approached only in the degenerate limit. The proof reduces the family to one scale-invariant parameter and an explicit monotonicity argument.

</details>


### [9] [An optimization-based property-preserving limiter for two-phase flow in porous media on non-matching meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33940)
*Sanghyun Lee,Seungmin Lee,Chen Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A bound-preserving, anti-oscillation postprocessing limiter for DG discretizations of two-phase flow in porous media on non-matching meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Saturation transport approximation can violate the maximum principle and produce spurious oscillations, even when solutions stay within physical bounds; existing limiter approaches lack guarantees on non-matching meshes.

Method: Combines an optimization-based cell average limiter (enforcing bounds on cell averages while preserving global mass by minimizing L2 distance), a minmod limiter activated only in cells flagged by a troubled-cell detector, and a Zhang-Shu scaling limiter enforcing bounds on point values; proven to preserve accuracy at fixed time on non-matching meshes.

Result: Proof that the cell average limiter preserves accuracy of the numerical solution at a fixed time on non-matching meshes; numerical experiments on homogeneous, non-homogeneous, and fractured media show accuracy and robustness.

Conclusion: The proposed bound-preserving anti-oscillation limiter effectively suppresses oscillations while maintaining mass conservation and accuracy for DG two-phase flow simulations on non-matching meshes.

Abstract: Two-phase flow in porous media arises in a wide range of applications in engineering and science. A fundamental challenge in its numerical simulation is that the approximation of the saturation transport equation can violate the maximum principle, and spurious oscillations can appear even when the solution remains within physical bounds. In this work, we propose a bound-preserving anti-oscillation postprocessing limiter for discontinuous Galerkin discretizations of the two-phase flow system on non-matching meshes. An optimization-based cell average limiter enforces the prescribed bounds on the cell averages while preserving global mass by minimizing the $L^2$ distance to the original cell averages. To suppress the remaining oscillations, the cell average limiter is combined with a minmod limiter, which is activated only in the cells identified by a troubled-cell detector to avoid unnecessary loss of accuracy, and a Zhang-Shu scaling limiter subsequently enforces the bounds on point values. In addition, we prove that the cell average limiter preserves the accuracy of the numerical solution at a fixed time on non-matching meshes. Numerical experiments on homogeneous, non-homogeneous, and fractured porous media demonstrate the accuracy and robustness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [10] [The Calderón problem for the multi-fractional Laplacian Schrödinger equation with drift terms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34077)
*Jin Wen,Yun-Long Liu,Jun-Liang Fu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper solves Calderón-type inverse problems for a multi-fractional Laplacian Schrödinger equation with drift, recovering drift, potential, and source from Dirichlet-to-Neumann data, proving global uniqueness and developing a numerical reconstruction framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse problems for multi-fractional (mixed-order) nonlocal operators with drift terms remain largely unexplored, despite existing theory for single-fractional or drift-free models. Recovering both coefficients and sources from boundary measurements in this richer setting is the gap the paper addresses.

Method: Uses well-posedness of the forward problem, a strong unique continuation principle, Runge-type approximation in fractional Sobolev and weighted C^k spaces, and generalized Alessandrini identities to establish global uniqueness. Numerically combines finite difference discretization, radial basis function surrogate modeling, and hierarchical Bayesian inversion.

Result: Global uniqueness is established: for f=0, both drift b and potential q are recovered from the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map; for f≠0, b, q, and source f are simultaneously determined. 1D numerical examples with smooth and discontinuous coefficients and sources confirm effectiveness and support the theory.

Conclusion: The work extends Calderón inverse theory to multi-fractional Laplacian Schrödinger operators with drift, providing rigorous uniqueness results and a practical Bayesian-based numerical scheme, with empirical validation in one dimension.

Abstract: This paper studies the Calderón inverse problem for the multi-fractional Laplacian Schrödinger equation with drift terms, whose leading operator is a sum of fractional Laplacians of different orders, perturbed by a drift vector field and a potential. We consider two inverse settings: in the homogeneous source case \(f=0\), we aim to simultaneously recover the drift coefficient \(b\) and the potential \(q\) from the associated Dirichlet-to-Neumann map; in the non-homogeneous case \(f\neq 0\), we aim to simultaneously determine \(b\), \(q\), and the source term \(f\). Under suitable assumptions, we establish the well-posedness of the forward problem, derive a strong unique continuation principle, prove Runge-type approximation properties in fractional Sobolev spaces and weighted \(C^k\) spaces, and obtain generalized Alessandrini identities. These tools yield global uniqueness results for the inverse problems. In addition, we develop a numerical reconstruction framework combining finite difference discretization, radial basis function surrogate modeling, and hierarchical Bayesian inversion. Numerical examples in one dimension for smooth and discontinuous coefficients and sources illustrate the effectiveness of the proposed method and support the theoretical uniqueness results.

</details>


### [11] [Convergence Analysis of Variable-Separation-Based Model Reduction for Linear Parametric Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34139)
*Liang Chen,Qiuqi Li,Yusong Ye,Pingwen Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves that the variable-separation (VS) model reduction method converges almost surely to the exact solution of linear parametric PDEs in the parameter-averaged energy norm, with explicit error bounds under finite-term separated representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The VS method is a practical greedy approach for solving parametric PDEs, but its theoretical convergence guarantees were previously lacking or incomplete, motivating a rigorous convergence analysis.

Method: Proves almost-sure convergence under symmetry, uniform coercivity, and uniform boundedness of bilinear forms plus finite-dimensional approximation conditions on the error family. For operators/RHS with finite-term separated representations, constructs finite-dimensional approximation spaces and bounds their dimensions/errors; also analyzes the fixed finite-dimensional solution family case.

Result: Establishes an O((log N)^{-1}) bound on the expected parameter-averaged error energy after N iterations; sharper O(N^{-1}) when solutions lie in a fixed finite-dimensional space; and exact recovery almost surely in finitely many iterations when the operator is parameter-independent. Numerical experiments confirm error decay and finite-step recovery.

Conclusion: The VS model reduction method is rigorously shown to converge with quantifiable rates, validating its use and providing theoretical guarantees for parametric PDE solution approximation.

Abstract: This paper analyzes the convergence of a variable-separation (VS) model reduction method that successively constructs separated approximations to the solutions of linear parametric partial differential equations (PDEs) by solving residual problems at sampled parameter values. We prove that the resulting VS approximations converge almost surely to the exact parametric solution in the parameter-averaged energy norm, under finite-dimensional approximation conditions on the error family and the assumptions of symmetry, uniform coercivity, and uniform boundedness of the governing bilinear forms. For parametric PDEs whose operators and right-hand sides admit finite-term separated representations, we construct finite-dimensional approximation spaces for the error family and bound their dimensions and approximation errors, thereby verifying these approximation conditions and obtaining an $O((\log N)^{-1})$ upper bound for the expected parameter-averaged error energy after $N$ iterations. When the solution families are contained in a fixed finite-dimensional space, we obtain the sharper $O(N^{-1})$ bound for the expected error energy and, if the operator is also parameter-independent, exact recovery for almost every parameter after an almost surely finite number of iterations. Numerical experiments illustrate the error decay of the VS approximations, as well as finite-step recovery in the parameter-independent case.

</details>


### [12] [An efficient method for reconstructing the potential in the fractional Calderón problem with a single measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34175)
*Yun-Long Liu,Jin Wen,Jun-Liang Fu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a numerical method to recover the potential function in 1D and 2D fractional-order Calderón problems from a single measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional Calderón problems are challenging inverse problems; determining the potential from limited (single) measurement requires efficient forward modeling and robust inversion.

Method: The fractional Laplacian is discretized via finite differences. A POD-RBF surrogate model is built for the forward problem, supplying predicted values and exact gradients. The inverse problem is solved with Tikhonov regularization and the conjugate gradient method.

Result: Numerical experiments demonstrate efficiency and effectiveness of the method, and verify the theoretical results.

Conclusion: The proposed POD-RBF-based approach with Tikhonov regularization and CG provides an effective numerical tool for fractional Calderón potential reconstruction in 1D and 2D.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop a numerical method for determining the potential function in one-dimensional and two-dimensional fractional-order Calderón problems through a single measurement. The fractional Laplacian is discretized using the finite difference method, and the POD-RBF method is used to construct the forward problem surrogate model that can provide the predicted value and its exact gradient in time. The inverse problem is solved using Tikhonov regularization and the conjugate gradient method. The efficiency and effectiveness of the developed method are demonstrated through numerical experiments, and the theoretical results are verified.

</details>


### [13] [Numerical Construction of Quasi-Periodic Solutions for Nonlinear PDEs: II. Unbounded Perturbation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34194)
*Mingwei Fu,Bin Shi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The authors propose alternating numerical schemes to construct quasi-periodic solutions for nonlinear PDEs with unbounded perturbations (spatial-derivative nonlinear terms), such as the NLAW and KdV equations, showing that the bad-frequency measure stays small despite polynomial growth factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear PDEs from physical models often contain unbounded perturbations (nonlinear terms with spatial derivatives), which obstruct standard numerical construction of quasi-periodic solutions. These perturbations are compositions of unbounded operators with bounded nonlinear maps, complicating measure estimates and resonance analysis.

Method: Apply inverse differential operators as preconditioning to make perturbations bounded (at the cost of rescaling the linear part by polynomial factors in spatial mode n). Use alternating numerical schemes for the NLAW and KdV equations. Control the measure of bad frequencies, and exploit a key observation that resonances cannot occur when dispersive frequencies grow polynomially with degree >1 and the temporal index magnitude is below a threshold proportional to |n|^(degree-1); treat the complementary regime separately and bound the counting loss.

Result: The measure of excluded (bad) frequency sets remains small despite polynomial enlargement in n. Resonances at large scales are shown to be impossible below a threshold, and at each scale at most one spatial index has a fully "bad" temporal box, yielding only a polynomial counting loss absorbable into the exponential-logarithmic threshold.

Conclusion: Control over bad-frequency measures is retained, enabling the alternating schemes to construct quasi-periodic solutions for unbounded-perturbation PDEs like NLAW and KdV.

Abstract: Many nonlinear partial differential equations (PDEs) arising in physical models involve unbounded perturbations, where nonlinear terms contain spatial derivatives. Such perturbations can be expressed as compositions of unbounded operators with bounded nonlinear maps. In this paper, we propose alternating numerical schemes for constructing quasi-periodic solutions to this class of PDEs, including the nonlinear acoustic wave (NLAW) equation and the Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation. To render the nonlinear perturbations bounded, we apply inverse differential operators as a preconditioning; this, however, also rescales the linear part by polynomial factors in the spatial mode $n$. Two main difficulties arise. The first is to control the measure of the resulting ``bad'' frequencies, since the bounds on the measures of the excluded frequency sets are enlarged by polynomial factors in $n$; we show that this measure nevertheless remains small. The second concerns resonances involving the perturbed eigenvalues of the linearized operator at large scales. Our key observation is that, when the dispersive frequencies grow polynomially in $n$ with degree greater than one, such resonances cannot occur if the magnitude of the temporal index lies below a threshold proportional to $|n|$ raised to one less than that degree. It therefore suffices to consider the complementary regime, in which the growth induced by the unbounded perturbation can be controlled in terms of the temporal index. At large scales, an additional complication arises: for a given spatial index $n$, all temporal indices in a box may be ``bad''. We show, however, that at each scale there is at most one such spatial index. Consequently, the resulting counting loss is only polynomial and can be absorbed into the exponential-logarithmic threshold, allowing us once again to retain control over the measures of the excluded frequency sets.

</details>


### [14] [ESPC-Transolver: An Equivalent-Surface Physics-Guided Coupling-Aware Transolver for 3D Multiple Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34246)
*Guoqing Zhao,Liang Li,Xing Li,Hao Kuang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: ESPC-Transolver is an equivalent-surface, physics-guided, coupling-aware Transolver that improves cross-geometry generalization for 3D acoustic multiple-scattering prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometry-conditioned multiple-scattering surrogates struggle to generalize to unseen geometries, since they must infer scattering properties from geometric features while capturing complex phase-sensitive interactions from repeated wave exchange.

Method: The model separates local scattering-response representations from inter-object interaction modeling. For each sound-hard scatterer, canonical scattered-pressure responses are built under shared spherical-wave excitations; a local Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and modal scattering conversion express them on an enclosing sphere as physically consistent input channels. Shared object-local Transolver blocks encode responses and incident-field traces, while relative-position-aware slice attention models inter-object coupling. Predicted interface pressure and normal flux define equivalent sources for reconstructing exterior near- and far-field scattering.

Result: On 3D acoustic multiple-scattering problems at fixed frequency, ESPC-Transolver achieves lower scattered-field prediction errors than geometry-conditioned predictors and alternative neural network backbones on held-out instances of familiar geometry families, and generalizes effectively to previously unseen Peanut and Torus geometries. Exterior-field reconstructions reproduce multiple-scattering interference patterns.

Conclusion: Linking cross-geometry interface prediction to equivalent-source-based near-field pressure and far-field scattering recovery yields an effective physics-guided framework for cross-geometry generalization in multiple-scattering surrogates.

Abstract: Cross-geometry generalization is particularly challenging because geometry-conditioned multiple-scattering surrogates must infer unseen objects' scattering properties from geometric features while accurately capturing complex, phase-sensitive interactions arising from repeated wave exchange. We introduce ESPC-Transolver, an equivalent-surface physics-guided, coupling-aware Transolver that separates local scattering-response representations from inter-object interaction modeling. For each sound-hard scatterer, canonical scattered-pressure responses are constructed under a shared set of spherical-wave excitations. A local Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) map and modal scattering conversion express these responses on an enclosing sphere, providing physically consistent input channels across geometries. Shared object-local Transolver blocks encode these responses and incident-field traces, while relative-position-aware slice attention models inter-object coupling. Predicted interface pressure and normal flux recovered from the local operator define equivalent sources for reconstructing exterior near- and far-field scattering. We evaluate the method on three-dimensional acoustic multiple-scattering problems at a fixed frequency, comparing it with geometry-conditioned predictors and alternative neural network backbones. ESPC-Transolver achieves lower scattered-field prediction errors on held-out instances of familiar geometry families and generalizes effectively to previously unseen Peanut and Torus geometries. Exterior-field reconstructions reproduce multiple-scattering interference patterns, linking cross-geometry interface prediction to near-field pressure and far-field scattering recovery.

</details>


### [15] [Recovering fractal boundary dimensions from Robin eigenvalues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34284)
*Habib Ammari,Yat Tin Chow,Fuqun Han*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops quantitative spectral and geometric-inverse results for Robin Laplacians where a small local boundary coefficient competes with the growing surface measure of a finite-depth fractal approximation, yielding eigenvalue expansions, uniform ground-state asymptotics, and recovery of Minkowski dimensions from boundary measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robin problems with vanishing local boundary coefficient interacting with fractal-approximation surface measures lack quantitative control of eigenvalue remainders and a rigorous link between ground-state measurements and fractal dimensions; the paper aims to fill this gap.

Method: Layer-potential Green function and eigenpair expansions with contour residues and finite-rank reduction (fixed smooth domains); uniform Poincaré inequality and normalized-surface trace estimates giving uniform ground-state expansion and rank-one resolvent singularity (varying Lipschitz domains); boundary-resolution hypotheses to recover lower/upper Minkowski dimensions for bounded total Robin mass; operator limits under compact Mosco convergence.

Result: Quantitative spectral-gap-dependent eigenvalue remainder; uniform ground-state expansion and rank-one resolvent singularity as total Robin boundary mass → 0; recovery of lower and upper Minkowski dimensions from ground-state measurements; characterization of Neumann, generalized Robin, and Dirichlet-type operator limits; Koch snowflake computations validating reconstruction, with an exact finite-stage area correction improving the adjacent-stage dimension estimator.

Conclusion: The framework connects small-coefficient Robin asymptotics to fractal surface geometry, enabling dimension reconstruction and precise operator limits, illustrated and refined numerically on Koch snowflakes.

Abstract: We study Robin problems in which a small local boundary coefficient competes with the increasing surface measure of a finite-depth fractal approximation. For a fixed smooth domain, layer potentials give Green function and eigenpair expansions; contour residues and a finite-rank reduction yield a quantitative spectral-gap-dependent eigenvalue remainder. For varying Lipschitz domains, a uniform Poincaré inequality and a trace estimate with respect to normalized surface measure give a uniform ground-state expansion and a rank-one resolvent singularity as the total Robin boundary mass tends to zero. Under the boundary-resolution hypothesis and the condition on the approximation scales, ground-state measurements with known Robin coefficients recover the lower and upper Minkowski dimensions for sequences with bounded total Robin boundary mass. We describe Neumann, generalized Robin, and Dirichlet-type operator limits under the corresponding compact Mosco convergence hypotheses. Koch snowflake computations illustrate the reconstruction formulas, and an exact finite-stage area correction improves the adjacent-stage dimension estimator.

</details>


### [16] [Lie Symmetry-Guided Adaptive Sampling for Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34345)
*Runze Zhao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes Lie-AS-ISC, a Lie symmetry-driven adaptive sampling framework for physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) that improves accuracy by exploiting symmetry transformations to construct basis points and combining absolute error with invariant surface conditions for adaptive sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs are highly sensitive to training point sampling, and conventional uniform sampling fails to resolve regions with complex solution features, causing low efficiency and limited accuracy.

Method: For PDEs admitting Lie symmetry groups, symmetry transformations derive intrinsic functional relationships between the solution and its variables, constructing basis points with exact solutions spanning the domain. An adaptive sampling strategy jointly considers absolute error and invariant surface conditions, with a dual-index loss to strengthen training constraints.

Result: The method significantly improves prediction accuracy and consistently outperforms existing methods in accuracy across experiments.

Conclusion: Lie symmetry-driven adaptive sampling offers an effective approach to enhance PINN efficiency and accuracy for PDEs possessing symmetry groups.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) have emerged as a promising mesh-free framework for solving partial differential equations (PDEs), with broad applications in physics, engineering, and medicine. However, their performance is highly sensitive to the sampling strategy for training points. Conventional uniform sampling often fails to adequately resolve regions with complex solution features, leading to low efficiency and limited accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose a Lie symmetry-driven adaptive sampling framework for PINNs, termed Lie-AS-ISC. For PDEs admitting Lie symmetry groups, symmetry transformations are exploited to derive intrinsic functional relationships between the solution and its variables, enabling the construction of basis points with exact solutions that span the solution domain. Building on this, an adaptive sampling strategy is developed by jointly considering absolute error and invariant surface conditions, while a dual-index loss is introduced to enhance training constraints. The proposed method significantly improves prediction accuracy. The experimental results show that this method consistently outperforms existing methods in accuracy.

</details>


### [17] [Second-Order Steps, Spectral Accuracy: Discrete Parallel Transport of Invariant Subspaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34494)
*Hanwen Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Discrete parallel transport only aligns eigenbases to second order, yet the resulting frames can be interpolated with spectral (geometric) accuracy for analytic families of invariant subspaces sampled at Chebyshev nodes, and the true continuous parallel-transported frame is recovered via a small matrix ODE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eigenvector bases are determined only up to a unitary gauge, so bases computed at different parameter values cannot be directly interpolated. Discrete parallel transport (via polar factors of consecutive overlaps) resolves this but only approximates the continuous parallel-transported frame to second order, raising the question of whether such frames can still be interpolated accurately.

Method: Analyze discrete parallel transport for analytic families of invariant subspaces sampled at N+1 Chebyshev nodes. Show the computed frames are exact samples of an analytic frame differing from the parallel-transported frame by a gauge within O(N^{-2}) of the identity uniformly on a fixed complex neighborhood. Use Chebyshev interpolation for the frame, and solve a small matrix differential equation to recover the continuous parallel-transported frame.

Result: Chebyshev interpolation of the discrete-parallel-transport frames converges geometrically (spectrally). The continuous parallel-transported frame is recovered to spectral accuracy with only a small matrix ODE, requiring no extra eigensolves or parameter-derivatives of the matrix family.

Conclusion: Despite the second-order local accuracy of discrete parallel transport, global Chebyshev interpolation achieves spectral accuracy. Results hold for non-Hermitian families and through eigenvalue crossings and branch points provided the selected invariant subspace is analytic in the parameter. Numerical examples confirm predicted convergence rates.

Abstract: Eigensolvers return orthonormal bases of invariant subspaces, but each basis is determined only up to a unitary factor, so bases computed at different parameter values cannot, in general, be interpolated directly. Discrete parallel transport resolves this ambiguity by aligning consecutive bases through the polar factors of their overlaps, but approximates the continuous parallel-transported frame only to second order. We show that the resulting frames can nevertheless be interpolated with spectral accuracy. For analytic families of invariant subspaces sampled at $N+1$ Chebyshev nodes, the computed frames are exact samples of an analytic frame related to the parallel-transported frame by a gauge that remains within $O(N^{-2})$ of the identity throughout a fixed complex neighborhood, uniformly in $N$. Consequently, Chebyshev interpolation approximates this analytic frame with geometric convergence. Moreover, the continuous parallel-transported frame can be recovered to spectral accuracy by solving a small matrix differential equation, without additional eigensolves or derivatives of the underlying matrix family. The results do not require the family to be Hermitian and remain valid through eigenvalue crossings and branch points, provided the selected invariant subspace depends analytically on the parameter. Numerical examples confirm the predicted rates.

</details>


### [18] [Energy stability of explicit--implicit--null time discretizations for nonlinear degenerate parabolic equations with state-dependent coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34539)
*Yue Wu,Chi-Wang Shu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper analyzes the explicit-implicit-null (EIN) time-stepping method for nonlinear degenerate parabolic equations with state-dependent coefficients and a scalar energy potential, proving unconditional nonlinear energy stability for first- to third-order schemes with various spatial discretizations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit time stepping suffers from severe time step restrictions, while fully implicit treatment of the nonlinearity requires costly Newton-Raphson iterations. The EIN method aims to overcome both drawbacks, but stability guarantees for such degenerate nonlinear problems across multiple orders and discretizations were lacking.

Method: The authors use an orthogonal projection of the Kirchhoff transform to prove nonlinear energy stability for first- to third-order EIN time stepping coupled with spatial discretizations admitting self-adjoint positive semidefinite discrete operators. Under an upper bound on the diffusion coefficient over relevant stage segments, they derive two IMEX coefficient conditions that yield sufficient stabilization bounds with no time step restriction.

Result: The estimates dissipate the original convex energy and permit degenerate diffusion without any time step restriction. The analysis is extended to diffusion systems with a scalar energy potential in a fixed positive definite metric, and to consistent positive-weight quadrature discretizations.

Conclusion: EIN schemes of order one to three are shown to be nonlinearly energy-stable for a broad class of degenerate parabolic problems, with applications including porous-medium flow, nonlinear heat transfer in plasma, and multicomponent diffusion.

Abstract: We study nonlinear degenerate parabolic equations with state-dependent coefficients admitting a scalar energy potential. The explicit--implicit--null (EIN) method avoids severe time step restrictions of explicit time stepping and costly Newton--Raphson iterations required by a fully implicit treatment of the nonlinearity. Using orthogonal projection of the Kirchhoff transform, we prove nonlinear energy stability for first- to third-order EIN time stepping coupled with a wide range of spatial discretizations that admit self-adjoint positive semidefinite discrete operators. Under an upper bound for the diffusion coefficient on relevant stage segments, two IMEX coefficient conditions yield sufficient stabilization bounds without any time step restriction. The estimates dissipate the original convex energy and allow degenerate diffusion. We also extend the analysis to diffusion systems with a scalar energy potential in a fixed positive definite metric, and to consistent positive-weight quadrature discretizations. Applications include porous-medium flow, nonlinear heat transfer in plasma, and multicomponent diffusion.

</details>


### [19] [Dynamics-Aware Sampling and Recovery for Tensor-Valued Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34621)
*Seok-Young Chung,Longxiu Huang,Yisen Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies sampling and recovery of tensor-valued dynamical systems governed by the t-product from partial spatial observations over time, establishing deterministic identifiability conditions via aggregated spectral blocks and providing recovery guarantees, stability analysis, and empirical validation through a Tensor Dynamical Sampling (Tensor DS) method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although the t-product Fourier transform turns tensor dynamics into slicewise matrix dynamics, recovery does not decouple across slices: eigenvalues shared across Fourier-domain frontal slices couple eigenspaces into aggregated spectral blocks, which govern the tensor-specific recovery geometry and space-time tradeoff. The authors aim to characterize these conditions and guarantees.

Method: They derive deterministic identifiability conditions for the initial tensor state as frame conditions on aggregated blocks (diagonalizable case), extended to non-diagonalizable systems via generalized eigenspaces and Jordan structure. A temporal-spectral factorization of the finite-horizon stacked recovery system underlying Tensor DS yields a decoupled two-stage formulation after the temporal threshold. Under independent spatial subsampling, they prove high-probability recovery guarantees using empirical Gram matrices on aggregated blocks.

Result: Bounds depend on aggregated multiplicities, incoherence, and population conditioning. Stability estimates separate the spatial frame factor from the temporal Vandermonde factor, showing that deeper temporal sampling can worsen numerical conditioning even after identifiability. Experiments show phase transitions matching predicted blockwise thresholds, and on sea surface temperature and turbulence velocity data the learned t-product operator yields more accurate, structurally faithful reconstructions than vectorized low-rank, Tucker, graph-regularized, graph-spectral, and vectorized dynamical sampling baselines.

Conclusion: The aggregated-block perspective provides a tensor-specific theory of sampling and recovery for t-product dynamical systems, unifying identifiability, probabilistic guarantees, and conditioning, and is validated practically by the Tensor DS implementation outperforming multiple baselines on synthetic and real data.

Abstract: We study sampling and recovery for tensor-valued dynamical systems evolving under the t-product, using partial spatial observations over time. Although the t-product Fourier transform yields slicewise matrix dynamics, recovery does not generally decouple across slices. Eigenvalues shared across Fourier-domain frontal slices couple the corresponding eigenspaces into aggregated spectral blocks, which determine the tensor-specific recovery geometry and space--time tradeoff.
  We establish deterministic identifiability conditions for the initial tensor state. In the diagonalizable case, these are frame conditions on the aggregated blocks. We extend them to non-diagonalizable systems through generalized eigenspaces and Jordan structure. A temporal--spectral factorization of the finite-horizon stacked recovery system underlying Tensor Dynamical Sampling (DS) yields a decoupled two-stage formulation once the temporal threshold is reached. Under independent spatial subsampling, we prove high-probability recovery guarantees using empirical Gram matrices on the aggregated blocks. The bounds depend on aggregated multiplicities, incoherence, and population conditioning. Stability estimates separate the spatial frame factor from the temporal Vandermonde factor and show that increasing temporal depth may worsen numerical conditioning even after identifiability is ensured.
  We implement Tensor DS and evaluate it on synthetic and real data. Phase transitions track the predicted blockwise sampling thresholds, and stability experiments illustrate Vandermonde conditioning. Experiments on sea surface temperature and turbulence velocity data show that a learned t-product system operator generally yields more accurate and structurally faithful reconstructions than vectorized low-rank, Tucker-type, graph-regularized, graph-spectral, and vectorized dynamical sampling baselines.

</details>


### [20] [A Two-Grid Finite Element Method for the Nonlinear Poisson-Boltzmann Equation Based on a Solution Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34661)
*Zhen Chao,Jingchun Chen,Cong He*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A two-grid finite element method for the nonlinear Poisson-Boltzmann equation, built on a singularity-removing solution decomposition, solving the nonlinear part only on a coarse mesh plus one Newton correction on the fine mesh, with proven quasi-optimal error estimates and a coarse mesh of size H~h^{2/5}.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The PBE is a widely used implicit-solvent model for biomolecular electrostatics, but its numerical solution is complicated by point charges, the dielectric jump across the molecular surface, and exponential nonlinearity. Existing approaches often need discrete maximum principles or mesh-angle conditions; this work seeks an efficient, provably accurate two-grid scheme avoiding such constraints.

Method: Uses the solution decomposition u=G+Psi+Phi_t (Xie 2014): G carries singularities analytically, Psi solves a linear interface problem, and Phi_t solves a semilinear interface problem for arbitrary ionic species. The nonlinear Phi_t problem is solved only on a coarse mesh, followed by a single Newton correction on the fine mesh, so only SPD linear systems are solved on the fine mesh. Analysis employs a C^2 truncation of the exponential growing quadratically beyond level tau, chosen large enough that the truncated and original problems share the same solution; no discrete maximum principle or mesh-angle condition is required. Proofs give quasi-optimal energy and L^2 estimates and an L^4 estimate for Phi_t's FE approximation.

Result: For the two-grid solution, proven error bound in 3D: ||u-u_tg|| <= C(h^{s-1}+H^{2(s-1)+t/2}), with t=min{s,sigma}-1. When the dual problem is at least as regular as the primal (sigma>=s), the coarse mesh can be H~h^{2/5} without loss of accuracy. A manufactured-solution test confirms predicted rates and the quadratic dependence of the two-grid correction on the coarse error.

Conclusion: The two-grid FEM provides an efficient and rigorously analyzed approach to the PBE: nonlinear solves are confined to a coarse mesh with only SPD systems on the fine mesh, yielding quasi-optimal error estimates, a coarse mesh of H~h^{2/5}, and numerical validation.

Abstract: The nonlinear Poisson--Boltzmann equation (PBE) is a widely used implicit-solvent model for the electrostatics of biomolecules in ionic solvent. Its numerical solution is complicated by the point charges, the dielectric jump across the molecular surface, and the exponential nonlinearity. We study a two-grid finite element method for the PBE built on the solution decomposition $u=G+Ψ+\Phit$ of Xie [J.~Comput.~Phys.~275 (2014) 294--309], in which $G$ carries the singularities analytically, $Ψ$ solves a linear interface problem, and $\Phit$ solves a semilinear interface problem for an arbitrary number of ionic species. The nonlinear problem for $\Phit$ is solved on a coarse mesh only, followed by a single Newton correction on the fine mesh, so that only symmetric positive definite linear systems are solved on the fine mesh. The analysis uses a $C^2$ truncation of the exponential that grows quadratically beyond a level $τ$; for $τ$ above an explicit bound the truncated problem has the same solution as the original one, and no discrete maximum principle or mesh-angle condition is needed. Under standard piecewise regularity assumptions we prove quasi-optimal energy and $L^2$ estimates and an $L^4$ estimate for the finite element approximation of $\Phit$, and for the two-grid solution we prove $\enorm{u-\utg}\le C\big(h^{s-1}+H^{2(s-1)+t/2}\big)$ in three dimensions, where $t=\min\{s,σ\}-1$. Whenever the dual problem is at least as regular as the primal one ($σ\ge s$), the coarse mesh can be chosen as $H\sim h^{2/5}$ without loss of accuracy. A manufactured-solution test confirms the predicted rates and the quadratic dependence of the two-grid correction on the coarse error.

</details>


### [21] [A Discontinuous Galerkin Method for Biharmonic Dirichlet Control Problems With Patch Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34669)
*Wei Gong,Ruo Li,Jun Yin*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An interior penalty DG method for Dirichlet boundary control of the biharmonic equation (control via normal derivative), using a reconstructed discontinuous approximation with one unknown per element and arbitrarily high order, with a priori energy and L² error estimates under limited polygonal-domain regularity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dirichlet boundary control governed by the biharmonic equation with control in the normal derivative is a challenging PDE-constrained optimization problem. Standard high-order methods typically need many degrees of freedom per element, and the analysis must cope with the limited regularity that solutions have on polygonal domains.

Method: Interior penalty discontinuous Galerkin discretization approximating state and adjoint state in a reconstructed discontinuous approximation space (arbitrarily high order with a single unknown per element). A priori error analysis in energy and L² norms, using a medius argument to handle limited regularity on polygonal domains.

Result: A priori error estimates in the energy and L² norms are derived for the state and adjoint state under limited regularity on polygonal domains, and numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rates.

Conclusion: The reconstructed-approximation IPDG scheme provides an efficient high-order (one unknown per element) method for biharmonic Dirichlet boundary control, with rigorously established convergence rates validated numerically.

Abstract: In this paper, we present and analyze an interior penalty discontinuous Galerkin method for Dirichlet boundary control problems governed by the biharmonic equation, with the control imposed through the normal derivative. The method is based on a reconstructed discontinuous approximation that admits an arbitrarily high-order approximation space with only one unknown per element. We construct a discretization that approximates the state and adjoint state in this space and derive a priori error estimates in the energy and $L^2$ norms. The analysis is carried out under the limited regularity available on polygonal domains by means of a medius argument. Numerical experiments are provided to support the predicted convergence rates.

</details>


### [22] [An efficient coupled block preconditioner for the Marker-and-Cell discretization of the Stokes-Darcy equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34692)
*Zhen Chao,Tianwen Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A coupled block preconditioner for MAC-discretized Stokes-Darcy double saddle-point systems that avoids approximating the hard second Schur complement, with spectrum analysis and a parameter-scaling rule giving mesh-independent, robust GMRES convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing block triangular preconditioners for Stokes-Darcy double saddle-point systems require approximations of two Schur complements, and the second one is difficult to approximate uniformly in the physical parameters (viscosity, permeability). The authors seek a preconditioner that does not need to approximate this second Schur complement at all.

Method: They keep the interface coupling and regularize the constraint block by adding a small multiple of the identity, yielding a coupled preconditioner. They analyze the spectrum of the preconditioned matrix in both the ideal case and the inexact computational version, and derive a rule for scaling the regularization parameter with the physical parameters.

Result: Numerical results show mesh-independent convergence of preconditioned GMRES and robustness with respect to viscosity and permeability.

Conclusion: The coupled regularized preconditioner eliminates the need to approximate the problematic second Schur complement while retaining parameter-robust and mesh-independent performance.

Abstract: We consider the iterative solution of the large, sparse and nonsymmetric double saddle-point systems arising from the stationary Stokes-Darcy equations in two dimensions, discretized by the Marker-and- Cell (MAC) finite difference method. Existing block triangular preconditioners rest on approximations of two Schur complements, the second of which is hard to approximate uniformly in the physical parameters. We propose instead a coupled preconditioner that keeps the interface coupling and regularizes the constraint block by a small multiple of the identity, so that the second Schur complement never has to be approximated. We analyze the spectrum of the preconditioned matrix, both in the ideal case and for the inexact version used in computations, and derive from it a rule for scaling the regularization parameter with the physical parameters. Numerical results show mesh-independent convergence of preconditioned GMRES and robustness with respect to the viscosity and the permeability.

</details>


### [23] [A High-Order Geometric-Compatible DGSEM with Subcell Limiting for the GPR System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34755)
*Jielin Yang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A new equation-of-state-independent finite-volume method (GC-FV) and a high-order discontinuous Galerkin extension (GC-DG) are developed for a reduced Godunov-Peshkov-Romenski system, blending the two operators on shared subcells to retain exact mass balance while capturing shocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Density is recovered as ρ=ρ0·det(A), making mass conservation a hidden companion law of the distortion equation; standard schemes struggle to preserve this exact mass balance while remainng EOS-independent and robust across shocks, elasticity, thermal waves and elastoplasticity.

Method: Starting from a straight-path Dal Maso-LeFloch-Murat fluctuation pair, minimum-change cofactor projections give a single-valued density flux and exact induced local Lax-Friedrichs dissipation without jump quotients. Summation-by-parts lifts the geometric two-state identity to high order, yielding an equivalent subcell representation. GC-DG is blended with GC-FV on the same Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto subcells; both induce the same boundary density flux, so the global subcell blend preserves the exact semidiscrete mass balance while suppressing shock oscillations. A modal/face-jump indicator sets the elementwise blending coefficient.

Result: Numerical experiments confirm high-order convergence, the expected temporal scaling of the fully discrete mass drift, and robust shock capturing for fluid, nonlinear-elastic, thermal-wave and elastoplastic problems, including genuinely multidimensional configurations.

Conclusion: The GC-FV/GC-DG framework provides an EOS-independent, high-order, mass-conservative scheme for the reduced GPR system that remains robust across a broad class of material and wave problems.

Abstract: We construct an equation-of-state-independent geometric-compatible finite-volume (GC-FV) method and a high-order nodal discontinuous Galerkin spectral-element extension (GC-DG) for a reduced Godunov-Peshkov-Romenski system on fixed Eulerian meshes. Density is recovered from $ρ=ρ_0\det(\mathbf{A})$, so mass conservation is a hidden companion law of the distortion equation. Starting from a straight-path Dal Maso-LeFloch-Murat fluctuation pair, minimum-change cofactor projections produce a single-valued density flux and exact induced local Lax-Friedrichs dissipation without jump quotients. The summation-by-parts property lifts the geometric two-state identity to high order and yields an equivalent subcell representation of GC-DG. We then blend GC-DG with the compatible GC-FV operator on the same Legendre-Gauss-Lobatto subcells. Because both operators induce the same boundary density flux, the global subcell blend retains the exact semidiscrete mass balance while suppressing shock-generated oscillations. A complementary modal/face-jump indicator supplies the elementwise blending coefficient. Numerical experiments verify high-order convergence, the expected temporal scaling of the fully discrete mass drift, and robust shock capturing for fluid, nonlinear-elastic, thermal-wave, and elastoplastic problems, including genuinely multidimensional configurations.

</details>


### [24] [Active Flux and Polygons: why a stabilisation and how to implement boundary conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34761)
*Remi Abgrall,Yongle Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper clarifies the mild instability in Active Flux-type schemes for polygonal meshes and rigorously justifies the earlier remedy, while providing a proper (non-heuristic) treatment of boundary conditions, illustrated on hybrid meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prior Active Flux algorithm for polygonal meshes (BP_Pampa_VEM) exhibited a mild instability when directly transposing the simplex-based algorithm to polygons; the proposed fix lacked rigorous explanation, and boundary condition implementation was only heuristic.

Method: The authors analyze the source of the instability in the polygonal Active Flux scheme, prove correctness of the previously proposed remedy, and build on pampa_dg to properly implement boundary conditions; the method is demonstrated on several hybrid-mesh examples.

Result: The instability is explained and the earlier remedy is shown to be correct; boundary conditions are implemented rigorously rather than heuristically; numerical examples on hybrid meshes validate the approach.

Conclusion: The paper solidifies the theoretical foundations of Active Flux methods on polygonal meshes by explaining the instability, justifying the remedy, and giving correct boundary condition treatment.

Abstract: In a recent paper, the authors developed an Active Flux-type algorithm for polygonal meshes (see \cite{BP_Pampa_VEM}). The main difference between Active Flux methods on polygonal meshes and those on Cartesian or simplex meshes is that the basis functions are not explicitly known. These functions describe the solution using point-value approximations on the polygon boundaries and the average value within the polygons. In \cite{BP_Pampa_VEM}, it was shown that directly transposing the algorithm from simplexes to polygons leads to a mild instability, and a remedy was proposed without a rigorous explanation. In the present paper, we clarify this issue and demonstrate that the solution presented in \cite{BP_Pampa_VEM} is indeed correct. Furthermore, another challenge-the implementation of boundary conditions-was previously solved heuristically. Building on \cite{pampa_dg}, we show how to properly implement these boundary conditions. Finally, the method is illustrated through several examples with hybrid meshes.

</details>


### [25] [Numerical Approximation of Maxwell Transmission Eigenvalue Problem in Dispersive Media via Holomorphic Operator Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34804)
*Chao Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A Nedelec-element discretization combined with a parallel multi-step contour integral method is developed to compute Maxwell transmission eigenvalues in dispersive media, with convergence justified by Karma's abstract approximation theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Maxwell transmission eigenvalue problem in dispersive media requires efficient and provably convergent numerical treatment, which existing approaches do not fully provide.

Method: The problem is cast as an eigenvalue problem of a holomorphic Fredholm operator function of index zero; it is discretized with Nedelec finite elements, convergence is shown via Karma's abstract approximation theory, and the resulting nonlinear matrix eigenvalue problems are solved by a parallel multi-step contour integral method.

Result: Convergence of the scheme is established theoretically, and numerical experiments confirm the viability and accuracy of the proposed approach.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a convergent and practically accurate tool for computing transmission eigenvalues in dispersive media.

Abstract: In this paper, we develop and analyze a numerical method for the Maxwell's transmission eigenvalue problem in dispersive media. The problem is formulated as the eigenvalue problem of a holomorphic Fredholm operator function of index zero. For discretization, we employ the Nedelec elements. Convergence of the scheme is established via the abstract approximation theory of Karma. To compute the resulting nonlinear matrix eigenvalue problems, we employ a parallel multi-step contour integral method. Numerical experiments are presented to demonstrate the viability and accuracy of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [26] [A discrete fractional Grönwall inequality for dissipative systems via resolvent kernel estimate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34827)
*Lei Li,Yunxiao Liu,Jiwei Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves a discrete fractional Gronwall inequality for dissipative time-dependent forced systems, bounding resolvent entries of completely positive discretizations by integrals of the continuous response kernel, and applies it to L1-type schemes for linear subdiffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing discrete fractional Gronwall-type tools do not adequately capture resolvent behavior of completely positive discretizations under time-dependent forcing, nor the interplay of initial-data decay and history/forcing contributions under L1 weight perturbations and stepsize restrictions.

Method: Establishes a discrete fractional Gronwall inequality for dissipative systems with time-dependent forcing. For completely positive discretizations, resolvent entries are bounded by integrals of the continuous response kernel, under controlled perturbation of L1 weights and a stepsize restriction. The proof constructs a supersolution for each resolvent column. Applies the framework to linear subdiffusion.

Result: Obtains Mittag-Leffler weights preserving both initial-data decay and historical forcing contributions; the framework covers L1 and several standard uniform-mesh schemes. For linear subdiffusion, it gives decay-preserving L1 error estimates separating first-order and (2-α)-order contributions, with stronger long-time decay under extra solution regularity.

Conclusion: The proposed discrete fractional Gronwall inequality provides a unified, decay-preserving analysis tool for completely positive schemes, yielding sharper L1 error estimates for subdiffusion that retain both initial and forcing-history effects.

Abstract: We establish a discrete fractional Gronwall inequality for dissipative systems with time-dependent forcing. For completely positive discretizations, we bound individual resolvent entries by integrals of the continuous response kernel, under controlled perturbation to the L1 weights and a stepsize restriction. The proof constructs a supersolution for each resolvent column. The resulting Mittag-Leffler weights retain both the decay of initial data and the historical contributions of forcing. The framework covers L1 and several standard schemes on uniform meshes. For linear subdiffusion, it yields decay-preserving L1 error estimates separating first order and $(2-α)$ order contributions, with stronger long-time decay under additional solution regularity.

</details>


### [27] [A Variational Geometric Approach to Exact Recovery by $\ell_1/\ell_2$ Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34842)
*Yulan Liu,Rongrong Lin*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A variational geometric framework characterizes exact recovery for affine-constrained l1/l2 minimization, yielding sharp null-space conditions with improved optimal coefficients for uniform local and global recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exact recovery guarantees for affine-constrained l1/l2 minimization with nonzero data lack precise pointwise characterizations; the paper seeks sharper and provably optimal conditions.

Method: A variational geometric approach using directional derivatives and cones tied to sublevel sets of the ratio objective, establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for local and global exact recovery; projection estimates and null-space conditions build toward uniform recovery.

Result: Local minimality, first-order growth, and tilt stability are shown equivalent. The uniform local recovery condition has coefficient c_s=max{1,(sqrt(s)-1)/2}, optimal for each fixed s; uniform global recovery gets a scalar bound with optimal coefficient two (improving three). Local margin gives explicit growth moduli, localization radii, and perturbation bounds.
