Conclusion: Sharp, optimal null-space conditions are derived for local and global recovery; uniform local recovery does not imply uniform global recovery, as shown by examples.

Abstract: For affine-constrained $\ell_1/\ell_2$ minimization with nonzero data, a variational geometric approach is developed to characterize exact recovery. Necessary and sufficient conditions for local and global exact recovery are established in terms of directional derivatives and cones associated with sublevel sets of the ratio objective. The local analysis further reveals that local minimality, first-order growth, and tilt stability are equivalent. These pointwise characterizations lead to improved sufficient conditions for uniform recovery of sparse signals. The sufficient condition for uniform local recovery has coefficient $c_s=\max\{1,(\sqrt{s}-1)/2\}$ for $s\ge2$. For each fixed $s$, the coefficient $c_s$ is optimal within the considered family of local null-space conditions. Projection estimates yield a scalar bound for uniform global recovery with coefficient two, improving the earlier coefficient three. The coefficient two is optimal within the considered family of global null-space bounds when both $s$ and $n$ may vary. A positive margin in the local null-space condition provides explicit growth moduli, localization radii, and bounds on linear objective perturbations that leave the localized minimizer fixed. Examples show that uniform local recovery does not imply uniform global recovery.

</details>


### [28] [Self-consistent flow maps based on commutator-free exponential integrators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35327)
*Xiaofeng Cai,Linghui Kong*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "flow-map framework" for nonlinear transport equations, built around a self-consistent exact frozen flow expressed via an exponential-pullback identity and realized numerically with a commutator-free exponential integrator. Applied to Vlasov-Poisson, it yields a volume-preserving diffeomorphism and shows design-order convergence and good long-time conservation at large time steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear transport equations such as Vlasov-Poisson are hard to integrate because the transport field evolves with the solution, coupling field variation, field approximation, and characteristic integration errors. The authors seek a self-consistent frozen-flow formulation and a numerical realization that cleanly separates and controls these error sources.

Method: An exponential-pullback identity represents each frozen transport exponential in a commutator-free exponential integrator as the pullback induced by its exact backward characteristic map. Composing these maps gives a self-consistent exact frozen flow, with autonomous frozen fields formed as linear combinations of transport fields evaluated at stage approximations. A local freezing-error identity and a one-step Lie-Euler estimate separate field-variation, field-approximation, and characteristic-integration errors. The construction is specialized to Vlasov-Poisson using velocity-space quadrature, discrete Poisson solve, field interpolation, and numerical integration of frozen characteristic equations.

Result: Under suitable regularity assumptions, the self-consistent exact frozen flow for Vlasov-Poisson is proven to be a volume-preserving diffeomorphism. Temporal self-convergence tests on Vlasov-Poisson and guiding-center benchmarks give rates consistent with design orders and illustrate conservation behavior and solution-structure evolution in long-time simulations with large time steps.

Conclusion: The flow-map framework offers a principled, self-consistent way to discretize nonlinear transport equations, with error components explicitly separated. For Vlasov-Poisson it preserves the volume-preserving diffeomorphism structure and shows order-consistent convergence and favorable long-time conservation at large time steps.

Abstract: We develop a flow-map framework for nonlinear transport equations, comprising a self-consistent exact frozen flow and its numerical realization. An exponential--pullback identity represents each frozen transport exponential in a commutator-free exponential integrator as the pullback induced by its exact backward characteristic map. Composing these maps defines the self-consistent exact frozen flow, a time-discrete construction with autonomous frozen fields formed from linear combinations of transport fields evaluated at the stage approximations. A local freezing-error identity and a one-step estimate for the Lie--Euler Vlasov--Poisson update separate field variation, field approximation, and characteristic integration errors; these estimates do not assert global PDE convergence. We specialize this construction to the Vlasov--Poisson system and obtain the self-consistent numerical flow through velocity-space quadrature, a discrete Poisson solve, field interpolation, and numerical integration of the frozen characteristic equations. Under suitable regularity assumptions, we prove that the self-consistent exact frozen flow for this system is a volume-preserving diffeomorphism. Temporal self-convergence tests on Vlasov--Poisson and guiding-center benchmarks yield rates consistent with the design orders and illustrate conservation behavior and the evolution of solution structures in long-time simulations with large time steps.

</details>


### [29] [One-Sided $p$-Explicit Stability for High-Order Cut Finite Element Nitsche Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34853)
*Erik Burman,Peter Hansbo,Mats G. Larson,Karl Larsson*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A one-sided stability argument for high-order cut finite elements with symmetric Nitsche boundary conditions, using an H^1-projection that splits discrete functions into a polynomial part and a stabilized residual, yielding explicit normal-flux bounds and hp-error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-order cut finite element methods with symmetric Nitsche conditions require stability arguments, but standard two-sided (full-element) estimates and polynomial continuation approaches suffer from exponential cost and loss of explicit control, especially regarding cut position and polynomial degree.

Method: An H^1-projection decomposes the discrete function on each boundary patch into one polynomial plus a stabilized residual. Inward segments control the polynomial inside the physical domain, while complete-element estimates control only the residual. This yields an explicit normal-flux estimate with factor p^2/h and a matching Nitsche penalty, with constants independent of cut position and degree. A neighboring-interval estimate quantifies the exponential cost of polynomial continuation, motivating its avoidance.

Result: For smooth exterior boundaries and a geometry-dependent mesh restriction, the method gives the explicit normal-flux estimate and sufficient penalty scaling. It yields hp-error estimates: half a power of degree lost in energy and a full power in L^2 relative to best approximation, plus exponential degree convergence for analytic extensions on sufficiently fine meshes. Translated-disk experiments confirm bounded normalized flux constants, stable penalty thresholds across degrees and cuts, and predicted convergence orders, with stabilization errors reported separately.

Conclusion: The one-sided stability framework provides robust, explicit control for high-order cut finite elements with symmetric Nitsche boundary conditions, avoiding polynomial continuation, and delivers sharp hp-error and exponential convergence results validated by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We give a one-sided stability argument for high-order cut finite elements with symmetric Nitsche boundary conditions. On each boundary patch, an $H^1$-projection splits the discrete function into a single polynomial and a stabilized residual. Inward segments control the polynomial inside the physical domain; complete-element estimates control only the residual. For smooth exterior boundaries and under a geometry-dependent mesh restriction, this gives an explicit normal-flux estimate with factor $p^2/h$ and a sufficient Nitsche penalty with the same scaling. The constants are independent of the cut position and polynomial degree. A neighboring-interval estimate quantifies the exponential cost of polynomial continuation and motivates avoiding it in the stability proof. We obtain $hp$-error estimates, with half a power of degree lost in energy and a full power in $L^2$ relative to best approximation, and exponential degree convergence for analytic extensions on sufficiently fine meshes. Translated-disk experiments show bounded normalized flux constants and penalty thresholds over the tested degrees and cuts, and resolved convergence curves follow the predicted orders. Physical and extension-dependent stabilization errors are reported separately.

</details>


### [30] [An explicit third-order low-regularity integrator for the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34918)
*Shengxin Luo,Bin Wang,Yan Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes an explicit third-order low-regularity integrator for the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the one-dimensional torus, with rigorous global error analysis showing third-order convergence under low-regularity initial data conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard numerical schemes for the cubic NLS often require high regularity of the initial data to achieve high-order accuracy. The authors aim to design a high-order (third-order) integrator that works under low regularity assumptions, extending low-regularity integrator techniques to third order.

Method: By carefully designing numerical approximations for the integrals appearing in Fourier space, the authors construct an explicit third-order low-regularity scheme for the cubic NLS on the 1D torus. They provide a rigorous global error analysis of the scheme.

Result: The scheme achieves third-order convergence in H^r(T) for initial data in H^{r+4}(T) with r>1/2, and third-order convergence in L^2(T) for initial data in H^4(T). Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical convergence results.

Conclusion: An explicit third-order low-regularity integrator for the cubic NLS on the one-dimensional torus is established with proven global third-order convergence under low-regularity data conditions, validated by numerical experiments.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose an explicit third-order low-regularity integrator for the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the one-dimensional torus $\mathbb{T}$. By carefully designing numerical approximations for the integrals in Fourier space, we construct a third-order scheme and provide a rigorous global error analysis. More precisely, we prove that the scheme achieves third-order convergence in $H^{r}(\mathbb{T})$ for the initial data in $H^{r+4}(\mathbb{T})$ with $r>1/2$. Furthermore, the third-order convergence in $L^{2}(\mathbb{T})$ is established for the initial data in $H^{4}(\mathbb{T})$. Numerical experiments are presented to confirm the theoretical convergence results.

</details>


### [31] [Randomized Krylov Subspace Methods for Shifted Linear Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34964)
*Xiang Yuan,Gang Wu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes shifted randomized GMRES/FOM algorithms (with deflated restarting variants) that share a single randomized basis to solve all shifted linear systems simultaneously, reducing orthogonalization overhead while preserving accuracy, and provides theoretical links between the randomized and traditional methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving shifted linear systems with traditional shifted GMRES/FOM requires costly orthogonal basis generation, repeated for each shift. The authors aim to cut this computational overhead while keeping orthogonality and solution accuracy, and to understand the theoretical relationship between randomized/sketched and traditional Krylov iterates.

Method: They combine randomized sketching, control of collinear residual directions, and deflated restarting into a unified framework, yielding RandGMRES-Sh, RandFOM-Sh, RandGMRES-Sh-DR, and RandFOM-Sh-DR. A single shared basis is used to solve all shifted systems at once, and they derive theoretical results: proving GMRES and sketched GMRES iterates lie in and span the same basic Krylov subspace, the relationship between RandGMRES-Sh and RandFOM-Sh solutions before the first restart, and how these relate to GMRES-Sh and FOM-Sh.

Result: Compared with standard traditional algorithms, the new methods show a steady but moderate improvement in convergence speed, with significantly reduced orthogonal basis generation cost under specific conditions. Theoretical proofs establish the shared-subspace property, the RandGMRES-Sh/RandFOM-Sh solution relationship, and their connection to traditional GMRES-Sh/FOM-Sh solutions.

Conclusion: The unified randomized framework successfully solves all shifted systems with one shared basis and lowers orthogonalization overhead, offering moderate convergence gains, while providing rigorous theoretical guarantees linking randomized and traditional shifted Krylov methods.

Abstract: We propose several novel Krylov subspace algorithms termed shifted randomized GMRES (RandGMRES-Sh), shifted randomized FOM (RandFOM-Sh), shifted randomized GMRES with deflated restarting (RandGMRES-Sh-DR), and shifted randomized FOM with deflated restarting (RandFOM-Sh-DR), for solving shifted linear systems. These methods are designed to significantly reduce the computational overhead of orthogonal basis generation while preserving orthogonality under specific conditions. Besides, this unified framework makes it possible to solve all shifted linear systems at the same time by using a single shared basis. These new approaches combine multiple advanced techniques together effectively, such as randomized sketching, the control of collinear residual directions, and deflated restarting methods. Compared with the standard versions of traditional algorithms, the new algorithms show a steady but moderate improvement in convergence speed. Furthermore, we theoretically prove that although GMRES and sketched GMRES have different algorithmic solution steps, their iterates are all confined to and together span the same basic Krylov subspace. We also work out the theoretical relationship between the solutions produced by RandGMRES-Sh and RandFOM-Sh at the same iteration step before the first restart. Moreover, we strictly prove how the solutions of RandGMRES-Sh and RandFOM-Sh are related to those of their traditional versions, GMRES-Sh and FOM-Sh, respectively.

</details>


### [32] [Structure-preserving discretisations for the quasi-incompressible, thermal Maxwell-Stefan equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35157)
*Boris D. Andrews,Aaron Baier-Reinio,Aaron Brunk,Patrick E. Farrell,Mária Lukáčová-Medviďová*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops two structure-preserving discretisation methods for a thermal Maxwell-Stefan-Navier-Stokes system describing miscible mixtures under a quasi-incompressibility constraint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors adopt the entropy balance law over the energy balance law because it facilitates the development of two structure-preserving discretisation schemes.

Method: The first method combines a semi-implicit Euler scheme in time with suitable finite elements in space, enforcing the constraint pointwise. The second uses a continuous Petrov-Galerkin discretisation in time.

Result: The first method generates entropy and enforces quasi-incompressibility pointwise but dissipates total energy numerically. The second also generates entropy and satisfies the constraint pointwise, while additionally conserving total energy and allowing high-order time approximations. Numerical experiments demonstrate the structure-preserving properties and behaviour of both methods.

Conclusion: Both schemes are structure-preserving, with the Petrov-Galerkin approach offering the advantage of energy conservation and high-order accuracy for the thermal Maxwell-Stefan-Navier-Stokes system.

Abstract: We study a thermal Maxwell-Stefan-Navier-Stokes system modelling miscible mixtures under a quasi-incompressibility constraint. The system consists of the conservation laws for mass and momentum, and the balance law for entropy, which is preferred over the balance law for energy because it facilitates the development of two structure-preserving discretisation methods. The first method applies a semi-implicit Euler scheme in time, together with suitable finite elements in space, which guarantees generation of entropy and enforces the quasi-incompressibility constraint pointwise, but numerically dissipates total energy. The second method employs a continuous Petrov-Galerkin discretisation in time, which also generates entropy and satisfies the constraint pointwise, and in addition conserves the total energy and allows high-order time approximations. The structure-preserving properties and behaviour of both numerical methods are demonstrated by a series of numerical experiments.

</details>


### [33] [Regularized Sequential Reconstructions of Dynamic Nonsmooth Sources from Sparse Boundary Flux Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35173)
*Davide Bianchi,Tianrui Dai,Hongqin Zhang,Shupeng Zhang,Zhidong Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies an inverse source problem for the heat equation on the unit disk with flux measurements at finite boundary points, where sources are time-piecewise-constant characteristic functions of unknown Lipschitz domains. It introduces a Hilbert-scale penalty to regularize an unbounded, non-closable forward operator and develops a convergent, unique sequential Galerkin–Tikhonov reconstruction scheme.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical regularization theory requires closed (or closable) unbounded operators, but the forward operator here is shown to be non-closable, so standard approaches fail. The authors are motivated to handle time-fractional piecewise sources with limited boundary flux data while accounting for history contributions and error propagation.

Method: The inverse problem is decomposed per time interval: subtracting the history term from earlier sources yields a linear inverse problem for a densely defined, unbounded operator F_tau. The authors prove F_tau is not closable and regularize it using a Hilbert-scale penalty that compactifies the operator. This yields a sequential Galerkin–Tikhonov reconstruction in which each source is recovered from flux data up to the end of its interval and from previously reconstructed sources.

Result: The authors prove convergence and uniqueness of the sequential reconstruction, show that earlier reconstruction errors propagate into later data with exponential decay in time, and establish stability estimates with respect to the number of sensors. A numerical experiment on noisy data confirms feasibility, with Fourier approximation and a neural-network boundary correction refining the results.

Conclusion: The Hilbert-scale penalty circumvents the non-closability of F_tau, enabling a rigorous sequential regularization framework. The method is convergent, unique, stable with sensor count, and error-robust over time, and is numerically validated including neural-network-based boundary refinement.

Abstract: We consider an inverse source problem for the heat equation on the unit disk $Ω\subset\mathbb R^2$ with flux measurements at finitely many boundary points. The source is piecewise constant in time on prescribed intervals and, on each interval, is the characteristic function of an unknown region with Lipschitz boundary. The measurements on each interval split into the contribution of the current source and a history term from earlier sources; subtracting the latter leaves a linear inverse problem for a forward operator $F_τ$ that is densely defined and unbounded on $L^2(Ω)$. We prove that $F_τ$ is not closable, so the classical regularization theory for closed unbounded operators does not apply. We regularize instead with a Hilbert-scale penalty that compactifies it. The resulting Galerkin--Tikhonov reconstruction is sequential: each source is recovered from the flux observed up to the end of its interval and from the previously reconstructed sources. We prove its convergence and uniqueness, show that earlier reconstruction errors propagate into later data with exponential decay in time, and establish stability estimates with respect to the number of sensors. A numerical experiment on noisy measurements shows the feasibility of the sequential approximations, refined by Fourier approximation and boundary correction by a neural network.

</details>


### [34] [A Numerical Method for the Fractional Semilinear Calderón Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35299)

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A finite element method is proposed for recovering a coefficient in a fractional semilinear equation using a single exterior measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inverse problems for fractional semilinear equations are challenging due to nonlocality and nonlinearity; existing approaches often require extensive data. The paper aims to reconstruct the coefficient from a single exterior measurement, minimizing data requirements.

Method: The authors formulate a finite element method (FEM) for the inverse coefficient problem, presumably combining discretization of the fractional operator with an optimization or regularization scheme to fit the single exterior measurement.

Result: The abstract is too brief to report quantitative results; the work presents the FEM-based reconstruction scheme as the main contribution.

Conclusion: The paper offers a FEM framework for coefficient reconstruction in fractional semilinear equations from limited (single exterior) data, targeting practical numerical solvability of such inverse problems.

Abstract: We present a finite element method for reconstructing a coefficient in a fractional semilinear equation from a single exterior measurement.

</details>


### [35] [A Robust ADI-Based Direct Discontinuous Galerkin Method for Two-Dimensional Singularly Perturbed Parabolic PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35343)
*Vijay Kumar,Gautam Singh*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes an operator-splitting direct discontinuous Galerkin (DDG) method for 2D one-parameter singularly perturbed parabolic problems, using ADI splitting, DDG on a Shishkin mesh, and backward Euler time stepping, with proven optimal convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Singularly perturbed parabolic problems in 2D exhibit sharp boundary layers that are hard to resolve accurately and efficiently with standard numerical methods, motivating a method that combines layer-adapted meshes with efficient dimensional splitting.

Method: An ADI operator-splitting approach decomposes the 2D problem into two 1D subproblems. Spatial discretization uses the DDG method on a piecewise-uniform Shishkin mesh to capture boundary layers; temporal discretization uses the backward Euler scheme for stability and robustness.

Result: Theoretical analysis establishes an optimal spatial convergence rate of order k (k being the polynomial degree) and (Δt)-order temporal convergence. The fully discrete scheme is proven consistent, stable, and convergent; numerical experiments confirm the theory and show effectiveness and robustness.

Conclusion: The operator-splitting DDG scheme with Shishkin meshing and backward Euler time integration is an effective, robust, and provably convergent approach for 2D singularly perturbed parabolic problems.

Abstract: This study proposes two-dimensional one-parameter singularly perturbed parabolic problems and develops an operator-splitting direct discontinuous Galerkin (DDG) method for their numerical solution. To improve computational efficiency, an alternating direction implicit (ADI) splitting approach decomposes the original two-dimensional problem into two separate one-dimensional subproblems. The spatial discretization is performed using the DDG method on a piecewise-uniform Shishkin mesh, enabling accurate resolution of the sharp boundary layers that arise in singularly perturbed problems (SPPs). For the temporal discretization is carried out with the backward Euler scheme to ensure robustness and stability. The theoretical analysis establishes an optimal convergence rate of order $k$ in the spatial discretization, where $k$ denotes the degree of the polynomial basis functions, and $(Δt)$-order convergence in temporal dimensions. We prove that the resulting fully discrete scheme is consistent, stable, and convergent. Numerical experiments confirm the theoretical results and demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [36] [An unconditionally convergent BDF2-type finite element integrator for the inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35382)
*Michele Aldé,Hywel Normington,Dirk Praetorius,Michele Ruggeri*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes and analyzes a fully discrete scheme for the inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (iLLG) equation: first-order finite elements in space and BDF2 (with convex combination with backward Euler for the inertial term) in time. The authors prove well-posedness, unconditional stability, explicit error bounds for the unit-length constraint, a discrete energy law, and unconditional convergence to a weak solution, supported by numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The iLLG equation models magnetization dynamics at subpicosecond time scales; a rigorous fully discrete numerical treatment with provable stability and convergence is needed.

Method: Fully discrete scheme: first-order finite elements for spatial discretization and BDF2 for time discretization, with the inertial term treated by a convex combination of BDF2 and backward Euler.

Result: Proofs of well-posedness and unconditional stability, explicit error bounds for the unit-length constraint, a discrete energy law resembling the continuous dissipative law, and unconditional convergence to a weak solution; numerical experiments validate the theory.

Conclusion: The proposed scheme is a robust, provably stable and convergent method for the iLLG equation, with theoretical guarantees confirmed by numerical experiments.

Abstract: We propose and analyze a fully discrete numerical scheme to approximate solutions to the inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (iLLG) equation, a state-of-the-art model to describe the dynamics of the magnetization at subpicosecond time scales. The method employs first-order finite elements for the spatial discretization and the two-step backward differentiation formula (BDF2) for the time discretization, where the inertial term is treated by a convex combination of BDF2 and the backward Euler method. We prove well-posedness and unconditional stability of the scheme, and obtain explicit bounds for the error in the realization of the iLLG-inherent unit length constraint satisfied by the magnetization. Moreover, we derive the discrete energy law satisfied by the approximations, which resembles the inherent dissipative energy law of the model. Finally, with a constructive approach, we prove unconditional convergence of the approximations toward a weak solution of the problem. Numerical experiments assess the performance of the scheme and validate the theoretical findings.

</details>


### [37] [A framework for trackable and modular rounding error analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35420)
*Erin Carson,Yuxin Ma,Meiyue Shao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes a trackable, modular framework and a new notation γ_n=(1-u)^(-n)-1 for rounding error analysis, illustrated via block LU factorization, distinguishing error sources across algorithmic variants and incorporating improved kernels into error bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern numerical computations involve rapidly evolving arithmetic formats, precisions, and computational kernels, making robust and meaningful rounding error analysis increasingly challenging with classical approaches.

Method: Introduce a modular framework with a new bounding notation γ_n=(1-u)^(-n)-1 (alternative to the classical n u/(1-n u)), keeping origin and propagation of individual error terms explicit; demonstrate on block LU factorization.

Result: The framework distinguishes error sources of different algorithmic variants and allows improved kernels (compensated summation, accurate matrix multiplication) to be incorporated directly into error bounds, without the n u < 1 restriction and handling division errors conveniently.

Conclusion: The trackable, modular framework and new notation offer a more flexible and informative alternative to classical rounding error analysis, adaptable across arithmetic formats, precisions, and kernels.

Abstract: Robust and meaningful rounding error analysis is becoming increasingly challenging as modern numerical computations involve a rapidly evolving variety of arithmetic formats, precisions, and computational kernels. We propose a trackable and modular framework for rounding error analysis, together with a new notation for bounding accumulated rounding errors. The analysis is trackable in the sense that the origin and propagation of individual error terms remain explicit in the final result, and modular in the sense that local error bounds can be replaced according to the arithmetic, precision, or computational kernel without repeating the structural analysis. We also introduce the notation $γ_n=(1-{\bm u})^{-n}-1$, which provides an alternative to the classical $γ_n^{\mathrm h}=n{\bm u}/(1-n{\bm u})$ notation. A key feature is that our notation does not require the restriction $n{\bm u}<1$ and conveniently handles errors arising from division. Using block LU factorization as a representative example, we show that the framework distinguishes the error sources of different algorithmic variants and permits improved kernels, such as compensated summation and accurate matrix multiplication, to be incorporated directly into the resulting error bounds.

</details>


### [38] [Acceleration of the BSS Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35474)
*Tizian Sommerfeld*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper improves the computational complexity of the BSS subsampling algorithm from O(σ²nm³) to O(σ²nm^2.5), with even faster implementations for special cases such as Fourier-type frames on a full frequency cube, achieving essentially O(σ²m²(n+m^1.5)).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The BSS subsampling algorithm (based on Batson, Spielman and Srivastava's work), as studied by Bartel, Schäfer and Ullrich, works well but its O(σ²nm³) complexity is the main bottleneck for a frame of n vectors in dimension m with oversampling σ². This work aims to overcome that computational bottleneck.

Method: The authors continue the prior analysis of the BSS subsampling algorithm and develop a more efficient implementation, reducing its running time to O(σ²nm^2.5). They additionally exploit structure in special cases to obtain further speedups, notably for Fourier-type frames on a full frequency cube, which can be subsampled in essentially O(σ²m²(n+m^1.5)).

Result: The BSS subsampling algorithm can be implemented in O(σ²nm^2.5), improving on the previous O(σ²nm³). For special cases, even faster implementations are possible; in particular, BSS subsampling of Fourier-type frames on a full frequency cube runs in essentially O(σ²m²(n+m^1.5)).

Conclusion: The computational bottleneck of BSS subsampling is significantly reduced, with complexity lowered to O(σ²nm^2.5) in general and to essentially O(σ²m²(n+m^1.5)) for Fourier-type frames on a full frequency cube, making the algorithm substantially more practical.

Abstract: Bartel, Schäfer and Ullrich investigated the BSS subsampling algorithm, which is based on the work by Batson, Spielman and Srivastava. While this algorithm works well, the bottleneck is its complexity of $\mathcal{O}(σ^2nm^3)$ for a frame of $n$ vectors of dimension $m$ and oversampling $σ^2$. We continue their work by showing that the algorithm can be implemented in $\mathcal{O}(σ^2nm^{2.5})$. Moreover, for some special cases, an even faster implementation is possible. In particular, BSS subsampling of Fourier-type frames on a full frequency cube can be implemented in essentially $\mathcal{O}(σ^2m^2(n+m^{1.5}))$.}

</details>

### [39] [Variational relaxation and gradient flows for degenerate elliptic conductivity problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33173)
*Jan Haskovec,Peter Markowich,Stefano Zampini*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: A convex-analytic L²-framework for gradient flows of pumping-energy functionals coupled with elliptic state equations and an evolving conductivity a ≥ 0, establishing subdifferential characterization via pressure-gradient L⁴ membership, maximal-monotone gradient flow, Mosco convergence as the cutoff vanishes, and concave/nonlinear-law regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conductivity may be merely square-integrable or degenerate, so formal differentiation need not yield an L²-subgradient; a rigorous variational framework is needed to handle degenerate/non-smooth conductivity and to study gradient flows and limiting behavior under vanishing cutoff.

Method: Impose lower cutoff r + a ≥ r > 0; define pumping-energy functional via a variational formula; prove convexity and lower semicontinuity; characterize L²-subdifferential as nonempty iff pressure gradient ∈ L⁴; construct unique maximal-monotone L²-gradient flow with energy identity; prove Mosco convergence as cutoff vanishes; introduce inverse-moment conductivity constraint as weak alternative to uniform lower bound; for nonlinear laws a = μ(b), analyze primal/flux representations — if μ concave the functional stays convex, if 1/μ concave it is concave (negative convex) with pointwise gradient flow on a uniformly elliptic box.

Result: L²-subdifferential nonemptiness equivalent to pressure gradient in L⁴; existence and uniqueness of maximal-monotone L²-gradient flow satisfying energy identity; Mosco convergence of functionals as cutoff vanishes; inverse-moment constraint as weak substitute for positive lower bound; admissible stationary points for power laws yield p-Laplacian equations with p > 2 (concave μ) and 1 < p < 2 (concave 1/μ).

Conclusion: A complete convex-analytic L²-gradient-flow theory for pumping-energy functionals with degenerate conductivity is obtained, unifying convex and concave nonlinear-law regimes and linking stationary points to p-Laplacian equations with p > 2 or 1 < p < 2.

Abstract: We develop a convex-analytic $L^2$-framework for gradient flows of pumping-energy functionals coupled with elliptic state equations and an evolving nonnegative conductivity $a\geq 0$. The main difficulty is that the conductivity may be merely square-integrable, or degenerate, and formal differentiation need not produce an $L^2$-subgradient. After imposing the strictly positive lower cutoff $r+a\geq r>0$, we define the pumping-energy functional by a variational formula, prove convexity and lower semicontinuity, and show that its $L^2$-subdifferential is nonempty if and only if the pressure gradient belongs to $L^4$. We also obtain a unique maximal-monotone $L^2$-gradient flow satisfying an energy identity. Moreover, we prove Mosco convergence of the pumping-energy functionals as the cutoff vanishes. An inverse-moment conductivity constraint provides a weak alternative to a uniform positive lower bound. For nonlinear laws $a=μ(b)$, primal and flux representations distinguish two complementary regimes. If $μ$ is concave, the pumping-energy functional remains convex, and we characterize its $L^2$-subdifferential and the induced gradient flow. If $1/μ$ is concave, the pumping-energy functional is concave, and therefore its negative is convex. We construct its pointwise gradient flow on a uniformly elliptic box. Admissible stationary points for power laws yield $p$-Laplacian equations with $p>2$ and $1 < p < 2$, respectively.

</details>


### [40] [$L^\infty$ blow-up in the fourth-order Jordan-Moore-Gibson-Thompson equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33213)
*Tahir Boudjeriou,Filippo Dell'Oro*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies the fourth-order Jordan-Moore-Gibson-Thompson equation with nonlinear source terms depending on u_{ttt} and u_{tt}, proving well-posedness of maximal strong solutions, a gradient-blow-up exclusion criterion (blow-up only via L^∞ norm), and finite-time L^∞ blow-up for convex superlinear nonlinearities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order-in-time nonlinear wave-type equations such as JMGT arise in acoustics/thermoviscous media, but rigorous theory—especially blow-up—for genuinely nonlinear fourth-order-in-time equations is largely missing. The authors aim to establish a complete local/global picture: local existence, an extensibility criterion, and actual finite-time blow-up.

Method: Under homogeneous Dirichlet conditions on a smooth bounded domain in R^n (n≤3), with al in R and bet,gamma,del,rho>0, they first prove existence and uniqueness of a maximal strong solution for sufficiently regular data with f in C^4, f(0)=0. They then combine three time-integrated formulations with a series of resolvent-regularized energy estimates to derive the extensibility criterion: if the maximal time is finite, then the L^∞ norm of u blows up. Finally, assuming f is convex with superlinear growth and divergent integral condition, they use the criterion to show finite-time L^∞ blow-up for a suitable class of initial data.

Result: A unique maximal strong solution exists; gradient blow-up is excluded so that finite-time breakdown forces limsup ||u(·,t)||_{L^∞}=∞; and under convexity plus superlinear growth conditions, solutions blow up in finite time in L^∞ for suitable initial data.

Conclusion: The paper gives the first rigorous description of finite-time blow-up for a genuinely nonlinear fourth-order-in-time equation, providing a blow-up criterion and explicit finite-time L^∞ blow-up results for the JMGT equation.

Abstract: We investigate the fourth-order Jordan-Moore-Gibson-Thompson equation
  \bee
  u_{tttt}+\al u_{ttt}+\bet u_{tt}-γ\Del u_{tt}-\del \Del u_{t}-\varrho \Del u=(f(u))_{ttt}+(f(u))_{tt}
  \eee 
  under homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions in a smooth bounded domain $\Om \subset \R^n$ with $n\leq 3$, where
  $\al \in \R$ and $\bet,γ,\del,\varrho>0$. First, for any suitably regular initial data and assuming that $f\in C^4(\R)$ with $f(0)=0$, we prove that the corresponding initial-boundary value problem admits a unique maximal strong solution $u$ on $(0, \tm)$.
  Second, by combining three time-integrated formulations with a series of resolvent-regularized energy estimates, we show that gradient blow-up is ruled out in the sense that
  \be{EE}
  \tm<\infty \quad\,\, \Rightarrow\quad\,\, \limsup_{t\nearrow \tm}\|u(\cd,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Om)}=\infty. \tag{$\star$}
  \ee 
  Finally, assuming in addition that $f$ is convex and grows superlinearly in the sense that
  \bee
  f''(ξ)\geq 0 \;\; \mbox{ for all}\;\;ξ\in\R, \qquad \frac{f(ξ)}ξ\rightarrow +\infty\;\;\mbox{ as}\;\; ξ\rightarrow +\infty , \qquad \int_{ξ_{0}}^{\infty}\frac{dξ}{f(ξ)}<\infty \;\; \mbox{ for some}\;\; ξ_{0}>0,
  \eee
  we employ the extensibility criterion (\ref{EE}) to prove that, for a suitable class of initial data, the solution blows up in finite time in the $L^\infty$-norm. To the best of our knowledge, this provides the first rigorous description of finite-time blow-up for a genuinely nonlinear equation of fourth order in time.

</details>


### [41] [Localized Hybrid Analytical-Finite-Element Thermal Modeling for Laser Powder Bed Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33273)
*Mohammad Amin Mohammadi Golafshani,Krishnan Suresh*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This work develops a space-time localized hybrid analytical-FE formulation for laser powder bed fusion thermal simulation, restricting where and for how long analytical scan-line contributions are evaluated, while residual terms and boundary conditions let the FE correction capture the thermal effect of removed contributions. It achieves ~25.5x analytical and ~3.92x total speedups on 500 lines with 0.139% nodal 2-norm error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hybrid analytical-finite-element methods enable scan-resolved, part-scale thermal simulation of laser powder bed fusion, but their analytical evaluation cost grows as scan-line contributions accumulate, limiting scalability.

Method: A space-time localized hybrid formulation that restricts where and how long each analytical contribution is evaluated. Residual terms and boundary conditions derived from the heat equation are added so the FE correction accounts for the thermal effect of contributions removed from the analytical field. Validated via a single-line benchmark against independent full FE and multiline comparisons with the conventional hybrid formulation, plus ablations against uncompensated temporal truncation.

Result: Localization limits retained analytical contributions while closely matching the conventional solution. The residual correction is shown to be essential for retaining the thermal effect of removed contributions. On a 500-line case, analytical and total simulation times are reduced by ~25.5x and ~3.92x respectively, with a relative nodal 2-norm difference of 0.139%.

Conclusion: Space-time localization with residual/boundary-condition compensation makes hybrid analytical-FE thermal simulation of LPBF scalable to many scan lines without meaningful accuracy loss.

Abstract: Hybrid analytical--finite-element methods support scan-resolved, part-scale thermal simulation of laser powder bed fusion, but their analytical evaluation cost grows as scan-line contributions accumulate. This work develops a space--time localized hybrid formulation that restricts where and how long each contribution is evaluated analytically. Residual terms and boundary conditions derived from the heat equation allow the finite-element correction to account for the thermal effect of contributions removed from the analytical field. The formulation is assessed using a single-line benchmark against an independent full finite-element solution and multiline comparisons with the conventional hybrid formulation. Localization limits the number of analytical contributions retained while maintaining close agreement with the conventional solution. A comparison with uncompensated temporal truncation demonstrates the importance of the residual correction in retaining the thermal effect of removed contributions. In the 500-line case, analytical and total simulation times are reduced by factors of approximately 25.5 and 3.92, respectively, with a relative nodal 2-norm difference of 0.139%.

</details>


### [42] [Consistency in fractional Korn's inequalities on Lipschitz domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33325)
*Tadele Mengesha,James M. Scott*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper studies fractional analogs of Korn's inequality on bounded Lipschitz domains, establishing constrained and unconstrained versions for all p in [1,∞] and s in (0,1), and analyzing degeneracy as s→1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Korn's inequality is central to elasticity and PDE theory; extending it to fractional Sobolev settings and widening the integrability range (especially endpoints p=1 and p=∞) addresses gaps in the literature and enables applications to nonlocal models.

Method: The authors prove fractional Korn inequalities (constrained and unconstrained) on bounded Lipschitz domains, including quotient spaces and spaces with boundary or volume constraints. A key tool is an explicit L^p-bounded projection operator onto infinitesimal rigid motions, used to quantitatively strengthen the inequalities; consistency as s→1 is examined.

Result: A fractional Korn first unconstrained inequality holds for vector fields in W^{s,p}(Ω;R^n) for all p in [1,∞] and s in (0,1). For p=1 and p=∞ no local analog exists; the universal constant stays stable for p in (1,∞) but degenerates at p∈{1,∞} as s→1.

Conclusion: Fractional Korn-type inequalities extend broadly in integrability and differentiability parameters, with endpoint cases p=1,∞ exhibiting genuinely nonlocal behavior; the explicit L^p-bounded rigid-motion projection provides a quantitative framework.

Abstract: We investigate fractional analogs of Korn's inequality on bounded Lipschitz domains in several contexts. Constrained and unconstrained inequalities are established; constraints include quotient spaces and spaces incorporating various classes of boundary conditions or volume constraints. We also expand the parameters of integrability for which the inequalities hold. Specifically, a fractional Korn's first unconstrained inequality holds for vector fields in the fractional Sobolev space $W^{s,p}(Ω;\mathbb{R}^n)$ for all $p \in [1,\infty]$ and all $s \in (0,1)$. In the cases $p = 1$ and $p = \infty$, the inequality has no local analog. We quantify this by investigating the consistency of the inequalities as the differentiability parameter $s \to 1$; the universal constant in the inequality remains stable for $p \in (1,\infty)$ but degenerates for $p \in \{1,\infty\}$. We introduce an explicit $L^p$-bounded projection operator onto infinitesimal rigid motions as a key tool to quantitatively strengthen the inequality.

</details>


### [43] [Initial Data Gluing for Asymptotically Hyperbolic Spacetimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33372)
*Aren Martinian*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: A new proof of Corvino-Schoen-type gluing (gluing up to linear obstructions) for asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes under smallness conditions, valid in arbitrary dimension and improving prior smallness requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing gluing results for asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes require restrictive smallness conditions on initial data (e.g., Chrusciél–Delay). The authors seek a more flexible technique that relaxes these requirements.

Method: A novel proof of Corvino-Schoen-type gluing up to linear obstructions, designed to be versatile and suited to highly anisotropic geometric settings, applied to asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes with appropriate smallness conditions in arbitrary dimension.

Result: The results recover and improve on previously known smallness requirements for such gluing, including those of Chrusciél and Delay, and hold in arbitrary dimension.

Conclusion: The technique is highly versatile and well-suited for gluing results in highly anisotropic geometric settings, offering a more general framework for Corvino-Schoen-type gluing in asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes.

Abstract: We give a new proof of Corvino-Schoen-type gluing, also known as gluing up to linear obstructions, for asymptotically hyperbolic spacetimes satisfying appropriate smallness conditions in arbitrary dimension. The results retrieve and improve on previous known smallness requirements on initial data to conduct such gluing, including the work of Chrusciél and Delay. Our technique is highly versatile and well-suited for gluing results in highly anisotropic geometric settings.

</details>


### [44] [Rigidity of Convex Entire Solutions to Sum Hessian Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33397)
*Changyu Ren,Xianlu Sun*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Every C^2 convex entire solution of the sum Hessian equation with positive constant RHS is a quadratic polynomial for 2≤k≤n and α≥0, with no growth assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Establish rigidity (quadratic-polynomial classification) of convex entire solutions to sum Hessian equations without imposing growth conditions at infinity.

Method: A dimension-lifting identity reduces the sum Hessian equation to a standard k-Hessian equation in one higher dimension; existing rigidity theorems are then applied. The linear case k=1 is also discussed.

Result: For 2≤k≤n, α≥0, and constant positive right-hand side, every C^2 convex entire solution is a quadratic polynomial.

Conclusion: The dimension-lifting technique yields unconditional rigidity for sum Hessian equations; the k=1 (linear) case is treated separately.

Abstract: We study the rigidity of convex entire solutions to sum Hessian equations. Using a dimension-lifting identity, we reduce the problem to a standard \(k\)-Hessian equation in one higher dimension. By applying existing rigidity theorems, we prove that every \(C^2\) convex entire solution is a quadratic polynomial for \(2\leq k\leq n\), \(α\geq0\), and a positive constant right-hand side, without any growth assumption at infinity. We also discuss the linear case \(k=1\).

</details>


### [45] [Observability Inequalities for Degenerate Schrödinger Equations via a Shape Approximation Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33406)
*Dong-Hui Yang,Hongli Sun,Weijia Wu,Yuanzhi Zhou*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves a boundary observability inequality for a 2D Schrödinger equation with a boundary-degenerate diffusion matrix A = diag(1, x_2^α), α ∈ (0,1), using a shape-design and approximation procedure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The diffusion operator degenerates on a portion of the boundary, which creates technical difficulties (e.g., loss of uniform coercivity, non-standard boundary behavior) that prevent a direct derivation of a boundary observability inequality.

Method: They remove a thin neighborhood of the degenerate boundary to build a family of uniformly (non-degenerate) Schrödinger equations on perturbed domains. They analyze spectral properties of the associated operators, prove convergence of the approximate problems to the degenerate one, derive boundary observability inequalities for the approximating equations via multiplier methods, and pass to the limit as the layer thickness tends to zero. Tools include spectral analysis, separation of variables, and shape design.

Result: Convergence of the approximate problems to the original degenerate problem is established, and a boundary observability inequality is obtained for the degenerate Schrödinger equation by taking the degenerate limit of the inequalities for the uniformly approximating problems.

Conclusion: The shape-design/approximation approach successfully circumvents boundary-degeneracy difficulties and yields a boundary observability inequality for the 2D degenerate Schrödinger equation with A = diag(1, x_2^α).

Abstract: We study a two-dimensional Schrödinger equation with a boundary degeneracy. More precisely, the diffusion matrix is given by $
  A=\operatorname{diag}(1,x_2^α), α\in(0,1)$,
  so that the partial differential operator degenerates on a portion of the boundary. The main objective is to establish a boundary observability inequality for the corresponding degenerate Schrödinger equation.
  To overcome the difficulties caused by the boundary degeneracy, we introduce a shape design and approximation procedure. By removing a thin neighborhood of the degenerate boundary, we construct a family of uniformly Schrödinger equations on perturbed domains. We first investigate the spectral properties of the associated operators and establish the convergence of the approximate problems to the original degenerate problem. We then derive a boundary observability inequality for the uniformly approximating equations and pass to the limit as the thickness of the removed boundary layer tends to zero. This yields a boundary observability inequality for the original degenerate Schrödinger equation.
  Our approach combines spectral analysis, separation of variables, and shape design techniques. By employing multiplier methods for the uniformly approximating problems and passing to the degenerate limit, we establish the boundary observability inequality for the original degenerate Schrödinger equation.

</details>


### [46] [Extremals for Trudinger-Moser inequalities via polygonal profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33439)
*José Francisco de Oliveira*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves existence of extremal functions for the Trudinger–Moser inequality with logarithmic weights $(-\log|x|)^\beta$ in two dimensions for all $0\le\beta<1$, and improves the classical critical supremum on the unit disk to $\mathrm{TM}_{4\pi}>4.38421793217\pi$ via an explicit 30-node polygonal profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existence of extremals for the weighted Trudinger–Moser inequality was incomplete over the full weight range, and recent results on local maximizers near the critical threshold needed extension. Additionally, the classical critical supremum value on the unit disk (previously only estimated at $4.3556\pi$ by computer experiments) lacked a rigorous, elementary proof.

Method: The authors construct polygonal profiles to derive a lower bound that is uniform in the weight parameter $\beta$. An explicit thirty-node profile is used with elementary inequalities and exact rational arithmetic to give a purely analytic proof of the bound.

Result: Existence of extremal functions is established for all $0\le\beta<1$; local-maximizer existence is extended beyond the critical threshold. The thirty-node profile yields $\mathrm{TM}_{4\pi}>4.38421793217\pi$, improving the prior $4.3556\pi$. Numerical experiments with up to 102400 nodes suggest the bound can reach $4.402732044\pi$, improving Li and Sun's computational lower bound.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the existence question across the full weight range and provides the first fully analytic, elementary proof of an improved lower bound for the classical critical Trudinger–Moser supremum, with numerics indicating further attainable improvements.

Abstract: The aim of this paper is to establish the existence of extremal functions for the Trudinger--Moser inequality with logarithmic weights $(-\log |x|)^β$ in two dimensions throughout the full range $0\le β<1$. We also extend recent results on the existence of local maximizers for the Trudinger--Moser functional beyond the critical threshold. Our approach uses polygonal profiles to establish a lower bound uniform in the weight parameter. An explicit thirty-node profile gives the lower bound $\mathrm{TM}_{4π}>4.38421793217\,π$ for the classical critical supremum on the unit disk, improving on the value $4.3556\,π$ obtained in the computer experiments of Carleson and Chang. This lower bound has a purely analytic proof, which uses only elementary inequalities and exact rational arithmetic. Numerical experiments with polygonal profiles of up to $102400$ nodes suggest that this lower bound can be raised to $4.402732044\,π$, which improves the recent computational lower bound by Li and Sun.

</details>


### [47] [Blow-up analysis and compactness for critical Dirac equations on $\mathbb S^n$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33507)
*Ting Li,Zhongwei Tang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper analyzes blow-up and compactness for a critical nonlinear Dirac equation on the round sphere S^n (n≥3) with variable coefficients H_i → H. Under a first-threshold critical mass bound, blow-up sequences concentrate at a unique point maximizing H, with rescaling to a ground-state Dirac bubble; sharp estimates, Green limit, and a Pohozaev obstruction (Hess H(p)=0) are derived, yielding uniform L^∞ bounds when H has negative Laplacian at global maxima.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical nonlinear Dirac equations on curved manifolds exhibit blow-up behavior that is poorly understood, particularly how variable coefficients and geometry influence concentration. The authors aim to characterize blow-up points, quantify concentration, and establish compactness (uniform boundedness) criteria under critical mass constraints.

Method: They bound critical mass at the first threshold and analyze blow-up sequences. A second rescaling plus Kelvin transform shows the blow-up point is isolated and simple; they derive pointwise and gradient estimates in cylindrical coordinates and the normalized Green limit. A Pohozaev identity is applied when coefficients converge in C^2 to obtain Hessian obstructions.

Result: Blow-up sequences (subsequentially) have a unique blow-up point p maximizing H, with rescaled solutions converging to a ground-state Dirac bubble carrying all limiting critical mass. The point is isolated and simple, with sharp pointwise/gradient estimates and an identified Green limit. Under C^2 convergence, Hess H(p)=0, and if ΔH(p)<0 at every global maximum, solutions slightly above first threshold are uniformly L^∞ bounded, stable under small C^2 perturbations.

Conclusion: Blow-up for this critical Dirac equation is governed by the maximum of H and obstructed by a Pohozaev/Hessian condition, giving a compactness criterion: negative Laplacian of H at global maxima ensures uniform boundedness near the first critical-mass threshold, robust to small coefficient perturbations.

Abstract: We study blow-up and compactness for the critical nonlinear Dirac equation \[ D_{g_0}ψ_i =
H_i|ψ_i|^{\frac{2}{n-1}}ψ_i \qquad \text{on }\mathbb S^n,\quad n\ge 3, \] where $g_0$ is the round metric and $H_i\to H>0$ in $C^0(\mathbb S^n)$. Under a first-threshold bound on the critical mass, we prove that every blowing-up sequence has, after passing to a subsequence, a unique blow-up point $p$ satisfying $H(p)=\max_{\mathbb S^n}H$. The rescaled solutions converge to a ground-state Dirac bubble that carries the entire limiting critical mass. Under local uniform $C^1$ bounds on $H_i$, we use the Kelvin transform and a second rescaling to prove that $p$ is an isolated simple blow-up point. We also derive sharp pointwise and gradient estimates in cylindrical coordinates and identify the normalized Green limit.
  If the coefficients converge in $C^2$ near $p$, the Pohozaev identity yields $\operatorname{Hess}_{g_0}H(p)=0$. Consequently, if a positive $H\in C^2(\mathbb S^n)$ satisfies $Δ_{g_0}H(p)<0$ at every global maximum, then solutions with critical mass bounded by a level slightly above the first threshold are uniformly bounded in $L^\infty(\mathbb S^n)$. This bound remains uniform under small $C^2$ perturbations of $H$.

</details>


### [48] [Mass concentration and ground states of Fermi systems in Hartree--Fock model with potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33530)
*Zhuocheng Li,Yuan-da Wu,Xiaoyu Zeng*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies collapse of finite fermionic Hartree–Fock ground states at a critical attractive interaction, establishing a finite-rank Hartree-type GNS inequality, a critical threshold K^(N), and sharp concentration asymptotics determined by the geometry of the trapping potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the onset and mechanism of collapse at critical attractive interaction is a fundamental problem in variational theory of finite fermionic systems. Unlike scalar Hartree problems, the nonlocal interaction here is coupled with pairwise L^2-orthogonality constraints, which complicates compactness and blow-up analysis.

Method: Works within the Hartree–Fock framework under various trapping potentials. Establishes a finite-rank Hartree-type Gagliardo–Nirenberg–Sobolev inequality to identify a critical threshold K^(N) for ground state existence. Analyzes concentration of ground states as the interaction parameter approaches K^(N), examining three representative potential classes (polynomial-type, ring-shaped, and potentials with zero set of nonempty interior) to derive concentration scales and sharp least-energy asymptotics.

Result: Identifies the critical threshold K^(N) governing ground state existence. For the three potential classes, derives the concentration scale and sharp asymptotics of the least energy, showing qualitatively different regimes: both the location and rate of concentration are selected by the local geometry of the potential near its minimum set.

Conclusion: The critical collapse phenomenon is governed by a threshold from a finite-rank Hartree-type inequality, and the trap's local geometry deterministically selects concentration location and rate. The interplay between nonlocal interaction and pairwise L^2-orthogonality constraints is the central source of analytical difficulty.

Abstract: Understanding the onset and mechanism of collapse at critical attractive interaction is a fundamental problem in the variational theory of finite fermionic systems. In this paper, we study this problem within the Hartree--Fock framework under various trapping potentials. By establishing a finite-rank Hartree-type Gagliardo--Nirenberg--Sobolev inequality, we identify a critical threshold $\mathcal{K}^{(N)}$ governing the existence of ground states. We then determine the concentration behavior of ground states as the interaction parameter approaches $\mathcal{K}^{(N)}$. To reveal how the geometry of the trap influences this critical phenomenon, we consider three representative classes of potentials: polynomial-type potentials, ring-shaped potentials, and potentials whose zero set has nonempty interior. For each class, we derive the concentration scale and sharp asymptotics of the least energy. The resulting regimes are qualitatively different, showing that both the location and the rate of concentration are selected by the local geometry of the potential near its minimum set. In contrast to scalar Hartree problems, the nonlocal interaction is coupled here with pairwise $L^2$-orthogonality constraints, and their interplay creates substantial difficulties in the compactness and blow-up analysis.

</details>


### [49] [Uniform boundary observability for periodic waves, I: up to the $\varepsilon^{-1}$ eigenvalue scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33547)
*Jun Geng,Shumao Wang,Wei Wang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: For the scalar periodic divergence-form operator, the authors prove boundary observability and projected exact controllability uniformly for spectral truncation levels N ≤ δε⁻¹, improving the previous N ≲ ε⁻¹/² cutoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work by Lin and Shen (JEMS 2022) only achieved uniform observability/controllability for spectral truncation N ≲ ε⁻¹/². Extending this range toward N ∼ ε⁻¹ is desirable for understanding high-frequency control of highly oscillatory (homogenized) operators.

Method: The key input is a two-sided boundary estimate for fixed-width spectral packets of √L_ε at frequencies satisfying εμ² ≪ 1. Its lower bound combines quantitative Dirichlet corrector estimates with a forcing-stable microscopic boundary Cauchy estimate. Miller's spectral packet criterion and the Hilbert uniqueness method (HUM) then convert the packet estimate into the corresponding wave observability and controllability results, with constants independent of ε and N.

Result: Whole-boundary observability and projected exact controllability hold uniformly in the range N ≤ δε⁻¹ (constants independent of ε and N), extending the earlier N ≲ ε⁻¹/² cutoff.

Conclusion: Uniform spectral observability/controllability for periodic divergence-form operators extends up to spectral level N ∼ ε⁻¹, achieved via a two-sided boundary estimate for spectral packets and standard wave-equation machinery (Miller's criterion, HUM).

Abstract: Let $\mathcal{L}_\varepsilon=-\operatorname{div}(A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla)$ be a scalar divergence-form operator on a bounded $C^3$ domain, where $A$ is symmetric, uniformly elliptic, Lipschitz continuous, and periodic. For initial data spectrally truncated at the eigenvalue level $N$, we establish whole-boundary observability and projected exact controllability, uniformly in the range $N\leqδ\varepsilon^{-1}$. This extends the cutoff $N\lesssim\varepsilon^{-1/2}$ obtained by Lin and Shen (J. Eur. Math. Soc. 2022). The key input is a two-sided boundary estimate for fixed-width spectral packets of $\sqrt{\mathcal{L}_\varepsilon}$ at frequencies that satisfy $\varepsilonμ^2\ll1$. Its lower bound combines quantitative Dirichlet corrector estimates with a forcing-stable microscopic boundary Cauchy estimate. Miller's spectral packet criterion and the Hilbert uniqueness method then convert this packet estimate into the corresponding wave results, with constants independent of $\varepsilon$ and $N$.

</details>


### [50] [Concave entire solutions of the Liouville equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33571)
*Changfeng Gui,Qinfeng Li,Chunhua Wang,Lu Xu*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Every concave entire solution of the Liouville equation −Δu=e^{2u} in R^n is one-dimensional, a result extended to a class of general semilinear equations; a PDE-based proof is given for the planar quasiconcave classification without complex analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concave solutions of the Liouville equation and related semilinear problems are not fully understood in higher dimensions, and prior classification of quasiconcave planar solutions relied on complex-analytic (Bergweiler-Eremenko-Langley) methods.

Method: Prove that concave entire solutions are one-dimensional by direct PDE analysis, generalize to semilinear equations under conditions on the nonlinearity, and give an alternative PDE-based argument in the planar quasiconcave case.

Result: Concavity forces all entire solutions of −Δu=e^{2u} in R^n to be one-dimensional; the result extends to general semilinear equations; and the planar quasiconcave classification is reproved via PDE methods.

Conclusion: Concavity imposes strong rigidity on entire solutions, yielding a unified PDE framework that avoids complex-analytic tools.

Abstract: We prove that every concave entire solution of the Liouville equation $-Δu=e^{2u}$ in $\R^n$ is one-dimensional, and we extend the result to a class of general semilinear equations under suitable conditions on the nonlinearity. In dimension two, we also provide a PDE-based proof of the known classification of quasiconcave solutions to planar Liouville solutions, without relying on complex-analytic methods developed in the previous work by Bergweiler-Eremenko-Langley \cite{BEL}

</details>


### [51] [Multiplicity of weak solutions for mixed variable exponent triharmonic problems with hardy potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33576)
*Khalid Soualhine,Adnane Belakhdar,Mohamed karim Hamdani*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves the existence of at least three distinct weak solutions for a sixth-order boundary value problem driven by mixed variable-exponent triharmonic operators plus a Hardy-type singular potential, using variational methods and abstract three-critical-point theorems for small μ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sixth-order problems combining mixed variable exponents with Hardy-type singular potentials have not been systematically treated; standard Hardy-Rellich inequalities and critical point tools do not directly apply to nonstandard (variable-exponent) growth settings, motivating new adapted inequalities and a multiplicity analysis.

Method: Variational approach on variable-exponent Sobolev spaces: it combines Ricceri's and Bonanno-Marano's abstract three critical points theorems with newly derived Hardy-Rellich inequalities adapted to the variable-exponent setting, under suitable assumptions on the exponents p(x), q(x), s(x), the weights a, b, m, and the nonlinearity f.

Result: At least three distinct weak solutions are established for the problem on a smooth bounded domain Ω ⊂ R^N (N > 3), for sufficiently small values of the parameter μ.

Conclusion: The work provides the first systematic treatment of sixth-order problems featuring simultaneously mixed variable exponents and Hardy-type singular potentials, demonstrating the effectiveness of the combined abstract multiplicity theorems with variable-exponent Hardy-Rellich inequalities.

Abstract: This paper is devoted to the study of a nonlinear boundary value problem driven by a combination of mixed variable-exponent triharmonic operators and a Hardy-type singular potential. Specifically, we consider \[ \begin{cases} -a(x)\,Δ_{p(x)}^{3}u - b(x)\,Δ_{q(x)}^{3}u + m(x)\,\dfrac{|u|^{s(x)-2}u}{|x|^{3s(x)}} = μf(x,u), & \text{in } Ω, \\ u = Δu = Δ^{2}u = 0, & \text{on } \partialΩ, \end{cases} \] where $Ω\subset \mathbb{R}^N$ is a smooth bounded domain with $N > 3$. The operators $Δ_{p(x)}^{3}$ and $Δ_{q(x)}^{3}$ represent triharmonic operators with variable exponents, creating a framework of nonstandard growth. Under suitable assumptions on the variable exponents $p(x), q(x), s(x)$, the weight functions $a(x), b(x), m(x)$, and the nonlinearity $f(x,u)$, we establish the existence of at least three distinct weak solutions. Our approach is variational, relying on critical point theory for functionals defined on variable-exponent Sobolev spaces. By combining Ricceri's and Bonanno-Marano's abstract three critical points theorems with new Hardy-Rellich inequalities adapted to the variable-exponent setting, we prove our main multiplicity result for small values of the parameter $μ$. To our knowledge, this work presents the first systematic treatment of sixth-order problems featuring simultaneously mixed variable exponents and Hardy-type singular potentials.

</details>


### [52] [Asymptotic analysis of Dirichlet problems in supercritically perforated domains via quantification by torsional rigidity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33621)
*Naoto Sato*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The authors identify torsional rigidity T(D^ε) as the natural normalization for Dirichlet solutions u^ε on varying domains that vanish in the supercritical regime. After normalization, u^ε/T(D^ε) converges weakly to kf with k a nonzero finite "effective measure" independent of the source f.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In the supercritical regime (e.g., periodically perforated domains) solutions vanish in the limit for every source, so the usual homogenization normalization fails. A natural scaling is needed to capture the limiting behavior of the solutions and understand what effective object governs the limit.

Method: The core ingredient is an estimate comparing u^ε with f times the torsion function, with a rate depending on the domains only through T(D^ε). This yields weak convergence of u^ε/T(D^ε) to kf. The authors show the rate is optimal, work without structural assumptions on perforations, allow relaxed Dirichlet problems, and characterize convergence for rough sources via torsion functions or Poincaré constants.

Result: Up to subsequences, u^ε/T(D^ε) ⇀ kf weakly as measures for every continuous f, where k is a nonzero finite effective measure independent of f. The rate is optimal, and every nonzero finite measure can arise as an effective measure (so continuity of the source cannot be dropped). The effective measure is local, satisfies optimal two-sided bounds, and is monotone in the coefficients; the role of antisymmetric parts of A^ε is also examined.

Conclusion: Torsional rigidity is the natural normalization in the supercritical vanishing regime, and the limit is described by an effective measure replacing the classical homogenized solution. The framework is general (no perforation structure assumed, relaxed Dirichlet allowed), and the effective measure has strong structural properties, though generality requires restrictions on sources.

Abstract: We study the Dirichlet problem $-\mathrm{div}(A^{\varepsilon}\nabla u^{\varepsilon}) = f$ on varying open sets $D^{\varepsilon}$, with uniformly elliptic coefficients $A^{\varepsilon}$, in the regime where the solutions vanish in the limit for every source. For periodically perforated domains, this is the supercritical regime. We show that the torsional rigidity $T(D^{\varepsilon})$ is the natural normalization: up to a subsequence, $u^{\varepsilon}/T(D^{\varepsilon})$ converges weakly as measures to $kf$ for every continuous source $f$, where $k$ is a nonzero finite measure independent of $f$, which we call the effective measure. The key ingredient is an estimate comparing $u^{\varepsilon}$ with the product of $f$ and the torsion function, whose rate depends on the domains only through $T(D^{\varepsilon})$. We also show that this rate is optimal. No structural assumption on the perforation is needed, and the results hold for relaxed Dirichlet problems. Every nonzero finite measure arises as an effective measure, so the continuity of the source cannot be dropped in general. For rougher sources, we characterize the convergence by a condition on the domains alone, expressed through their torsion functions or, equivalently, their Poincaré constants. We also prove that the effective measure is local, satisfies optimal two-sided bounds and is monotone with respect to the coefficients, and we study the role of the antisymmetric parts of the coefficients.

</details>


### [53] [Improved Global Boundedness Criteria via Refined Bootstrap Estimates for a Quasilinear Chemotaxis-May-Nowak System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33657)
*Xueli Bai,Fang Li,Suying Liu,Xuxuan Luo*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Improved global existence and uniform boundedness criteria are derived for quasilinear chemotaxis–May–Nowak systems with nonlinear diffusion, via two refined estimates that lower the required diffusion exponent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previously known sufficient conditions for global boundedness in quasilinear chemotaxis–May–Nowak systems are restrictive; the authors aim to weaken them by sharper estimates that exploit the infection source's structure.

Method: Analysis of a parabolic–elliptic–parabolic system and a fully parabolic system with nonlinear diffusion growing at least like s^{m-1}. Two refined estimates: (i) weighted space–time control of the nonlinear infection source giving low-order L^q bounds on free virus density to start a bootstrap; (ii) preserving the product structure of the source in interpolation so the chemotactic term is absorbed more efficiently by nonlinear diffusion.

Result: Global boundedness for parabolic–elliptic–parabolic when m>3−4/n for n∈{2,3} and m>1+n/4 for n≥4; for the fully parabolic system, the same holds under m>1+n/4 for n≥4.

Conclusion: The refined estimates improve previously known sufficient conditions for global boundedness of these chemotaxis–May–Nowak systems.

Abstract: We study a quasilinear chemotaxis--May--Nowak system with nonlinear diffusion growing at least as $s^{m-1}$ at high densities and derive improved criteria for global existence and uniform boundedness. For the parabolic--elliptic--parabolic system, we establish global boundedness when $m>3-\frac{4}{n}$ for $n\in\{2,3\}$ and when $m>1+\frac{n}{4}$ for $n\ge4$. For the fully parabolic system, the same conclusion holds under $m>1+\frac{n}{4}$ for $n\ge4$. The proof relies on two refined estimates. Weighted space--time control of the nonlinear infection source yields the low-order $L^q$-bounds for the free virus density needed to initiate the bootstrap in the parabolic--elliptic--parabolic case. Subsequently, preserving the product structure of this source in the interpolation argument allows the chemotactic contribution to be absorbed more efficiently by the nonlinear diffusion. These estimates improve the previously known sufficient conditions for global boundedness.

</details>


### [54] [Global Boundedness and Finite-Time Blow-up for a Quasilinear Chemotaxis--May--Nowak System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33664)
*Xueli Bai,Fang Li,Suying Liu*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper extends prior work on a quasilinear chemotaxis–May–Nowak system, proving global boundedness in 3D for m>4/3 in both parabolic–elliptic–parabolic and fully parabolic versions, and constructing finite-time blow-up solutions in balls below this threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuing the work of Bai, Li, Liu and Luo, the authors aim to sharpen the understanding of the critical exponent governing boundedness versus blow-up for a quasilinear chemotaxis–May–Nowak system.

Method: The authors prove global boundedness of solutions in three dimensions for both the parabolic–elliptic–parabolic and fully parabolic systems when m>4/3, and construct finite-time blow-up solutions in balls via suitable energy/ODE arguments.

Result: Global boundedness holds in 3D for m>4/3 in both system variants. Finite-time blow-up occurs in balls when n=3 with 1<m≤4/3, and when n≥4 with 0<m≤2−2/n.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a sharp boundedness/blow-up dichotomy for the quasilinear chemotaxis–May–Nowak system, identifying m=4/3 (in 3D) and m=2−2/n as critical thresholds separating global bounded solutions from finite-time blow-up.

Abstract: This paper continues the work of Bai, Li, Liu and Luo \cite{BaiLiLiuLuoPreprint} on a quasilinear chemotaxis--May--Nowak system. We establish global boundedness in three dimensions for both the parabolic--elliptic--parabolic and fully parabolic systems when $m>4/3$. For the former, we construct finite-time blow-up solutions in balls when $n=3$ and $1<m\le4/3$, and when $n\ge4$ and $0<m\le2-2/n$.

</details>


### [55] [Pseudospectral phenomena preventing the analyticity sector of a Ornstein-Uhlenbeck semigroup to be ruled by the spectral angle of its generator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33683)
*Karel Pravda-Starov*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies elliptic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operators in $L^2$ spaces with respect to invariant measures, which have discrete spectra and generate analytic semigroups, and characterizes when the analyticity angle of the semigroup equals the complementary angle of the generator's spectral angle via a necessary and sufficient criterion, with pseudospectral phenomena explaining failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The angle defining the analyticity sector of an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck semigroup is not always the complementary angle of the spectral angle of its generator; the general relation between these two angles is not understood.

Method: Analyze elliptic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operators in $L^2$ spaces with respect to invariant measures, comparing the analyticity sector angle of the generated semigroup with the complementary spectral angle of the generator, and use pseudospectral analysis to diagnose discrepancies.

Result: A necessary and sufficient criterion is provided ensuring the two angles are complementary, and pseudospectral phenomena are shown to account for their failure to be complementary.

Conclusion: The complementarity of the analyticity angle and the complementary spectral angle is fully characterized; pseudospectra explain when and why this complementarity fails.

Abstract: We study elliptic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operators in $L^2$ spaces with respect to invariant measures. These operators have discrete spectra and generate analytic semigroups. The angle defining the analyticity sector of a Ornstein-Uhlenbeck semigroup is not always equal to the complementary angle of the spectral angle of its generator. In this work, we study the link between these two angles.
