Conclusion: The boundary-adapted Jacobi spectral-Galerkin approach offers an efficient quantum route for elliptic PDEs with favorable scaling in precision, though functional extraction still suffers an O(1/ε) overhead.

Abstract: This paper presents an efficient quantum spectral-Galerkin method for constant-coefficient second-order linear elliptic equations. The method employs a boundary-adapted Jacobi tensor-product basis that incorporates the boundary condition into the approximation space and produces Galerkin systems with polynomially bounded condition numbers and sparsity. For analytic solutions satisfying a suitable uniform regularity condition, spectral convergence allows the normalized Galerkin coefficient state to be prepared with complexity $\mathrm{poly}(d,\log(1/ε))$, where $d$ is the spatial dimension and $ε$ is the error tolerance, under standard sparse-access and state-preparation assumptions. At the state-preparation level, this improves the precision dependence over fixed-order finite-difference and finite-element discretizations. We further show how to estimate prescribed linear functionals of the solution, with an additional $O(1/ε)$ factor arising from norm and inner-product estimation.

</details>


### [115] [Autonomous atomistic-continuum coupling through uncertainty-aware active learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33272)
*Daniel Abdulah,Hariswaran Sitaraman,Ross E. Larsen,Kevin Patrick Griffin,Grace Wei,Janelle Domantay,Ethan Young,Marc T. Henry de Frahan*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: An uncertainty-aware surrogate couples atomistic KMC surface chemistry with a continuum fluid solver for halide-perovskite vapor deposition, using predicted uncertainties to trigger new KMC runs on demand, achieving 10-20% accuracy with 100-1000x fewer atomistic simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct coupling of atomistic KMC with continuum fluid dynamics across disparate length and time scales is computationally prohibitive, since it requires running expensive atomistic simulations at every continuum step. A more efficient coupling scheme is needed that preserves accuracy.

Method: The authors build a surrogate model that predicts boundary mass fluxes for six species along with their uncertainties. The continuum solver monitors these uncertainties and automatically triggers additional KMC simulations when new training data are needed (autonomous online learning). The KMC solver uses a DFT-calibrated energetics model constructed in this work and provides net deposition and desorption rates for six species. The approach is demonstrated on reactive halide-perovskite vapor deposition with a multi-species vapor jet impinging on a reactive substrate.

Result: With a conservative uncertainty threshold, the surrogate-coupled approach reproduces cumulative mass fluxes of direct coupling with errors typically within 10-20%, while capturing non-monotonic dependence of net deposition on species partial pressure. Species-resolved mass fluxes provide a sufficiently low-dimensional representation of the atomistic response, so detailed crystal structure need not be explicitly represented. The online-learning scheme reduces required atomistic simulations by two to three orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-aware surrogate coupling enables efficient, accuracy-controlled multiscale simulation of reactive vapor deposition, showing that species-resolved mass fluxes suffice to represent atomistic response without explicit crystal structure and cutting atomistic simulation cost by 100-1000x.

Abstract: We develop an uncertainty-aware surrogate modeling approach for coupling an atomistic kinetic Monte Carlo (KMC) surface-chemistry solver with a continuum multi-species fluid dynamics solver across disparate physical length and time scales. The surrogate model predicts boundary mass fluxes for six species and their uncertainties, which the continuum solver monitors to automatically trigger additional KMC simulations when new training data are needed. This enables efficient multiscale coupling and maintains a user-specified level of accuracy. We demonstrate the approach for reactive halide-perovskite vapor deposition, which involves a multi-species vapor jet impinging on a reactive substrate. The KMC solver uses a DFT-calibrated energetics model that we construct as part of this work and provides net deposition and desorption rates for six species. With a conservative uncertainty threshold, the surrogate-coupled approach reproduces cumulative mass fluxes of the directly coupled approach with errors typically within 10-20%, while capturing the non-monotonic dependence of net deposition on species partial pressure. Importantly, the results show that detailed crystal structure need not be explicitly represented to predict quantities exchanged between atomistic and continuum solvers; species-resolved mass fluxes provide a sufficiently low-dimensional representation of the atomistic response. For this accuracy target, the autonomous online-learning approach reduces the number of required atomistic simulations by two to three orders of magnitude relative to direct multiscale coupling.

</details>


### [116] [A neural accelerated surface-roughness-based Discrete Element Method (SR-DEM) for modeling granular systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33363)
*Xiwei Pan,Liuchi Li*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The authors propose SR-DEM, a surface-roughness-based Discrete Element Method framework that replaces phenomenological friction laws with explicit resolution of grain surface topography, letting friction emerge from collective frictionless asperity interactions. To manage cost, they add GPU-parallelized FE²-like and FiLM-based neural surrogate accelerators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DEM frameworks all rely on a phenomenological friction law (typically Amontons-Coulomb) to model grain-grain friction. This is a crude, non-physics-based assumption for disordered granular media. Inspired by tribology research, the authors aim for a more physics-based treatment of contact friction.

Method: In SR-DEM, each grain's surface topography is explicitly resolved, and friction emerges from collective frictionless interactions of surface asperities rather than an imposed friction law—trading phenomenology for physics at higher computational cost. To mitigate cost, they introduce an FE²-like algorithm and a FiLM-based neural network surrogate, both parallelized on GPU to accelerate contact computation, and apply the framework in 2D.

Result: Using SR-DEM in 2D, they (1) demonstrate that a two-body-based Amontons-Coulomb-type friction behavior can be recovered with a simple surface topography representation, and (2) identify many-body-based frictional behaviors—specifically angle of repose values—that current DEM frameworks cannot always capture at the same level of model complexity.

Conclusion: SR-DEM offers a physics-based alternative to phenomenological friction in DEM, capable of both recovering classical friction behavior and revealing many-body effects beyond current frameworks. The authors discuss future directions and potential opportunities for applications including suspension rheology design and metamaterial mechanics.

Abstract: The Discrete Element Method (DEM) is an indispensable tool for modeling and understanding the basic mechanical behaviors of (dry) granular media, a class of disordered material systems of significant interest to multiple scientific domains from (geo-)physics to (chemical) engineering. At the core of all present DEM frameworks is a phenomenological friction law--commonly chosen to be of the Amontons-Coulomb type--that models the frictional interaction among constituent grains. Here, inspired by research in the tribology community, we present an alternative, fundamentally different framework, termed surface-roughness-based DEM (SR-DEM), in which contact friction is handled in a more physics-based manner. In this framework, instead of using a friction law, we explicitly resolve the surface topography of each grain and let friction emerge from collective frictionless interactions of surface asperities. In essence, we trade phenomenology for physics at the cost of an increased computational demand. To mitigate the cost, we introduce an FE$^2$-like algorithm and a feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM)-based neural network surrogate, both of which are parallelized on GPU to accelerate contact computation. Crucially, using this framework in 2D, we (1) demonstrate the possibility of recovering a two-body-based Amontons-Coulomb-type friction behavior using a simple representation of surface topography, and (2) identify many-body-based frictional behaviors--more specifically, the angle of repose values--that cannot always be captured by current DEM frameworks under the same level of model complexity. We conclude by discussing future directions for SR-DEM, as well as exciting new opportunities it could unlock for a wide range of emerging applications, including (designing) suspension rheology and metamaterial mechanics.

</details>


### [117] [A Differentiable Framework for Inverse Identification of Heterogeneous Cohesive Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33711)
*Sida Hao,Jinkyo Han,Bahador Bahmani*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A differentiable finite-element framework using physics-constrained neural networks and discrete adjoints identifies traction-separation laws (cohesive laws) from global force-displacement data and sparse strain measurements, including spatially heterogeneous responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global force-displacement responses alone are insufficient to recover underlying cohesive laws; the inverse problem is high-dimensional and history-dependent, requiring a differentiable, regularized identification approach.

Method: Differentiable FEM with intrinsic cohesive-zone formulation; physics-constrained neural networks represent thermodynamically structured cohesive laws; automatic differentiation plus a discrete adjoint for history-dependent damage evolution; a shared low-dimensional family of cohesive laws with latent coordinates enables spatially heterogeneous, irreversible interfacial damage.

Result: On double-cantilever beams and fiber-matrix composites, matching global force-displacement responses does not guarantee accurate recovery of cohesive laws; spatially resolved strain data substantially improves identification even under a learned prior; the approach outperforms bilinear cohesive models and Bayesian optimization.

Conclusion: Sparse strain measurements are essential for reliable constitutive identification, and learned, regularized constitutive priors with adjoint-based sensitivities offer clear advantages for high-dimensional, history-dependent inverse problems.

Abstract: This work develops a differentiable finite element framework based on an intrinsic cohesive-zone formulation for identifying traction-separation laws from global force-displacement histories and spatially sparse strain measurements. The framework employs physics-constrained neural networks to represent flexible, thermodynamically structured cohesive laws, with sensitivities computed through automatic differentiation and a discrete adjoint that accounts for the history-dependent evolution of interfacial damage. To enable identification of spatially heterogeneous cohesive behavior, we extend learned constitutive priors to irreversible interfacial damage by constructing a shared, low-dimensional family of cohesive laws from representative traction-separation histories. This representation exploits common constitutive structure and parameter sharing to reduce the number of unknowns and regularize the inverse problem, while allowing local cohesive behavior to vary spatially through latent coordinates. Numerical investigations involving double-cantilever beams and fiber-matrix composites demonstrate that accurate reproduction of global force-displacement responses does not necessarily imply accurate recovery of the underlying cohesive laws. Incorporating spatially resolved strain measurements substantially improves constitutive identification, even when the admissible responses are constrained by a learned prior. Comparisons with conventional bilinear cohesive models and Bayesian optimization further illustrate the benefits of the learned constitutive representation and adjoint-based identification for high-dimensional inverse problems.

</details>


### [118] [Two-dimensional framework for modeling load transfer in nanocomposites with thin, stiff, and prestressed reinforcements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34014)
*Rohit Satish Patil,Zhilin Han,Sofia G. Mogilevskaya*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A novel IGABEM framework based on Gurtin–Murdoch surface elasticity simulates load transfer in nanocomposites with thin, stiff, prestressed nanosheet/nanoplatelet reinforcements modeled as open material surfaces (curves) in plane strain, validated against benchmarks with parametric studies including a graphene nanoplatelet/epoxy example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing models struggle to capture load-transfer mechanisms in nanocomposites reinforced by thin, stiff, prestressed nanosheets/nanoplatelets, whose membrane-like elastic properties and residual surface tension, along with arbitrary curved shapes and interactions, need accurate treatment.

Method: Reinforcements are modeled as open material surfaces (membranes) with their own elasticity and residual surface tension, matrix as isotropic elastic. Uses Gurtin–Murdoch theory with surface traction jumps; matrix fields written as exact integral representations in terms of unknown traction jumps depending on second-order displacement derivatives. Solved with isogeometric BEM using NURBS via coupled singular boundary integral equations; then SIFs extracted at surface tips.

Result: Validated against benchmark solutions for multiple surfaces along straight segments or circular arcs; parametric studies assess effects of surface curvature, relative spacing, orientation, and interactions; a graphene nanoplatelet-reinforced epoxy example is presented.

Conclusion: The proposed IGABEM-based Gurtin–Murdoch framework provides an accurate and efficient means to simulate load transfer and stress fields in nanocomposites with arbitrarily shaped prestressed nanosheet reinforcements, capturing curvature and interaction effects and enabling SIF evaluation.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel framework for simulating load-transfer mechanisms in nanocomposites reinforced by thin, stiff, and prestressed reinforcements, such as, e.g., nanosheets or nanoplatelets. The reinforcements are modeled as open material surfaces treated as membranes endowed with their own elastic properties and residual surface tension; the matrix is assumed to be isotropic elastic. In the two-dimensional plane-strain setting considered here, each material surface is represented by a sufficiently smooth curve of otherwise arbitrary shape. The theoretical part of the framework is based on the Gurtin--Murdoch theory in which the surfaces are represented by traction jumps across them. The elastic fields in the matrix are exactly expressed by the integral representations written in terms of the unknown traction jumps that are functions of the second-order derivatives of the displacements. The computational part is based on the isogeometric boundary element method (IGABEM) that utilizes non-uniform rational B-splines (NURBS). The unknown traction jumps are found from the numerical solutions of the coupled system of singular boundary integral equations, after which the elastic fields in the matrix are evaluated. The procedure of extracting the values of the stress intensity factors (SIFs) at the tips of the surfaces is described. The proposed algorithm is validated against available benchmark solutions for multiple surfaces located along either straight segments or circular arcs. Selected parametric studies are performed to examine the influence of surface curvatures, relative spacing, orientation, and interactions. An example involving a graphene nanoplatelet-reinforced epoxy is presented.

</details>


### [119] [kstress: a LAMMPS package for calculating the 2D wavevector-dependent stress tensor and its anisotropy in amorphous solids](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34206)
*Olivier Benzerara,Jörg Baschnagel,Alexander N. Semenov,Liudmyla Klochko*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: An optimized LAMMPS module computes the wavevector- and time-resolved stress tensor for amorphous solids, with a validation protocol demonstrated on a 2D polydisperse Lennard-Jones glass, revealing q-dependent affine moduli and vibrational-frequency features relevant to the boson peak.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The q-resolved stress tensor is essential for accessing length-scale-dependent elastic moduli and long-range stress correlations that distinguish amorphous solids from liquids, but its evaluation requires summing over all interacting pairs via the MD force loop, making efficient, accurate computation a technical challenge.

Method: Two technical contributions: (i) an optimized implementation of the LAMMPS stress-tensor module where trigonometric functions are computed only for basic wavevectors and relevant wavevectors are supplied as an explicit list rather than a full grid, so only wavevectors of interest are computed and stored; (ii) a validation protocol illustrated with a 2D polydisperse Lennard-Jones glass-forming liquid.

Result: Using the validated code, the authors show the longitudinal affine modulus exhibits a minimum near q_max (the main structure-factor peak), and vibrational frequencies for both transverse and longitudinal modes peak at wavevectors well below q_max.

Conclusion: The optimized method enables efficient, validated q-resolved stress computation, and the observed low-q vibrational frequency peaks suggest potential relevance to the boson peak in amorphous solids.

Abstract: The stress tensor resolved in wavevector $\qvec$ and time $t$, $σ_{αβ}(\qvec,t)$, gives access to the elastic moduli of amorphous solids as a function of the probed length scale, and to the long-range stress correlations that distinguish an amorphous solid from a liquid. Its evaluation requires the contribution of every interacting pair and therefore an interface to the force loop of the molecular dynamics code. In the current work, we present: i) an optimized implementation of the LAMMPS~\cite{lammps} module for calculating $σ_{αβ}(\qvec,t)$, in which trigonometric functions are calculated only for the basic wavevectors and, in addition, all the relevant wavevectors are supplied as an explicit list rather than generated as a full grid, so that only the wavevectors of interest are computed and stored, and ii) a validation protocol for the code illustrated for a two-dimensional polydisperse Lennard-Jones (2DpLJ) glass-forming liquid. Based on these technical achievements we elucidated how the affine moduli for the 2DpLJ model depend on the norm $q$ of the wavevector. In particular, we demonstrate that the longitudinal affine modulus shows a minimum near $q_\text{max}$ corresponding to the main peak of the structure factor. Our data also point to the peaks in the $q$-dependencies of vibrational frequencies well below $q_\text{max}$ for both transverse and longitudinal modes. The potential relevance of this feature to the ``boson peak'' is discussed as well.

</details>


### [120] [A GPU-efficient local ADER finite-difference method for compressible-flow DNS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34252)
*Hong Zhang,Xian Wang,Xinliang Li,Yibing Chen,Xing Ji*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The authors propose a local ADER finite-difference method for GPU CFD that replaces multistage Runge-Kutta time advancement with a compact ten-point quadratic predictor and conservative central corrector, achieving ~1.94x speedup over RK on a V100 GPU while retaining third-order convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting CFD solvers to GPUs has focused on spatial discretization, while time advancement typically uses multistage Runge-Kutta methods. These RK methods require repeated residual evaluations and full-domain operations, inflating the cost of compressible-flow DNS on GPUs. The work aims to reduce this time-integration overhead.

Method: A local arbitrary high-order derivatives (ADER) finite-difference method couples a compact ten-point quadratic predictor to a conservative central corrector via operator matching. It uses two quadrature nodes, one Picard correction, and selective dissipation, keeping temporal states kernel-local while overlapping halo communication with interior computation.

Result: Smooth convective tests recover third-order convergence under fixed-CFL refinement and near-seventh-order spatial behavior when background error dominates. On a 302^3-node viscous Taylor-Green benchmark (no heat conduction), throughput reaches 230.87 million node updates per second on one V100 GPU in double precision—1.944x that of spatially matched three-stage RK. Weak-scaling efficiency is 99.44% from 4 to 32 V100 GPUs at 1.812 billion nodes.

Conclusion: The local ADER approach delivers a substantial GPU throughput advantage over RK-based time integration while maintaining accuracy and excellent weak scalability, making it an effective time-advancement strategy for compressible-flow DNS on GPUs.

Abstract: Algorithm design tailored to graphics processing units (GPUs) can substantially improve the efficiency of computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulations. Efforts to adapt CFD solvers to GPUs often focus on spatial discretization, while time advancement commonly relies on multistage Runge--Kutta (RK) methods. However, these RK methods require repeated residual evaluations and full-domain operations, increasing the cost of compressible-flow direct numerical simulation (DNS). To reduce this overhead, we develop a local arbitrary high-order derivatives (ADER) finite-difference method that couples a compact ten-point quadratic predictor to a conservative central corrector through operator matching. With two quadrature nodes, one Picard correction, and selective dissipation, smooth convective tests recover third-order convergence under fixed-CFL refinement and near-seventh-order spatial behavior when background spatial error dominates. Temporal states remain kernel-local, and halo communication overlaps interior computation. Tests of smooth advection, periodic turbulence, and wall-bounded flows assess accuracy and flow statistics. On a $302^3$-node viscous Taylor--Green benchmark without heat conduction, throughput reaches 230.87 million node updates per second on one V100 GPU in double precision, 1.944 times that of spatially matched three-stage Runge--Kutta integration. Weak-scaling efficiency reaches 99.44\% from 4 to 32 V100 GPUs at 1.812 billion nodes.

</details>


### [121] [Towards Exact Multidimensional Fluxes: A Structure-Preserving Data-Informed Framework for Riemann Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35265)
*Yucheng Zhang,Clint Dawson*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The authors show that the exact multidimensional space-time flux integral is polynomial in time step and face dimensions, and propose a structure-preserving learned Riemann solver that predicts the polynomial coefficients via a neural network trained on high-resolution data, yielding stable and efficient unsplit schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finite-volume and DG methods rely on space-time flux integrals over cell faces that depend on local multidimensional Riemann problems, which are hard to evaluate exactly due to complex wave interactions. The authors seek a tractable alternative to exact multidimensional Riemann solvers.

Method: They prove the exact space-time flux integral is polynomial in time step and face dimensions, so only the mapping from initial states to polynomial coefficients must be learned. A neural network trained on high-resolution simulation data learns this mapping within a structure-preserving, data-informed Riemann solver framework, with consistency and symmetry enforced by construction.

Result: On 2D Euler implosion and explosion problems, in an unsplit MUSCL-Hancock scheme the learned solver stays stable at CFL 0.9 where the exact 1D Riemann solver fails. At CFL 0.45 it is slightly more accurate than the 1D-solver scheme and runs about 30% faster.

Conclusion: Learning polynomial coefficients of the flux integral is a viable path to near-exact multidimensional flux integrals, giving more stable and efficient unsplit schemes, provided high-resolution data converge to exact integrals and the network predicts coefficients accurately.

Abstract: Finite-volume and discontinuous Galerkin methods update cells using the space-time flux integral over their faces, which typically depends on the solutions to local Riemann problems. In the multidimensional Riemann problem setting, this integral is difficult to evaluate exactly because of complex wave interactions. In this work, we show that the exact space-time flux integral is a polynomial in the time step and face dimensions. This motivates a learning-based approach, as only the mapping from the initial states to the polynomial coefficients needs to be learned. We propose a structure-preserving, data-informed Riemann solver framework that uses a neural network trained on high-resolution simulation data to learn this mapping. Key structural properties, including consistency and symmetry, are enforced by construction. Provided that the high-resolution simulations converge to the exact flux integrals and the network accurately predicts the coefficients, this framework yields near-exact flux integrals for use in numerical simulations.
  We demonstrate the framework using classical implosion and explosion problems for the two-dimensional Euler equations. In an unsplit MUSCL-Hancock scheme, the learned Riemann solver remains stable at a CFL number of 0.9, whereas the same scheme using the exact one-dimensional Riemann solver fails. Compared with the latter scheme at CFL=0.45, the learned scheme is slightly more accurate and requires approximately 30% less computational time.

</details>


<div id='physics.plasm-ph'></div>

# physics.plasm-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [122] [An In-depth Study of Stochastic Stellarator Coil Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33374)
*Vincenzo Gallegos,Evan Laljit,Julianne Stratton,Gabe Perez-Giz,Alan Kaptanoglu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The authors extend stochastic coil optimization for stellarators to account for manufacturing errors, assembly errors (centroid translation and angular offset), and coil current errors, and show the method regularizes the optimization landscape and improves robustness across three stellarators, with the robustness metric Q improving 4.50%–729%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stellarator coils are subject to real-world fabrication, assembly, and current errors that degrade performance; standard deterministic coil optimization does not account for these perturbations, so there is a need for methods that produce coil sets robust to such errors.

Method: The authors extend stochastic coil optimization to incorporate manufacturing errors, assembly errors represented by centroid translations and angular offsets, and coil current errors. They compare different perturbation methods and combine them with the Augmented Lagrangian algorithm, then perform hyperparameter scans (training perturbation size, out-of-sample perturbation size) across three stellarators.

Result: Stochastic methods regularize the coil optimization landscape and improve robustness in every case, with improvement magnitude varying substantially across plasmas. The robustness metric Q improves by 4.50% to 729%. Hyperparameter scans reveal coupled and de-coupled training tolerance gains ranging from 0.09 mm to 1.35 mm depending strongly on the specific stellarizer.

Conclusion: Stochastic coil optimization is an effective general strategy for improving stellarator coil robustness to realistic errors, though the gains depend strongly on the specific configuration, emphasizing the importance of configuration-specific hyperparameter tuning.

Abstract: We extend stochastic coil optimization for stellarators and analyze the effect of optimizing for coil manufacturing errors, assembly errors represented by centroid translations and angular offsets, and errors in the coil currents. We further analyze the impact of different perturbation methods and use them in conjunction with the Augmented Lagrangian algorithm. For three stellarators, we show that stochastic methods regularize the coil optimization landscape and improve robustness in every case, with the magnitude of the improvement varying substantially across different plasmas. Our metric for coil robustness, $Q$, is improved by $4.50\%$ to $729\%$ across different configurations. Lastly, we perform numerous hyperparameter scans, including e.g. scanning the training perturbation size and the out-of-sample (OOS) evaluation perturbation size and find coupled and de-coupled training tolerance gains ranging from $0.09$ mm to $1.35$ mm depending strongly on the particular stellarator.

</details>


### [123] [A Novel Linked-Mirror Device for Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33529)
*Haorong Qiu,Xiang Li,Guodong Yu,Zhexuan Zheng,Caoxiang Zhu,Guoyong Fu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a linked-mirror device (LMD) with a magnetic well using near-axis expansion, connecting two parallel mirror sections with 3D stellarator-like fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single magnetic mirrors suffer from end-loss of particles. The goal is to eliminate this end-loss problem while maintaining plasma stability against interchange modes.

Method: The authors design an LMD with two parallel straight mirror sections joined at both ends by three-dimensional stellarator-like magnetic fields, constructed via the near-axis expansion (NAE) method. Stability is addressed by engineering a magnetic well. Finite-beta equilibria are verified with numerical solutions of the 3D equilibrium code GVEC.

Result: An LMD configuration with a magnetic well is obtained, providing stability against interchange modes, and finite-beta equilibria of the LMD are demonstrated via GVEC.

Conclusion: The LMD is potentially a novel magnetic confinement solution for fusion reactors, overcoming single-mirror end losses while maintaining interchange stability.

Abstract: We design a novel linked-mirror device (LMD) with a magnetic well using the near-axis expansion (NAE) method. The design consists of two parallel straight mirror sections connected at both ends by three-dimensional stellarator-like magnetic fields , thereby eliminating the end-loss problem of a single magnetic mirror. By designing appropriate three-dimensional magnetic fields, we obtain an LMD configuration with a magnetic well, ensuring stability against interchange modes. The existence of finite-\b{eta} equilibria of the LMD is demonstrated by numerical solutions of the 3D equilibrium code GVEC. This design is potentially a novel solution to magnetic confinement for fusion reactors.

</details>


### [124] [Accelerating high-fidelity gyrokinetic fusion simulations with iGENE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33561)
*Gabriele Merlo,Victor Artigues,Carl-Martin Pfeiler,Frank Jenko*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: iGENE is a fully JAX-rewritten, end-to-end differentiable gyrokinetic code that matches GENE physics, fixes earlier derivative errors, and achieves strong GPU scaling plus multiple >1.8–4× performance speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a differentiable gyrokinetic simulation tool for gradient-based optimization and uncertainty quantification, addressing accuracy and performance limitations of the earlier TensorFlow version of iGENE.

Method: Rewrote iGENE in JAX with a multispecies, collisional, fully electromagnetic nonlinear gyrokinetic model while preserving end-to-end differentiability. Verified against GENE for linear ITG, TEM, KBM, and nonlinear turbulent fluxes; tested forward-mode derivatives vs finite differences; used native JAX collectives and explored three speedup routes: bfloat16 custom CUDA FFT kernels, multi-rate sub-cycling of parallel dynamics, and a Hermite-Laguerre velocity-space representation.

Result: Agreement with GENE for linear and nonlinear cases; forward-mode growth-rate derivatives match finite differences across five parameters, resolving the prior 30–50% discrepancy in ŝ and ε; strong-scaling from 2 to 32 H100 GPUs with 86% parallel efficiency; bfloat16 CUDA FFT up to 3.97× faster on transforms and 2.63× faster per time step than single-precision GENE on one H100; multi-rate sub-cycling gives 1.89× further speedup with kinetic electrons.

Conclusion: iGENE delivers a verified, differentiable, well-scaling gyrokinetic code, and combining mixed-precision FFTs, multi-rate sub-cycling, and Hermite-Laguerre representations provides a promising path to shorter time-to-solution.

Abstract: We present the latest version of iGENE, a differentiable implementation of the local (flux-tube) gyrokinetic code GENE, now fully rewritten in JAX. iGENE features a multispecies, collisional, fully electromagnetic nonlinear gyrokinetic model while preserving end-to-end differentiability. The code is verified against GENE for linear ITG, TEM and KBM physics and for nonlinear turbulent fluxes with adiabatic and kinetic electrons. Forward-mode derivatives of the linear growth rate agree with finite differences for all five parameters tested, resolving the 30--50\% discrepancy in $\hat{s}$ and $ε$ reported for the earlier TensorFlow version. All collectives are native JAX operations, and the code strong-scales from 2 to 32 H100 GPUs with 86\% parallel efficiency. We then assess three routes to a shorter time to solution. Custom CUDA FFT kernels operating in bfloat16 are up to $3.97\times$ faster than fp32 cuFFT on the transforms, and on a single H100 iGENE is $2.63\times$ faster per time step than single-precision GENE. Multi-rate sub-cycling of the parallel dynamics yields a further $1.89\times$ speedup with kinetic electrons. Finally, a Hermite-Laguerre velocity-space representation is implemented, offering tunable fidelity.

</details>


### [125] [Triple Decomposition of Field Gradient Tensors in Space Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33563)
*Virgilio Quattrociocchi,Giuseppe Consolini,Maria Federica Marcucci*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The authors extend a real-Schur-based Triple Decomposition to MHD turbulence, separating normal strain, pure shear, and true rigid-body rotation in velocity and magnetic gradient tensors. Applied to MMS solar wind and magnetosheath data, it shows solar wind is strain-dominated while magnetosheath is shear-dominated, with cross-terms suppressed (≤7%) versus neutral fluids (~13%) due to magnetic anisotropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical double decomposition (symmetric strain + antisymmetric rotation) conflates rigid-body rotation with planar sliding shear, obscuring the true local topology of MHD turbulence, which matters for understanding dissipation, intermittency, and coherent structures.

Method: Extend the real-Schur-based Triple Decomposition to MHD flows, rigorously isolating irrotational normal strain (N, M), residual pure shear (H, K), and true rigid-body rotation (R, B) for velocity (A_ij) and magnetic (Z_ij) gradient tensors. Apply to high-resolution NASA-MMS multi-spacecraft observations in the solar wind and Earth's magnetosheath.

Result: Solar wind is predominantly strain-dominated (spherical radial expansion); magnetosheath turbulence is overwhelmingly shear-dominated (boundary interactions). Cross-term contributions (2RH, 2KB) are suppressed to ≤7% in both regimes, versus ~13% in neutral fluid turbulence, attributed to background-field-induced anisotropy (k_perp >> k_parallel) confining shearing motions perpendicular to primary rotation axes.

Conclusion: By cleanly separating genuine vortex cores from current sheets and shear layers, the Triple Decomposition offers an objective framework for characterizing plasma turbulence and analyzing data from future multi-spacecraft missions.

Abstract: Characterizing local topology in magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) turbulence is fundamental to understanding energy dissipation, intermittency, and coherent structures in space plasmas. Classical double decomposition of velocity ($A_{ij}$) and magnetic ($Z_{ij}$) field gradient tensors into symmetric strain and anti-symmetric rotation components conflates rigid-body rotation with planar sliding shear. Here, we extend the real-Schur-based Triple Decomposition framework to MHD flows, rigorously isolating irrotational normal strain ($N, M$), residual pure shear ($H, K$), and true rigid-body rotation ($R, B$). Applying this method to high-resolution NASA-MMS multi-spacecraft observations in the solar wind and Earth's magnetosheath reveals distinct topological signatures. Solar wind dynamics are predominantly strain-dominated due to spherical radial expansion, whereas magnetosheath turbulence is overwhelmingly shear-dominated, driven by boundary interactions. Crucially, cross-term contributions ($2RH$ and $2KB$) are suppressed to $\le 7\%$ across both plasma regimes, contrasting sharply with neutral fluid turbulence ($\sim 13\%$). This suppression is driven by background magnetic-field-induced anisotropy ($k_\perp \gg k_\parallel$), which confines shearing motions perpendicular to the primary rotation axes. By cleanly separating genuine vortex cores from current sheets and shear layers, the Triple Decomposition provides an objective framework for characterizing plasma turbulence and analyzing data from future multi-spacecraft missions.

</details>


### [126] [Wave-branch classification of ideal MHD discontinuities in Q-variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33787)
*Anna Krupka,Tom Van Doorsselaere*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: This paper applies the Q-variable formalism, which combines velocity and magnetic field into branches aligned with a chosen MHD wave, to the full catalogue of ideal-MHD discontinuities, showing how each classical discontinuity type is characterized by constraints on the two discontinuity-frame branches Q'±.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Q-variable formalism has recently been shown to reproduce the ideal-MHD Rankine-Hugoniot conditions exactly, and it offers a characteristic-aligned (wave-based) interpretation of plasma variables. However, it had not yet been systematically applied to classify all standard ideal-MHD discontinuities. Such a classification is needed as a background interface structure for later studies of reflection, transmission, coupling, and mode conversion.

Method: The authors adopt the Q-variable reformulation of the ideal-MHD jump conditions, in which velocity and magnetic field are combined into branches associated with a selected MHD wave, parameterized by α (α choice yielding fast, Alfvén, slow characteristics in homogeneous ideal MHD, or a global mode dispersion relation in structured plasmas). They examine the two discontinuity-frame branches Q'±, their sums, and their α-normalised differences across contact, tangential, and rotational discontinuities and across the principal shock families.

Result: Material interfaces are distinguished by their normal branch content; rotational discontinuities by an Alfvénic tangential relation; and fast, slow, and intermediate shocks by the magnitude or orientation of the α-normalised tangential branch difference. Perpendicular, parallel, switch-on, and switch-off shocks emerge as geometrical or limiting cases of this scheme.

Conclusion: The classical discontinuity conditions can be recast in a wave-aligned form, and via the characteristic interpretation of Q0±, the classification shows how ideal-MHD discontinuities constrain changes in characteristic propagation direction and speed across an interface. This provides the background interface structure needed for subsequent studies of reflection, transmission, coupling, and mode conversion.

Abstract: The $Q$-variable formalism combines velocity and magnetic field into branches aligned with a selected MHD wave. For homogeneous ideal MHD, appropriate choices of the branch parameter $α$ correspond to the fast, Alfvén, and slow characteristic families, with the background variables $\boldsymbol{Q}_0^{\pm}$ representing the propagation direction and speed of the corresponding characteristics. In structured plasmas, $α$ may instead be selected from the dispersion relation of a global mode such as a kink wave. Recent work reformulated the ideal-MHD Rankine-Hugoniot conditions in $Q$-variables and established their exact equivalence with the classical conservative jump conditions. Here, we apply this formulation to the standard ideal-MHD discontinuity catalogue. We determine how the two discontinuity-frame branches $\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime+}$ and $\boldsymbol{Q}^{\prime-}$, their sums, and their $α$-normalised differences are constrained across contact, tangential, and rotational discontinuities and across the principal shock families. Material interfaces are distinguished by their normal branch content, rotational discontinuities by an Alfvénic tangential relation, and fast, slow, and intermediate shocks by the magnitude or orientation of the $α$-normalised tangential branch difference. Perpendicular, parallel, switch-on, and switch-off shocks appear as geometrical or limiting cases. The resulting classification expresses the classical discontinuity conditions in a wave-aligned form and, through the characteristic interpretation of $\boldsymbol{Q}_0^{\pm}$, shows how ideal-MHD discontinuities constrain changes in characteristic propagation direction and speed across the interface. It therefore establishes the background interface structure required for subsequent studies of reflection, transmission, coupling, and mode conversion.

</details>


### [127] [Non-Ideal Relativistic Plasma Mirrors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33796)
*Zahra M. Chamazkoti,Luís O. Silva*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The paper derives the reflection coefficient of a laser pulse interacting with relativistic plasma mirrors in underdense plasmas, showing that reflectivity depends strongly on the mirror's density profile, and confirms via particle-in-cell simulations that the density spike at the rear of the plasma bubble is approximately parabolic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relativistic plasma mirrors formed behind intense laser pulses in underdense plasmas are promising for efficient attosecond pulse generation and enhanced focusing toward ultra-high intensity fields, but the reflectivity's dependence on the mirror's density profile has not been well characterized analytically.

Method: The authors analytically derive the reflection coefficient for a laser interacting with relativistic mirrors, considering flat-top, parabolic, and Dirac-delta density profiles. They compare their analytical model against fully kinetic multi-dimensional particle-in-cell (PIC) simulations.

Result: Analytical expressions are obtained for flat-top and parabolic density mirrors and in the Dirac-delta limit, demonstrating strong dependence of reflectivity on the density profile. PIC simulations confirm that the density spike at the rear of the plasma bubble has an approximately parabolic profile, consistent with the model.

Conclusion: A laser wakefield setup can be optimized to maximize the reflection coefficient under specific laser driver conditions, governed by the balance between the moving plasma mirror's proper density, thickness, and relativistic momentum.

Abstract: Relativistic plasma mirrors formed in the wake of intense laser pulses propagating in underdense plasmas can provide an efficient mechanism for generating attosecond pulses, and for enhanced focusing towards ultra-high intensity fields. We derive the reflection coefficient of a laser pulse interacting with relativistic mirrors, assuming different density profiles for the mirror. Analytical expressions are obtained for flat-top and parabolic density mirrors, and in the Dirac-delta limit, demonstrating the strong dependence of the reflectivity on the plasma mirror density profile. Fully kinetic multi-dimensional particle-in-cell simulations and our analytical model confirm that the density spike at the rear of the plasma bubble exhibits an approximately parabolic profile. Our results show that a laser wakefield setup can be optimized to maximize the reflection coefficient under specific laser driver conditions, determined by the balance between the moving plasma mirror proper density, thickness, and relativistic momentum carried by the mirror.

</details>


### [128] [Generalization of the Rosenbluth-Putvinski avalanche growth rate to the drift kinetic regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33917)
*Christopher J. McDevitt,Jonathan S. Arnaud*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A tilted magnetic-well effect in tokamaks causes collisionless detrapping that can halve runaway electron generation rates; a single dimensionless parameter captures the transition and yields an extended avalanche growth-rate formula.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong inductive electric fields during tokamak disruptions tilt the magnetic well, and existing bounce-averaged RE avalanche theory (Rosenbluth-Putvinski) does not account for collisionless detrapping that alters generation rates.

Method: Identify and derive a collisionless detrapping mechanism from the tilted magnetic well, and reduce its effect to a single dimensionless parameter that characterizes distinct RE generation regimes; fit avalanche growth-rate results with a two-parameter function.

Result: Modifications to the avalanche growth rate across a broad range of plasma parameters collapse onto a one-dimensional curve when expressed via the composite parameter, and are accurately fit by a two-parameter function.

Conclusion: An analytical formula is obtained that extends the classic Rosenbluth-Putvinski avalanche growth rate beyond the bounce-averaged limit into regimes where collisionless detrapping strongly modifies RE generation.

Abstract: A new collisionless detrapping mechanism is shown to modify the rate of runaway electron (RE) generation by as much as a factor of two for strong inductive electric fields. This detrapping mechanism results from the magnetic well inherent to tokamak geometry being tilted by the strong inductive electric fields present during tokamak disruptions. The impact of this collisionless detrapping mechanism is found to be described by a single dimensionless parameter, whose identification enables the transition between distinct regimes of RE generation to be characterized. Using this composite parameter, it is shown that modifications to the avalanche growth rate by tokamak geometry across a broad range of plasma parameters collapse onto a one-dimensional curve that can be accurately fit with a two parameter function. The resulting analytical formula extends the classic Rosenbluth-Putvinski avalanche growth rate expression beyond the bounce-averaged limit into RE regimes where collisionless detrapping strongly modifies RE generation rates.

</details>


### [129] [Identification of rippling interface between laser-ablated plasma and ambient uniform plasma at rest](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34057)
*Ryo Yamazaki,S. Tomita,T. Takezaki,T. Morita,S. J. Tanaka,Y. Ohira,T. Umeda,K. Aihara,S. Kambayashi,R. Kawamura,K. Obayashi,M. Murakoshi,Y. Sato,R. Shiina,A. Takata,K. Matsui,S. Ueno,Y. Kidokoro,S. Yakura,Y. Okada,S. Suzuki,H. Kondo,N. Shimoda,J. Shiota,Y. Sudo,D. Suzuki,K. Sugiyama,A. Morita,H. Kawakatsu,A. Kashima,Y. Ishimura,K. Umehara,S. Egashira,H. Ishihara,O. Kuramoto,Y. Matsumoto,K. Maeda,Y. Suzuki,M. Hanano,Y. Toyoda,K. Kuramoto,F. Nikaido,K. Iida,Y. Naito,Y. Sato,K. Takahashi,K. Nakahara,Y. Maenosono,Y. Muramoto,Y. Kanesada,K. Koba,T. Ogawa,R. Shiiba,T. Oguchi,M. Edamoto,M. Iwamoto,M. Hoshino,T. Inoue,S. Isayama,A. T. Ishii,Y. Kuramitsu,S. Matsukiyo,N. Ohnishi,K. Sakai,T. Sano,K. Tomita,Y. Sakawa*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Laser-produced Al piston plasma interacting with a magnetized ambient N plasma reproduces early-stage quasi-perpendicular supercritical collisionless shock formation; the rippling emission marks the Al-N interface with electron-kinetic-scale width, not Kelvin-Helmholtz-driven.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the early formation of quasi-perpendicular supercritical magnetized collisionless shocks is difficult in space and requires controllable laboratory platforms; a key issue is disentangling shock physics from the contaminating piston material.

Method: Laboratory experiments: laser-driven expanding Al piston plasma in uniform 1 Torr nitrogen gas under an external magnetic field of controlled polarity. Diagnostics of asymmetric self-emission and its polarity-dependent global morphology reversal, supported by 2D ideal-MHD simulations.

Result: The emission morphology reverses with magnetic-field polarity and is reproduced by 2D ideal-MHD simulations. Rippling is not driven by the Kelvin-Helmholtz instability but by vortex formation at the rounded leading edge of the piston. The bright rippling emission traces the Al-N interface, whose intrinsic width matches an electron kinetic scale, with the upstream side dominated by N plasma.

Conclusion: Identifying the emitting structure as the Al-N interface with N-dominated upstream allows supercritical collisionless shock formation in magnetized N plasma to be studied with minimal Al contamination, providing a clean laboratory platform for early-stage shock formation.

Abstract: We present laboratory experiments on the early stage of the formation process of quasi-perpendicular supercritical magnetized collisionless shocks using laser-produced plasmas in an ambient uniform nitrogen (N) gas with 1~Torr under the external magnetic field with controlled polarity. An expanding aluminum (Al) piston plasma arising from laser irradiation interacts with a magnetized ambient N plasma, producing a bright, asymmetric self-emission whose global morphology reverses with the magnetic-field polarity. Two-dimensional ideal-magnetohydrodynamics simulations reproduce the main features of the observed structures and indicate that the ripple formation is unlikely to be driven by the Kelvin--Helmholtz instability, but is instead associated with vortex formation at the rounded leading edge of the piston plasma. We show that the bright, rippling emission delineates the Al-N interface, whose intrinsic width is consistent with an electron kinetic scale, and that its upstream side is predominantly composed of N plasma. This identification allows the formation of a supercritical collisionless shock in the magnetized N plasma to be discussed with minimal Al contamination.

</details>


### [130] [Experimental Evidence for Reflected Ions and Ion Temperature Anisotropy in a Forming Supercritical Magnetized Collisionless Shock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34058)
*Ryo Yamazaki,T. Takezaki,T. Morita,S. Tomita,S. J. Tanaka,Y. Ohira,T. Umeda,K. Aihara,S. Kambayashi,R. Kawamura,K. Obayashi,M. Murakoshi,Y. Sato,R. Shiina,A. Takata,K. Matsui,S. Ueno,Y. Kidokoro,S. Yakura,Y. Okada,S. Suzuki,H. Kondo,N. Shimoda,J. Shiota,Y. Sudo,D. Suzuki,K. Sugiyama,A. Morita,H. Kawakatsu,A. Kashima,Y. Ishimura,K. Umehara,S. Egashira,H. Ishihara,O. Kuramoto,Y. Matsumoto,K. Maeda,Y. Suzuki,M. Hanano,Y. Toyoda,K. Kuramoto,F. Nikaido,K. Iida,Y. Naito,Y. Sato,K. Takahashi,K. Nakahara,Y. Maenosono,Y. Muramoto,Y. Kanesada,K. Koba,T. Ogawa,R. Shiiba,T. Oguchi,M. Edamoto,M. Iwamoto,M. Hoshino,T. Inoue,S. Isayama,A. T. Ishii,Y. Kuramitsu,S. Matsukiyo,N. Ohnishi,K. Sakai,T. Sano,K. Tomita,Y. Sakawa*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Laser experiments on a forming quasi-perpendicular collisionless shock show upstream emission, ion heating, and temperature anisotropy ahead of a ramp-like region, consistent with reflected-ion-related heating during early shock formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supercritical magnetized collisionless shocks need ion reflection for dissipation, but the role of reflected ions during shock formation is unclear.

Method: High-power lasers with self-emission imaging and collective Thomson scattering to probe a forming quasi-perpendicular shock.

Result: An upstream emission tail consistent with a foot-like structure; ion heating and temperature anisotropy extend upstream of a ramp-like region, indicating reflected-ion-related heating.

Conclusion: Evidence of reflected-ion heating in early shock formation, though incident and reflected populations are unresolved; future ion-distribution and wave measurements could clarify dissipation and energization.

Abstract: Supercritical magnetized collisionless shocks require ion reflection for dissipation, yet its role during formation remains unclear. Using high-power lasers, self-emission imaging, and collective Thomson scattering, we investigate a forming quasi-perpendicular shock. An upstream emission tail is consistent with a foot-like structure, while ion heating and temperature anisotropy extend upstream of a ramp-like region. These observations provide evidence of reflected-ion-related heating during early shock formation, although the incident and reflected ion populations are not separately resolved. Future measurements of ion distributions and plasma waves could clarify how this heating mediates shock dissipation and particle energization.

</details>


### [131] [Laser wakefield acceleration and x-ray emission in plasma produced by the electrical explosion of an aluminum wire](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34859)
*Heng Wang,Liang Sheng,Jun Liu,Yang Li,Yanfei Li,Xinjian Tan,Dongwei He*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A petawatt femtosecond laser interacting with an exploding-wire aluminum plasma is shown via PIC simulations to drive wakefield-like acceleration to near-GeV electrons plus directional broadband x-rays, supporting exploded metallic plasma as a viable target for laser wakefield acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underdense plasmas for relativistic laser-plasma interactions are almost always made from gases; low-density metallic plasmas are nearly unexplored, so it is unknown whether metallic targets can serve as practical wakefield-acceleration platforms.

Method: Particle-in-cell simulations of a femtosecond petawatt laser in expanded aluminum plasma, using an experimentally motivated exploding-wire density profile. 3D runs assess propagation, wakefields, electron energies, and x-ray emission with charge-state-resolved diagnostics; complementary 2D runs quantify energy gain and test sensitivity to initial ionization and density conditions.

Result: The laser propagates through the low-density metal plasma, drives a pronounced wake-like structure, accelerates electrons to near-GeV energies, and emits directional broadband x-rays. Electrons from higher Al ionization stages are disproportionately represented among energetic electrons. Net electron energy gain is dominated by longitudinal electric-field work, and relativistic electron generation persists under varied ionization/density assumptions, though the yield is quantitatively sensitive to plasma state.

Conclusion: Electrically exploded aluminum plasma is a feasible target platform for laser wakefield acceleration and associated x-ray generation, extending underdense plasma sources beyond gases to metals.

Abstract: Underdense plasmas used in relativistic laser-plasma interactions are conventionally produced from gaseous targets, whereas low-density metallic plasmas remain comparatively unexplored. Here, we investigate the interaction of a femtosecond petawatt laser with expanded aluminum plasma using particle-in-cell simulations based on an experimentally motivated exploding-wire density profile. Three-dimensional simulations show that the laser propagates through the low-density metallic plasma, drives a pronounced wake-like structure, accelerates electrons to near-GeV energies, and produces directional broadband x-ray emission. Charge-state-resolved diagnostics further show that electrons associated with higher aluminum ionization stages are disproportionately represented in the energetic population. Complementary two-dimensional simulations indicate that the net electron energy gain is dominated by longitudinal electric-field work and show that relativistic electron generation persists under variations of the assumed initial ionization and density conditions, although the yield remains quantitatively sensitive to the plasma state. These results demonstrate the feasibility of electrically exploded aluminum plasma as a target platform for laser wakefield acceleration and associated x-ray generation.

</details>


### [132] [Toward Integrated Microwave Diagnostics for Future Fusion Devices: Design of a Compact Waveguide Diplexer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34881)
*Xiaoliang Li,Valerian Hall-Chen,Umesh Kumar,Eddie Chua,Tengfei Tang,Andy Yeoh,Khadija Rahman,Xinhang Xu,Yilun Zhu*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A compact waveguide diplexer combining a T-junction with two iris-coupled 6th-order Chebyshev bandpass filters lets reflectometry and ECE diagnostics share one transmission line, achieving <1 dB insertion loss and >40 dB isolation in the 65-70 GHz and 85-90 GHz bands for J-TEXT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future burning-plasma reactors have severely constrained diagnostic access due to breeding blankets, neutron shielding, and compact geometries, requiring reduced blanket opening areas while preserving diagnostic capability. Front-end integration of multiple microwave diagnostics into a shared transmission line addresses this.

Method: The authors design a waveguide diplexer that combines a waveguide T-junction with two iris-coupled sixth-order Chebyshev bandpass filters, enabling microwave reflectometry and ECE to share a common line in separate frequency bands. As an initial study, it is designed for the 65-70 GHz reflectometry and 85-90 GHz ECE systems on J-TEXT, validated by full-wave electromagnetic simulations.

Result: Full-wave simulations show insertion losses below 1 dB and channel isolation exceeding 40 dB across both operating bands.

Conclusion: The proposed methodology offers a compact, scalable approach to microwave diagnostic integration, providing a practical basis for multi-diagnostic front-end systems in future fusion devices.

Abstract: Microwave diagnostics are essential for plasma control and physics studies in magnetic confinement fusion, providing measurements of electron density, electron temperature, and energetic-particle behavior. However, diagnostic access in future burning-plasma reactors will be severely constrained by breeding blankets, neutron shielding, and compact reactor geometries, requiring reduced blanket opening areas while maintaining diagnostic capability. This work presents a compact waveguide diplexer for front-end integration of multiple microwave diagnostics. The proposed diplexer combines a waveguide T-junction with two iris-coupled sixth-order Chebyshev bandpass filters, enabling microwave reflectometry and electron cyclotron emission (ECE) diagnostics to share a common transmission line while operating in separate frequency bands. As an initial study, the diplexer is designed for the 65-70 GHz reflectometry and 85-90 GHz ECE systems on the J-TEXT tokamak. Full-wave electromagnetic simulations demonstrate insertion losses below 1 dB and channel isolation exceeding 40 dB across both operating bands. The proposed methodology provides a compact and scalable approach to microwave diagnostic integration and establishes a practical basis for multi-diagnostic front-end systems in future fusion devices.

</details>


### [133] [Non-Maxwellianity in Temperature Relaxation between Plasma Species](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35009)
*Yao Guo,Dong Wu,Jie Zhang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Interspecies collisions in plasmas generate a universal three-lobe distortion that slows temperature relaxation well beyond Landau-Spitzer predictions, up to 47% longer for ICF-relevant DT plasmas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Landau-Spitzer theory of temperature relaxation assumes Maxwellian distributions held throughout, ignoring distortions that collisions themselves produce. The authors test whether this assumption is valid for two Maxwellian species relaxing in homogeneous isotropic plasmas.

Method: Analytical derivation showing that interspecies collisions generate a universal three-lobe initial distortion, combined with full Fokker-Planck simulations that capture deformation competing with self-thermalization.

Result: The distortions initially reduce energy exchange over a broad parameter range. Full Fokker-Planck simulations show substantial relaxation delays. For post-shock DT plasmas with T_T/T_D = 1.5, relaxation time exceeds the LS prediction by 47%; distortions lower the apparent DD/DT temperature ratio and produce positive DD and negative DT Gamow-shift residuals.

Conclusion: LS theory underestimates relaxation times and introduces diagnostic residuals when non-Maxwellian distortions matter. The findings also motivate examining non-Maxwellianity in fuel heating by fusion alpha particles.

Abstract: Temperature relaxation between plasma species is usually described by Landau--Spitzer (LS) theory, which assumes Maxwellian distributions throughout. We show analytically that, for two Maxwellian species in homogeneous isotropic plasmas, the interspecies collisions driving relaxation generate a universal three-lobe initial distortion and that these distortions initially reduce energy exchange over a broad parameter range. Full Fokker--Planck simulations reveal substantial relaxation delays when interspecies deformation competes with self-thermalization. For post-shock deuterium--tritium plasmas relevant to inertial confinement fusion, with $T_{\mathrm{T}}/T_{\mathrm{D}}=1.5$, the relaxation time exceeds the LS prediction by 47%. The accompanying distortions lower the apparent DD/DT temperature ratio and produce positive DD and negative DT Gamow-shift residuals relative to Maxwellian predictions. These results also motivate examining non-Maxwellianity in fuel heating by fusion $α$ particles.

</details>


### [134] [Bright Compton source based on the interaction of an ultraintense laser pulse with a relativistically transparent foil and a plasma mirror](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35039)
*Daria A. Chekmareva,Artem V. Korzhimanov*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: A compact x-ray source using one laser pulse that first accelerates electrons through a relativistically transparent thin overdense foil and then reflects off a plasma mirror to collide head-on, yielding bright 10–250 keV x-rays.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a bright, efficient compact x-ray source and improve on the spectral yield per joule of existing gas-jet, betatron, and near-critical-density schemes.

Method: 2D kinetic (particle-in-cell) simulations of a single laser pulse at ~10^21 W/cm^2 passing through a relativistically transparent thin overdense foil, then reflecting from a plasma mirror to undergo head-on collision with the accelerated electron bunch.

Result: With optimal foil parameters, electrons reach ~100 MeV, and the source delivers up to 10^7 photons per 0.1% bandwidth per joule in the 10–250 keV range—two orders of magnitude above a similar gas-jet scheme and ~one order above the best gas-target betatron sources, comparable to the best near-critical-density experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed one-laser, foil-plus-plasma-mirror scheme offers a highly efficient bright x-ray source competitive with or surpassing leading compact sources.

Abstract: We propose and numerically study an x-ray source in which a single laser pulse first accelerates electrons while passing through a relativistically transparent thin overdense foil and then, after reflection from a plasma mirror, collides head-on with them. Two-dimensional kinetic simulations show that for a laser pulse with an intensity of the order of $10^{21}$~W/cm$^2$ and optimal foil parameters, electrons are accelerated to energies of the order of 100~MeV, and the source can deliver up to $10^{7}$ photons per 0.1\% bandwidth per joule of laser energy in the 10--250~keV range, two orders of magnitude more than a similar gas-jet scheme. This also exceeds the spectral yield per joule of the best gas-target betatron sources by about an order of magnitude and is comparable to the best experimental results obtained with near-critical-density targets.

</details>


### [135] [Explosive instability, quiet plasma: phase-space cascades, coherent vortices, and transport suppression in a dipole pair plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35048)
*Daniel Kennedy,Gabriel G Plunk*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: In a magnetized dipole pair plasma, turbulence drives a dual cascade in four-dimensional phase space, transferring electrostatic energy into coherent flows while moving entropy to small scales, with zonal bands, a transport floor, and breathing vortices appearing depending on instability regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper seeks to understand how turbulence in a magnetised dipole pair plasma redistributes electrostatic energy and entropy, and how transport is regulated by zonal structures and instability thresholds.

Method: The authors analyze a dual cascade in four-dimensional phase space, where advection by the fluctuating electric field nonlinearly phase-mixes the transit-averaged particle motion, and they examine regimes governed by a critical pressure gradient.

Result: Below a critical pressure gradient, zonal bands suppress the kinetic mode leaving a transport floor; when the fluid mode is unstable, an explosive burst collapses into a zonal condensate on which two vortices "breathe" via reversible energy exchange without net transport.

Conclusion: Turbulence in this system couples energy and entropy transport through phase-space cascades, and zonal structures regulate transport, producing either suppressed transport or non-transporting coherent vortex dynamics.

Abstract: Turbulence in a magnetised dipole pair plasma can transfer electrostatic energy into coherent flows while carrying entropy to small scales. This occurs via a dual cascade in a four-dimensional phase space, whereby advection by the fluctuating electric field nonlinearly phase-mixes the transit-averaged particle motion. Below a critical pressure gradient, zonal bands suppress the kinetic mode, leaving a transport floor. When the fluid mode is unstable, an explosive burst collapses into a zonal condensate on which two vortices "breathe" by reversible energy exchange, without causing net transport.

</details>


### [136] [Drift wave turbulence and transport analysis of hot ion L-mode plasma with fishbone instability on the HL-3 tokamak](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35072)
*Y. K. Shu,W. Chen,Z. J. Li,S. Xu,Y. F. Liu,B. L. Hao,Z. Wang,D. L. Yu,S. S. Yang,W. Y. Zhai,X. L. Zhu,P. W. Shi,X. L. Xu,T. Wu,Z. B. Shi,HL-3 Team*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: In HL-3 hot-ion-mode plasmas, a single m=1,n=1 fishbone mode excited by a steep fast-ion pressure gradient links macroscopic MHD activity to both toroidal rotation collapse (sawtooth-triggered drop from >100 to 60 krad/s) and microturbulence stability, redistributing resonant fast ions that otherwise stabilize ITG/TEM modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a cross-scale coupling mechanism by which a macroscopic MHD mode (fishbone) simultaneously governs momentum balance (toroidal rotation) and drift-wave microturbulence in hot-ion-mode plasmas, a regime where high T_i/T_e and fast ions strongly affect transport but the MHD–turbulence link is unclear.

Method: Combined multi-diagnostic measurements, OMFIT modeling, and TGLF simulations for HL-3 hot ion mode (T_i ≈ 3T_e, T_i(0) ≈ 9.8 keV) with 2.5 MW co-injected NBI. Analysis of fast-ion pressure gradient, fishbone excitation, torque decoupling, sawtooth crash effects on rotation, and TGLF scans of the effective ITG threshold (temperature-ratio, fast-ion dilution, E×B shear terms).

Result: Co-NBI drives a steep fast-ion pressure gradient exciting an m=1,n=1 fishbone; the mode redistributes resonant fast ions outward and decouples NBI torque, while the triggered sawtooth crash collapses rotation from >100 to 60 krad/s. The same fast ions both drive the fishbone and stabilize ITG/TEM; their redistribution lowers the instability threshold and erodes the internal transport barrier. High T_i/T_e suppresses all transport fluxes, synergistically with the fast-ion population.

Conclusion: A single macroscopic MHD mode governs both momentum balance and microturbulence stability, demonstrating fishbone-mediated cross-scale coupling in hot-ion-mode plasmas.

Abstract: We establish the cross-scale coupling by which a fishbone mode governs both toroidal rotation and drift-wave turbulence in the HL-3 hot ion mode ($T_i \approx 3T_e$, $T_i(0)\approx9.8$\,keV). Combining multi-diagnostic measurements, OMFIT modeling, and TGLF simulations, we show that under 2.5\,MW co-injected NBI a steep fast-ion pressure gradient excites an $m=1,n=1$ fishbone mode. The mode redistributes resonant fast ions outward and decouples the NBI torque, and the sawtooth crash it triggers collapses the rotation from above $100$ to $60$\,krad/s. The same fast ions drive the fishbone and stabilize ion scale ITG/TEM turbulence. Their redistribution lowers the instability threshold and erodes the internal transport barrier. The effective ITG threshold combines a dominant temperature-ratio term, a fast-ion dilution factor, and an $E\times B$ shear term. TGLF scans show the high $T_i/T_e$ ratio suppresses all transport fluxes, synergistically with the fast-ion population. A single macroscopic MHD mode governs both momentum balance and microturbulence stability.

</details>


### [137] [Kinetic modifications to parallel transport in a low-recycling scrape-off layer: A one dimensional gyrokinetic study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35455)
*A. Maan,E. Perez,M. Francisquez,M. Dorf,D. P. Boyle,R. Majeski,A. Hakim,G. W. Hammett*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Gyrokinetic simulations of LTX-β scrape-off-layer conditions show that low-recycling liquid lithium edges become so collisionless that kinetic electrons, non-Maxwellian ions, mirror trapping, and ambipolar potential fundamentally alter parallel transport versus fluid expectations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Liquid lithium boundaries reduce recycling and improve confinement, but the resulting low-density, high-temperature edge can be collisionless enough that standard fluid models fail. Simple analytic estimates from SOL density decay lengths are insufficient and need proper kinetic treatment, especially to understand how kinetic effects shape parallel transport and confinement.

Method: The authors run Gkeyll continuum gyrokinetic simulations (1 configuration-space dimension, 2 velocity-space dimensions) of low- and higher-recycling LTX-β SOL conditions along a single open field line, self-consistently evolving ion and electron distribution functions, electrostatic potential, collisions, sources, and sheath losses constrained by experimental midplane measurements. They also perform collisionality scans.

Result: The hotter low-recycling case develops larger parallel potential variation and a density response not captured by an adiabatic Boltzmann-electron closure, showing the importance of kinetic electrons and ambipolar potential in reducing electron end losses. As ion neoclassical collisionality drops below unity, the ion distribution develops pronounced non-Maxwellian features. SOL particle and energy confinement times increase at low collisionality, particularly for low recycling, as ions become mirror-trapped and electron losses are throttled by the ambipolar potential.

Conclusion: Low-recycling lithium boundaries in LTX-β access a regime where mirror trapping and electrostatic confinement fundamentally change parallel residence times relative to conventional fluid predictions, an effect expected to be more pronounced in larger devices.

Abstract: A liquid lithium boundary offers a route to improved energy confinement via reduced recycling, but the resulting low-density, high-temperature edge can become sufficiently collisionless such that standard fluid models are no longer applicable. Experimental measurements of scrape-off-layer (SOL) parameters, including density decay length ($λ_n$) estimates presented here can be used for simple analytic estimates of kinetic effects, but need more complete kinetic treatment. We present gyrokinetic simulations, with one configuration-space dimension and two velocity-space dimensions, of low and higher-recycling LTX-$β$ SOL conditions using Gkeyll, an open-source continuum gyrokinetic code. The simulations follow a single open field line, self-consistently evolving kinetic ion and electron distribution functions, electrostatic potential, collisions, sources, and sheath losses constrained by experimental midplane measurements. The hotter low-recycling case develops a larger parallel potential variation, and a density response not captured by an adiabatic Boltzmann-electron closure of the field equations, demonstrating the importance of kinetic electrons and the ambipolar potential in reducing electron end losses. Collisionality scans show that, as ion neoclassical collisionality falls below unity, the ion distribution develops pronounced non-Maxwellian features. The corresponding SOL particle and energy confinement times increase at low collisionality, particularly for low recycling, because ions become mirror-trapped and electron losses are throttled by the ambipolar potential. These results indicate that low-recycling lithium boundaries in LTX-$β$ access a regime where mirror trapping and electrostatic confinement fundamentally alter parallel residence times relative to conventional fluid expectations, an effect expected to be more pronounced in larger devices.

</details>


### [138] [Maximization of nuclear fusion reactivity via optimal energy distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35507)
*Roberto Onofrio,Kamel Ourabah,Carlo Presilla*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: Nuclear fusion reactivity is optimized over the energy probability distribution: a single Maxwell-Boltzmann (MB) distribution is optimal where reactivity is concave (near the peak), while a bimodal MB distribution is optimal in convex regions. Superstatistical gains are always bounded by this optimal distribution, and optimal bimodal distributions at low average energies enhance D-D and D-T reactivity by ~2x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper asks which ion energy probability distribution maximizes nuclear fusion reactivity, motivated by the possibility that non-Maxwellian or superstatistical distributions (continuum of MB distributions) could increase reaction rates, relevant to magnetically confined plasmas.

Method: The authors formulate reactivity optimization as a functional problem over energy distributions, classifying regimes by the concavity/convexity of reactivity versus average available kinetic energy. They compare optimal distributions against three classes of generalized superstatistical distributions, then apply the results to D-D and D-T fusion using empirical cross-section parametrizations.

Result: A single MB distribution is optimal where reactivity is concave (near the peak); a bimodal MB distribution is optimal where it is convex. Superstatistical reactivity gains are bounded by the proposed optimal distribution in all cases examined. For D-D and D-T, optimal bimodal MB distributions at low average energies (relevant to magnetically confined plasmas) give ~2x reactivity enhancement.

Conclusion: Reactivity maximization depends on the local curvature of the reactivity function, selecting single versus bimodal MB distributions. No superstatistical distribution outperforms the optimal distribution, and low-energy bimodal shaping offers roughly a factor-of-2 gain for D-D and D-T, suggesting a potential route to enhanced fusion performance.

Abstract: We investigate the optimization of nuclear fusion reactivity relative to the choice of energy probability distribution. A single Maxwell-Boltzmann (MB) distribution is found to be optimal in the region where reactivity is a concave function of the average available kinetic energy, specifically near the reactivity peak. Conversely, in regions where the reactivity curve is convex, optimization is achieved through a bimodal MB distribution. We then discuss our results in the context of three classes of generalized energy distributions (superstatistics), which are modeled as a continuum of MB distributions. As expected, in all cases examined, the reactivity gains provided by superstatistics are bounded by the proposed optimal distribution. Finally, we apply these findings to deuterium-deuterium and deuterium-tritium fusion processes using empirical parametrizations of their respective cross sections. For both reactions, employing optimal bimodal MB distributions at low average energies-of particular interest for magnetically confined plasmas-yields a reactivity enhancement of approximately a factor of 2.

</details>


### [139] [Relativistic Space-Charge-Limited Current in Electrostatic Limit for a Finite-Width Diode with Arbitrary Injection Energy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35625)
*Y. L. Liu,S. W. Choo,L. K. Ang*

Main category: physics.plasm-ph

TL;DR: The authors develop a weakly relativistic, electrostatic model of space-charge-limited current (SCLC) with arbitrary injection energy for both accelerating-diode and drift-space regimes, then extend it to 2D finite-width planar diodes via two approaches (field-balance, FB; density-weighted, DW), validating against PIC simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SCLC models often assume non-relativistic or zero injection velocity conditions. A unified formulation covering arbitrary injection energy, relativistic effects, and finite emission-area (2D) geometry is needed to accurately predict current in practical diode/drift-space devices.

Method: Derives a 1D relativistic SCLC model from a stationary-current condition and validates it numerically. Extends it to a 2D planar diode of width W > gap D using two finite-area corrections: the field-balance (FB) approach (opposing space-charge fields at the potential minimum) and the density-weighted (DW) approach (mean absolute electron distance from the minimum).

Result: The two approaches give different corrections at finite injection energy even in the non-relativistic limit, with FB predicting a larger leading-order enhancement.
