# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [158] [On Gill's problem with anomalous diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35078)
*A. Barletta*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: The paper revisits Gill's classical linear-stability proof for buoyant flow in a vertical porous channel, extending it to anomalous mass diffusion modeled by a time-dependent diffusivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical Gill's theorem assumes normal (Fickian) diffusion. The authors want to test whether the linear stability result survives when mass transfer follows anomalous diffusion, a more general and physically relevant regime.

Method: The steady parallel buoyant flow in a vertical porous layer bounded by impermeable walls is formulated with a diffusion equation having time-dependent mass diffusivity, and the linear stability proof is analyzed under this generalized model.

Result: The implications of the anomalous diffusion model for the claim that the buoyant flow is always linearly stable are examined, revealing how the time-dependent diffusivity affects Gill's stability argument.

Conclusion: The study generalizes Gill's stability theorem to anomalous diffusion and clarifies the conditions under which the linear stability conclusion holds.

Abstract: The classical formulation of Gill's theorem establishing the linear stability of the buoyant flow in a vertical porous channel subject to transverse diffusion and bounded by impermeable walls is here reconsidered from the perspective of anomalous mass diffusion. Anomalous diffusion is a physical effect implying a more general formulation of the governing equations of mass transfer either in a fluid or in a fluid-saturated porous medium. Anomalous diffusion is mathematically modelled via a diffusion equation with a time-dependent mass diffusivity. In this study, the stationary and parallel buoyant flow in a vertical porous layer saturated by a fluid is investigated. The implications of the anomalous diffusion model for the proof that such a flow is always linearly stable are analysed.

</details>


### [159] [Instability of exact finite-amplitude acoustic-gravity waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35210)
*Christian Puntini*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Compressible Gerstner-type surface and edge waves are shown to be linearly unstable above a critical steepness, with an isentropic threshold of e>1/3 and a much lower threshold near a subharmonic resonance relevant to observed airglow waves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Godin (2015) showed compressible fluids in uniform gravity support exact finite-amplitude incompressible Gerstner-type waves transporting currents; the linear stability of these waves was unknown and is investigated here.

Method: The short-wavelength instability method for compressible flows reduces the stability of each fluid level to a two-parameter Floquet problem governed by the steepness e=e^{ka_2} and ν²=(1−e²)ν_0², with ν_0²=cos²α N_0²/Ω².

Result: In an isentropic fluid a level is unstable if e>1/3, recovering Leblanc's Gerstner threshold and extending it to compressible surface/edge waves; weak stratification lowers it to 1/3−(32/81)ν_0². In an isothermal atmosphere, principal subharmonic resonance near wavelength 5.5H/cosα makes the critical steepness vanish; for the fast airglow wave of Hecht et al. the critical steepness is ~0.1 rather than 1/3. Instability is confined to a layer beneath the surface (surface waves) and a wedge at the shoreline (edge waves).

Conclusion: Compressible Gerstner-type waves are unstable above a critical steepness that depends on stratification and can be strongly reduced by resonance, refining the interpretation of observed airglow waves.

Abstract: Godin (\textit{J. Fluid Mech.}, vol. 767, 2015, pp. 52--64) showed that compressible fluids in a uniform gravity field support finite-amplitude incompressible wave motions, in which every parcel conserves pressure and density, and constructed exact Gerstner-type surface and edge waves, possibly carried by currents. Using the short-wavelength instability method for compressible flows, we show that these waves are linearly unstable above a critical steepness. The stability of each fluid level reduces to a Floquet problem depending on two parameters: the steepness $\mathfrak{e}=e^{ka_2}$ and $ν^2=(1-\mathfrak{e}^2)ν_0^2$, where $ν_0^2=\cos^2α\,N_0^2/Ω^2$, $N_0$ is the buoyancy frequency of the hydrostatic reference state, $α$ the inclination of the plane of motion and $Ω=(gk\cosα)^{1/2}$ the intrinsic orbital frequency. In an isentropic fluid, a level is unstable if $\mathfrak{e}>1/3$, recovering Leblanc's threshold for Gerstner waves (\textit{J. Fluid Mech.}, vol. 506, 2004, pp. 245--254) and extending it to compressible surface and edge waves; weak stable stratification lowers the threshold to $\frac13-\frac{32}{81}ν_0^2+O(ν_0^4)$. In an isothermal atmosphere, $ν_0^2=κ\cosα/(kH)$, where $H$ is the pressure scale height. For horizontal wavelengths near $5.5\,H/\cosα$, principal subharmonic resonance makes the critical steepness vanish. For the fast airglow wave observed by Hecht et al. (\textit{J. Geophys. Res.}, vol. 107, 2002, D20, 4444), which Godin interpreted with his solution, the lidar temperature places the layer in which this interpretation is consistent on this resonance, where the critical steepness is typically 0.1 instead of $1/3$. Instability is confined to a layer beneath the free surface of surface waves and to a wedge at the shoreline of edge waves.

</details>


### [160] [Thermosolutal Marangoni instabilities in a weakly viscoelastic falling film with soluble surfactant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35481)
*Sanghasri Mukhopadhyay,Severine Millet,Bastien Di Pierro,Asim Mukhopadhyay*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: This study analyzes the linear stability and nonlinear evolution of a heated falling film with a soluble surfactant and a weakly viscoelastic (Walters short-memory) liquid, showing how elastic memory and solutal/thermal Marangoni stresses control instability onset and finite-amplitude wave dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Falling films with surfactants and viscoelasticity appear in coating and heat/mass transfer applications, but how elastic memory interacts with coupled thermal and solutal Marangoni stresses in controlling instability and nonlinear wave growth is not well understood.

Method: An Orr-Sommerfeld analysis and its long-wave limit determine the instability threshold and how elastic memory and the two Marangoni stresses enter it. A five-field weighted-residual model is derived for finite-amplitude disturbances, with bulk and surface transport equations written with compatible fluxes that conserve the combined projected surfactant inventory in a periodic domain.

Result: Solutal Marangoni stress suppresses disturbance growth and reduces nonlinear wave amplitude. Thermal Marangoni stress increases the maximum growth rate, though its effect on the unstable wavenumber band width differs. Elastic memory alters both the onset and the finite-wavelength response. Nonlinear profiles show a main hump with capillary ripples and variations in bulk/surface concentration and interfacial temperature.

Conclusion: The results capture the competition between thermocapillary forcing, surfactant redistribution, and elastic stress within the retained long-wave model. Larger elastic parameters are interpreted as formal predictions of the truncated constitutive equations rather than a controlled extension to strongly elastic flow.

Abstract: We examine the linear stability and nonlinear evolution of a heated falling film containing a soluble surfactant and a weakly viscoelastic liquid. The stress response is described by the short-memory Walters model, while surface tension depends on temperature and surface concentration. An Orr--Sommerfeld analysis and its long-wave limit determine how elastic memory and the two Marangoni stresses enter the instability threshold. A five-field weighted-residual model is then derived to describe finite-amplitude disturbances. The bulk and surface transport equations are written with compatible fluxes, which conserve their combined projected surfactant inventory in a periodic domain. The numerical examples show that solutal Marangoni stress suppresses disturbance growth and reduces the amplitude of the nonlinear waves. Thermal Marangoni stress increases the maximum growth rate, although its effect on the width of the unstable wavenumber band is different. Elastic memory changes both the onset and the finite-wavelength response. The nonlinear profiles develop a main hump and capillary ripples, accompanied by variations in bulk concentration, surface concentration, and interfacial temperature. These results describe the competition between thermocapillary forcing, surfactant redistribution and elastic stress within the retained long-wave model. The larger elastic parameters are interpreted as formal predictions of the truncated constitutive equations, rather than as a controlled extension to strongly elastic flow.

</details>


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [161] [A Gaussian Process State Space Formulation for Materials Constitutive Modeling: Application to Quasi-brittle Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33175)
*Houlin Xu,Chenyang Li,K. T. Ramesh,Michael D. Shields*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: A Gaussian-process state-space model (GP-SSM) replaces fixed constitutive forms and phenomenological internal variables with GP regression and latent state variables learned from stress-strain histories, demonstrated for quasi-brittle materials under triaxial compression and deployed in Abaqus/Explicit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Constitutive models for history- and path-dependent materials rely on prescribed functional forms and phenomenological, model-dependent, non-observable internal variables (e.g., damage, plastic strain). These rigid forms lack flexibility to match observations, make calibration potentially non-unique, and complicate uncertainty quantification.

Method: The authors replace the fixed constitutive form with flexible GP regression and replace prescribed internal variables with latent state variables learned from observable stress-strain histories. Latent evolution is driven by current-state stress invariants and equivalent-strain increment to capture irreversible, pressure-sensitive history. A sparse multi-output GP maps latent state plus current strain tensor to six-component stress, trained via variational sparse inference. The trained model is exported to Abaqus/Explicit via a user material subroutine (VUMAT).

Result: Four verification cases: (1) offline rollout on validation/test histories; (2) single element under changing confinement; (3) frozen-memory unloading-reloading; (4) multi-element structural model vs. Karagozian and Case Concrete (KCC). Results show closed-loop rollout reproduces held-out RVE-scale histories, tracks evolving confinement, supports unloading without violating irreversibility of the learned history state, and reproduces structural behavior consistent with KCC.

Conclusion: The GP-SSM offers a flexible, uncertainty-aware, physics-constrainable alternative to phenomenological constitutive models, with demonstrated ability to generalize in closed-loop rollout and to be deployed in finite-element structural analysis.

Abstract: Constitutive models for history-dependent, path-dependent materials typically encode this dependence in prescribed functional forms through internal variables such as damage or plastic strain that are phenomenological, model-dependent, and not directly observable. These rigid forms lack flexibility to match observations, make calibration potentially non-unique, and complicate uncertainty quantification. This paper develops a Gaussian-process state-space model (GP-SSM) that replaces the fixed form of the constitutive model with a flexible GP regression, and the prescribed internal variables with latent state variables learned directly from observable stress-strain histories. As a GP, the model is naturally endowed with a measure of uncertainty and can be constrained to satisfy physical principles. We demonstrate this framework for quasi-brittle materials under triaxial compression, where the latent variable evolution is driven by the current-state stress invariants and the increment of equivalent strain to reflect irreversible, pressure-sensitive history. A sparse multi-output GP then maps the latent state and current strain tensor to the six-component stress response, trained by variational sparse inference. The trained model is exported into Abaqus/Explicit through a user material subroutine. Four verification cases are reported: (1) offline rollout on validation and test histories; (2) a single element under changing confinement; (3) a frozen-memory unloading-reloading extension; and (4) a multi-element structural model compared against the Karagozian and Case Concrete (KCC) model. These results show that the learned state-space update reproduces held-out RVE-scale histories in closed-loop rollout, tracks evolving confinement, supports unloading without violating the irreversibility of the learned history state, and reproduces structural-level behavior consistent with KCC.

</details>


### [162] [Multi-Fidelity Particle Flow Filters based on Log Homotopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35712)
*Abhinab Bhattacharjee,Andrey A. Popov,Amit N. Subrahmanya,Adrian Sandu*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Multi-Fidelity Particle Flow Filter (MFPFF) that combines a linear control variate framework with multilevel Monte Carlo, using many cheap reduced-order particles as control variates to augment a few expensive high-fidelity particles, achieving high-fidelity-level accuracy at much lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional particle filters suffer from weight collapse and particle degeneracy in high-dimensional spaces. While particle flow filters avoid resampling by smoothly transporting prior particles to the posterior, running enough high-fidelity particles remains computationally expensive, motivating a multi-fidelity approach to improve accuracy at comparable cost.

Method: Constructs multi-fidelity particle flow filters by augmenting a small number of expensive high-fidelity particles (full-order model runs) with a large number of inexpensive low-resolution particles built from reduced-order surrogates via intrusive Galerkin projection. These surrogates serve as control variates in a linear control variate framework combined with multilevel Monte Carlo to enhance the statistical estimate of the full-order posterior. Two log-homotopy-based flows are examined: the stochastic Gromov flow and the deterministic Exact flow.

Result: Numerical experiments on the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) and Quasi-Geostrophic (QG) test problems show that MFPFF achieves accuracy comparable to high-fidelity simulations while significantly reducing computational expense.

Conclusion: Multi-fidelity particle flow filtering with reduced-order control variates is an effective strategy for obtaining accurate posterior estimates at substantially lower computational cost, addressing the dimensionality and cost limitations of conventional particle filters.

Abstract: This work proposes a new Multi-Fidelity Particle Flow Filter (MFPFF) based on a linear control variate framework and multilevel Monte Carlo methods. Conventional particle filters often suffer from weight collapse and particle degeneracy in high-dimensional spaces. Particle flow filters address this by smoothly transporting prior particles toward the posterior distribution, thereby avoiding the need for resampling. This work constructs multi-fidelity particle flow filters by augmenting a small number of expensive high-fidelity particles (full-order model runs) with a large number of inexpensive low-resolution particles (reduced-order surrogates constructed via intrusive Galerkin projection). These surrogates act as control variates to enhance the statistical estimate of the full-order posterior, yielding enhanced analysis accuracy at comparable computational cost. The work focuses on two particle flows based on log homotopy, the stochastic Gromov flow and deterministic Exact flow. Numerical experiments with the Korteweg-De Vries (KdV) and Quasi-Geostrophic (QG) test problems demonstrate that the proposed MFPFF achieves accuracy comparable to high-fidelity simulations while significantly reducing computational expense.

</details>


<div id='math-ph'></div>

# math-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [163] [On the Energy Minimizing Problem in Plasma Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33040)
*Maurice de Gosson*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: Plasma minimum-energy states can be found via a quantum-mechanical reformulation using the Wigner transform and the symplectic geometry of Gaussian states, avoiding the topological difficulties of Qin's classical "Gromov ground states" approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Finding minimum energy states of plasma is fundamental to fusion theory. Gardner's classical approach uses only volume-preserving diffeomorphisms and misses symplectic rigidity; Qin's "Gromov ground states" incorporate this but remain in the classical Hamiltonian domain, leading to topological difficulties.

Method: The authors introduce a quantum-mechanical formulation of the minimum-energy problem, based on the Wigner transform and the symplectic geometry of Gaussian states, to circumvent the topological obstructions of the classical formulation.

Result: The quantum-mechanical formulation circumvents the topological difficulties encountered in Qin's classical Gromov ground state construction (concrete quantitative results are not stated in the abstract).

Conclusion: Reformulating plasma minimum-energy states through Wigner transforms and Gaussian-state symplectic geometry offers a viable route beyond classical Hamiltonian constructions, though the abstract leaves the detailed consequences to the full paper.

Abstract: A fundamental question in plasma physics and its applications to fusion theory is to find minimum energy stares of plasma.. Hong Qin has suggested the use of what he calls "Gromov ground states" which take into account the phenomenon of symplectic rigidity, as opposed to Gardner's classical approach which only use volume-preserving diffeomorphisms. Howev4er Qin's approach leads to topological difficulties because his constructions remain in the classical (Hamiltonian) domain. We circumvent these difficulties by introducing a quantum-mechanical formulation of the problem, based on the Wigner transform and the symplectic geometry of Gaussian state

</details>


### [164] [Measurement-dependent reduction of open dynamics in the quantum phase estimation algorithm: the visible subspace and the damped Fejér kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35332)
*Marina O. Gordeichuk,Oleg M. Kiselev*

Main category: math-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies the minimal observable space needed to predict the full output distribution of quantum phase estimation (QPE) under open dynamics of the counting register, using Krylov reduction theory, and gives an explicit structure, exact dimension formula, and error estimates for approximate reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting QPE output distributions under noise requires knowing which observables actually matter; understanding the minimal sufficient observable space can reduce complexity and reveal how different noise channels affect the distribution.

Method: Builds on Krylov reduction theory and applies it to the full Fourier measurement of QPE. Computes the visible observable space via cyclic sums of the state matrix; for adjoint generators diagonal on matrix units, uses spectral classes inside residue classes of index difference modulo M. Also develops approximate reduction via a finite-time Gramian with a posteriori residual estimate.

Result: Gives a constructive basis, exact dimension formula, and attainable bounds M ≤ dim(visible space) ≤ M²−M+1. Covers independent qubit dephasing; an analytically solvable GKLS model yields a damped Fejér kernel; two random-unitary channels with equal Ramsey contrast are distinguished by higher harmonics. Approximate reduction has quadratic error order for reducing unperturbed subspace and compatible initial state; numerics confirm consistency and error behavior under non-Toeplitz perturbations.

Conclusion: The minimal observable space for QPE under open dynamics has an explicit, computable structure with sharp dimension bounds, and approximate Krylov reduction provides controlled, quadratic-order error, enabling analysis and discrimination of noise models from QPE distributions.

Abstract: For the quantum phase estimation (QPE) algorithm we study the minimal space of observables that is sufficient to predict the entire output distribution under open dynamics of the counting register. Building on the general Krylov theory of reduction, we compute the structure of this space explicitly for the full Fourier measurement. The outcome probabilities are determined by cyclic sums of the state matrix. If the adjoint generator is diagonal on the matrix units, the spectral classes inside the residue classes of the index difference modulo the register dimension $M$ give a constructive basis, an exact dimension formula, and attainable bounds: the dimension of the visible space lies between $M$ and $M^2-M+1$. The class includes independent qubit dephasing, and an analytically solvable reference model of Gorini--Kossakowski--Lindblad--Sudarshan (GKLS) type yields a damped Fejér kernel; two random-unitary channels with the same Ramsey contrast show that higher harmonics are distinguishable in the QPE distribution. For approximate reduction we use a finite-time Gramian and an a posteriori residual estimate, with a quadratic error order for a reducing unperturbed subspace and a compatible initial state. Numerical calculations check the consistency of the representations and the behaviour of the error under non-Toeplitz perturbations.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.SR'></div>

# astro-ph.SR [[Back]](#toc)

### [165] [Trapped sausage modes in magnetic pores: existence criteria and viscous damping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33251)
*Zanyar Ebrahimi*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: This study numerically investigates trapped sausage waves in photospheric magnetic pores modeled as cylindrical flux tubes with smooth radial profiles, examining the limits of analytical cut-off criteria and the role of viscous dissipation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytical cut-off criteria for sausage waves were derived assuming step-function equilibrium profiles, but real pores have smoothly varying magnetic field and density. The applicability of these criteria and the role of viscous dissipation in realistic pore conditions remain unclear.

Method: A pore is modeled as a cylindrical magnetic flux tube with smoothly varying radial profiles, colder than the surrounding atmosphere. For finite plasma-β, the linearized sausage-mode equations are derived and solved numerically as an initial-value problem to obtain full spatio-temporal wave evolution. Viscous damping is studied via wave energy evolution.

Result: The step-function analytical cut-off predictions depend on equilibrium parameters and are of limited applicability. Different parameter regimes show distinct wave behaviors, including simultaneous excitation of trapped fast and slow sausage waves. Energy dissipation increases with decreasing viscous Reynolds number, increasing longitudinal wavenumber, and increasing radial magnetic-field contrast, consistent with phase-mixing-enhanced viscous damping.

Conclusion: Radial equilibrium structure is important for assessing sausage-wave trapping, and wave-energy dissipation is sensitive to the physical parameters of photospheric magnetic pores.

Abstract: The conditions governing the existence and evolution of trapped sausage waves in photospheric magnetic pores are investigated, with particular emphasis on the limitations of analytical cut-off criteria and the role of viscous dissipation. A pore is modelled as a cylindrical magnetic flux tube with smoothly varying radial profiles of magnetic field and density, subject to the constraint that its temperature is lower than that of the surrounding atmosphere. For finite plasma-$β$, the linearised equations governing sausage-mode perturbations are derived, and an initial-value problem is solved numerically to obtain the full spatio-temporal evolution of the waves. The applicability of the analytical cut-off relation derived for a flux tube with step-function equilibrium profiles is examined, revealing that its predictions depend on the equilibrium parameters. The simulations reveal distinct wave behaviours across different parameter regimes, including the simultaneous excitation of trapped fast and slow sausage waves for a representative photospheric parameter set. Viscous damping is further investigated through the temporal evolution of the wave energy. Energy dissipation increases with decreasing viscous Reynolds number, increasing longitudinal wavenumber, and increasing radial magnetic-field contrast. These trends are consistent with enhanced viscous damping associated with increasingly fine spatial gradients generated through phase mixing. The findings highlight the importance of radial equilibrium structure in assessing sausage-wave trapping and demonstrate the sensitivity of wave-energy dissipation to the physical parameters of photospheric magnetic pores.

</details>


### [166] [Magnetic switchback development from the evolution of torsional Alfvén waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34518)
*Peter F Wyper,Etienne Pariat,Lorenzo Matteini,Jonathan Squire,Jade Touresse*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: Torsional Alfvén waves, launched by surface rotational motions into a super-radially expanding coronal hole, can evolve into switchbacks in a ring configuration via wave steepening, an induced parallel plasma jet, and approximate spherical polarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic switchbacks—largely Alfvénic deflections or reversals of the solar wind magnetic field—remain poorly understood, and their origin is highly debated despite intense research.

Method: 3D simulation study: surface rotational motions launch torsional Alfvén waves into a super-radially expanding magnetic field of a coronal hole; analysis of wave propagation, jet formation, switchback geometry, and scaling laws.

Result: In the expanding wind the waves steepen while inducing a cylindrical parallel plasma jet; the combination of jet shear and wave evolution under approximate spherical polarization (|B| ≈ const.) forms ring-configuration switchbacks. The study characterizes their 3D nature, formation location, peak deflection, parallel/perpendicular length scales, and derives a linear-regime deflection expression.

Conclusion: Torsional Alfvén waves provide a viable mechanism for switchback formation; results are discussed in relation to recent observations and prior switchback formation studies.

Abstract: Magnetic switchbacks are mostly Alfvénic local deflections, or even reversals, of the ambient magnetic field of the solar wind. Despite intense research their origin remains unclear and is highly debated. Here we describe a new simulation study demonstrating how torsional Alfvén waves can evolve into switchbacks. We use surface rotational motions to launch torsional Alfvén waves into the super-radially expanding magnetic field of a coronal hole. The simulations demonstrate that in such an expanding wind, the waves initially steepen as they propagate while also inducing a cylindrical parallel plasma jet. A combination of shear from the jet and wave evolution under the constraint of approximate spherical polarisation ($|\mathbf{B}|\approx const.$) then leads to the formation of switchbacks in a ring configuration. We explore the 3D nature of the switchbacks, how and where they from, and present scalings for their peak deflection and parallel and perpendicular length scales. We also derive an expression for switchback deflection in the linear regime which we contrast against our results. The results are discussed in relation to recent observations and previous switchback formation studies.

</details>


<div id='physics.optics'></div>

# physics.optics [[Back]](#toc)

### [167] [Effects of Pulse Duration on Kerr Self-Focusing and Weld Formation in Ultrafast Laser Welding of Fused Silica](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35250)
*Chen Yitong,Li Nan,Han Zhijie,Li Yuxuan,Zhang Qingwei,Dong Zihui,Sun Yuming,Lin Zhe,Zhu Gangyi,Yang Shanglu*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This study examines how laser pulse duration (262 fs–10 ps) controls Kerr self-focusing, focal position drift, and weld formation in laser welding across a joining interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kerr self-focusing shifts the laser interaction region away from the geometric focus, making energy deposition near the joining interface hard to control. The authors want to understand how pulse duration governs this nonlinear focusing and the resulting weld quality.

Method: Pulse duration is varied from 262 fs to 10 ps. ICCD imaging tracks the axial position and shape of plasma emission during pulse accumulation. A ray-optics model incorporating the Kerr effect is used to calculate focal shift and compare with experimental trends.

Result: Distinct axial plasma positions arise for different pulse durations within the first 2000 pulses and continue to evolve with accumulation. Final weld morphology, weld position, and shear strength change accordingly. The Kerr-inclusive simulation reproduces the experimental focal shift trend.

Conclusion: Pulse duration strongly links nonlinear focusing, interaction position, and weld formation, and Kerr self-focusing explains the observed focal position changes—offering guidance for controlling energy deposition in interface welding.

Abstract: Kerr self-focusing can shift the laser interaction region away from the geometrical focus, making energy deposition near the joining interface difficult to control. Here, the effect of pulse duration on nonlinear focusing and weld formation is investigated from 262 fs to 10 ps. ICCD imaging shows clear changes in the axial position and shape of the plasma emission. Different pulse durations already produce distinct axial positions during the initial 2000 pulses, and these positions continue to evolve with pulse accumulation. The final weld morphology, weld position, and shear strength show corresponding changes. A ray-optics model including the Kerr effect is used to calculate the focal shift. The simulated trend agrees with the experiment and supports the role of Kerr self-focusing in the observed focal position change. These results clarify the relationship between pulse duration, nonlinear focusing, interaction position, and weld formation.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.dis-nn'></div>

# cond-mat.dis-nn [[Back]](#toc)

### [168] [Symmetry-driven in-situ physical adjoint computing beyond reciprocity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34637)
*Cunshen Li,Rong Li,Jiongning Che,Cheng-Zhen Wang,Liang Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Sym-iPAC enables in-situ physical adjoint computing in nonreciprocal systems by exploiting transpose symmetries (local geometric permutations, complex gauge reweighting, latent port symmetry via Schur reduction), validated on programmable nonreciprocal microwave networks for closed-loop optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In-situ physical adjoint optimization normally relies on reciprocity to obtain adjoint responses, which fails in nonreciprocal systems where directional (asymmetric) transport breaks that assumption.

Method: Proposes symmetry-driven in-situ physical adjoint computing (Sym-iPAC), obtaining adjoint responses on the original hardware through three classes of transpose symmetry: local geometric permutations, complex gauge reweighting, and latent symmetry in effective port dynamics revealed by Schur reduction; tested in programmable nonreciprocal microwave networks.

Result: Experiments validate these transpose relations and yield adjoint gradients, enabling closed-loop optimization of nonreciprocal systems.

Conclusion: Experimentally accessible transpose symmetry provides a route to in-situ physical adjoint computing beyond the reciprocity constraint.

Abstract: Nonreciprocity enriches wave control and information processing through directional transport, yet challenges conventional in-situ physical adjoint optimization, which relies on reciprocity to acquire adjoint responses. Here we demonstrate symmetry-driven in-situ physical adjoint computing (Sym-iPAC) in nonreciprocal systems, which enables access to adjoint responses on the original hardware through three classes of transpose symmetry: local geometric permutations, complex gauge reweighting, and latent symmetry in effective port dynamics revealed by Schur reduction. Experiments with programmable nonreciprocal microwave networks validate these transpose relations and yield adjoint gradients, enabling closed-loop optimization. These results establish experimentally accessible transpose symmetry as a route to in-situ physical adjoint computing beyond reciprocity.

</details>


<div id='math.FA'></div>

# math.FA [[Back]](#toc)

### [169] [Optimal Non-Homogeneous Improvements of the Anisotropic Hardy Inequality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33346)
*Giuseppina di Blasio,Giovanni Pisante,Georgios Psaradakis*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The paper establishes optimal non-homogeneous improvements of the Hardy inequality for a strongly convex Finsler norm F on R^n across all 1<p<∞, identifying the optimal logarithmic weight exponents in three regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Hardy inequality for a Finsler norm F is a classical result, but its optimal non-homogeneous (logarithmic-weight) improvements across the full range of p, in the anisotropic setting, are not fully characterized—particularly the borderline case p=n, where the optimal exponent was previously known only for anisotropically radial functions.

Method: A common ground-state reduction is used, combined with a splitting into an F_o-radial part and a mean-free part for 1<p≤n, and with an anisotropic Morrey representation formula for p>n.

Result: The anisotropic Hardy difference controls a weighted critical Sobolev norm for 1<p<n, a weighted Trudinger-type exponential integrability (with the Hardy term replaced by its Leray logarithmic correction) for p=n, and a weighted Hölder seminorm for p>n; in each regime the logarithmic weights have optimal exponents, and the p=n result extends to general functions the optimal exponent previously known only for anisotropically radial functions.

Conclusion: The anisotropic Hardy inequality admits optimal non-homogeneous improvements in all regimes 1<p<∞, with optimal logarithmic exponents, and the borderline p=n case is resolved for general functions.

Abstract: We consider the Hardy inequality associated with a strongly convex Finsler norm $F$ on $\mathbb{R}^n$ and we study its optimal non-homogeneous improvements in the whole range $1<p<\infty$. We show that the anisotropic Hardy difference controls a weighted critical Sobolev norm when $1<p<n$, a weighted exponential integrability of Trudinger type when $p=n$, where the Hardy term is replaced by its Leray logarithmic correction, and a weighted Hölder seminorm when $p>n$, proving in each regime that the logarithmic weights appear with the optimal exponents. In the borderline case $p=n$ this extends to general functions the optimal exponent previously known only for anisotropically radial functions. The proofs rely on a common ground-state reduction, combined with a splitting into an $F_o$-radial and a mean-free part for $1<p\le n$, and with an anisotropic Morrey representation formula for $p>n$.

</details>


### [170] [Boundedness and continuity of phase-corrected gradients on Sobolev spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35584)
*Lars Eric Hientzsch,Raffaele Scandone,Robert Wegner*

Main category: math.FA

TL;DR: The phase-corrected gradient ψ↦∇∘ψ (and more generally f(|ψ|⁻¹ψ)∇ψ) is shown to be bounded and continuous from W^{s,2} to W^{s-1,2} for 1 ≤ s < 3/2, with the threshold s = 3/2 sharp; results extend to non-decaying functions relevant to Ginzburg–Landau theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The phase-corrected gradient ∇∘ψ = |ψ|⁻¹ψ̄∇ψ arises in quantum hydrodynamic models (its imaginary part Λ) and fits nonlinear composition operator theory since Re[∇∘ψ] = ∇|ψ|. Understanding the regularity/continuity of ψ↦∇∘ψ on Sobolev spaces is needed, notably to resolve an open question about continuity of ψ↦|ψ|.

Method: Treat ψ↦∇∘ψ as a nonlinear composition operator. Prove boundedness and continuity from W^{s,2}(R^d) to W^{s-1,2}(R^d) for 1 ≤ s < 3/2, establish sharpness of the s = 3/2 threshold, extend to functions without decay at infinity (Ginzburg–Landau setting), and generalize to gradients f(|ψ|⁻¹ψ)∇ψ with Lipschitz f: S¹→C.

Result: The map ψ↦∇∘ψ is bounded and continuous from W^{s,2} to W^{s-1,2} for 1 ≤ s < 3/2, and the threshold s = 3/2 is sharp. This answers an open question on continuity of ψ↦|ψ|. Results hold for functions not decaying at infinity, and extend to general Lipschitz phase-corrected gradients.

Conclusion: The phase-corrected gradient behaves as a bounded, continuous Sobolev mapping up to the critical exponent 3/2, with broad applicability to quantum hydrodynamics, Ginzburg–Landau theory, and certain parabolic PDEs.

Abstract: For a function $ψ: \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathbb{C}$, we define the phase-corrected gradient $\nabla_{\!\circ} ψ= |ψ|^{-1} \overlineψ\,\nablaψ$ and study the regularity of the map $ψ\mapsto \nabla_{\!\circ} ψ$ on Sobolev spaces. Since $\mathrm{Re}[\nabla_{\!\circ} ψ] = \nabla |ψ|$, the phase-corrected gradient fits naturally into the framework of nonlinear composition operators. Its imaginary part $Λ= \mathrm{Im}[\nabla_{\!\circ} ψ]$, on the other hand, plays a key role in quantum hydrodynamic models. We prove that $ψ\mapsto \nabla_{\!\circ} ψ$ is bounded and continuous from $W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ to $W^{s-1,2}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ for $1 \leq s < \frac32$, and we show that the threshold $s = \frac32$ is sharp. In particular, this answers an open question concerning the continuity of the map $ψ\mapsto |ψ|$ on Sobolev spaces. We also show that our results remain valid for functions that do not necessarily decay at infinity, a relevant setting in the context of Ginzburg--Landau theory. Finally, we extend our analysis to more general phase-corrected gradients of the form $f(|ψ|^{-1} ψ) \nabla ψ$, where $f: \mathbb{S}^1 \rightarrow \mathbb{C}$ is Lipschitz, and discuss implications for certain parabolic PDEs.

</details>


<div id='gr-qc'></div>

# gr-qc [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [Gravitational Radiation Coupled to a real massive scalar Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33928)
*Yue Ma,Xianghao Wu,Weidong Zhang*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: The paper studies small-data global solutions of the Einstein–Klein–Gordon system (positive-mass real scalar field, wave gauge) and shows the metric perturbation's linearized trace component decays optimally like t^{-1}, plus a quantitative one-point observability estimate bounding initial Klein–Gordon energy by its leading coefficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding precise late-time asymptotics of coupled Einstein–scalar-field systems, and whether asymptotic data (a single point/coefficient) can recover initial energy, is a natural question in mathematical relativity that the authors address.

Method: Asymptotic analysis of small-data global solutions in wave gauge; the linearized trace component of the metric perturbation is analyzed to derive sharp decay rates, and a one-point observability inequality is established relating the initial Klein–Gordon energy to the leading-order coefficient.

Result: The linearized trace component of the metric perturbation decays at the optimal rate t̂^{-1} ≃ t^{-1}; a quantitative one-point observability estimate holds, controlling the initial Klein–Gordon energy by the leading-order coefficient.

Conclusion: The results give sharp decay and an observability/energy-recovery statement for small-data Einstein–Klein–Gordon solutions, providing a quantitative link between late-time asymptotic data and initial energy.

Abstract: We study the asymptotic behavior of small-data global solutions to the Einstein--Klein--Gordon system with a positive-mass real scalar field in wave gauge. We show that the linearized trace component of the metric perturbation admits a $\hat t^{-1}\simeq t^{-1}$ optimal decreasing rate. Moreover, a quantitative one-point observability estimate is established: the initial Klein-Gordon energy is controlled by this leading order coefficient.

</details>


### [172] [Existence of static axisymmetric solutions to the Einstein equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34555)
*Philippe G. LeFloch,Luc Rozoy*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: The authors construct smooth Riemannian metrics and positive scalar potentials on a ball with two disjoint balls removed (a three-manifold with three boundary components), invariant under a circle action, that solve the static Einstein equations with positive cosmological constant and correspond to a configuration with two small black holes (two inner horizons) plus an outer horizon. Existence is obtained via a weighted-Hölder contraction argument, with the two masses as free parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to establish existence of static solutions of the vacuum Einstein equations with positive cosmological constant (Einstein–Kottler / de Sitter family) describing multiple black holes in a single cosmological-static region—a multipole/black-hole-condensation-type configuration. Prior constructions typically handle one horizon or use gluing; here the goal is a rigorous smooth construction with two inner non-degenerate horizons whose areas are controlled by two essentially arbitrary and comparable small mass parameters, sitting inside one outer cosmological horizon.

Method: Smooth, circle-invariant ansatz on a ball minus two open balls. The potential/lapse is positive in the interior and vanishes on all three boundary components (non-degenerate horizons). Regions are matched: Kottler (Schwarzschild–de Sitter-like) collars near each inner horizon and near the outer horizon, de Sitter space in the far/intermediate region. The core analysis solves a reduced elliptic Einstein equation carrying two obstruction tensors; weighted Hölder estimates are derived to control the growth of the inverse operator and to bound the nonlinear remainder, enabling a contraction argument. Simultaneous control is needed near the shrinking horizons, between the centers, and in the outer region, with two small scales: each inner horizon radius scaling linearly in the individual mass, and the separation of reference centers scaling like the cube root of the total mass; these vanish at different rates while the cosmological length stays fixed. The two obstruction tensors' coefficients are removed by varying the two axial positions; the contracted Bianchi identity plus an energy estimate for an auxiliary vector field (a Killing field used in the projection) show the remaining obstructions vanish.

Result: Existence of smooth circle-invariant static solutions with positive cosmological constant on a compact three-manifold given by a ball with two disjoint open balls removed. The potential is positive in the interior and vanishes on three non-degenerate horizons; each inner horizon radius scales linearly with its individual mass, and the center separation scales as the cube root of the total mass. In addition, a rigidity/uniqueness-type statement: for fixed cosmological constant, the intrinsic metric of one boundary component determines a static solution with simply connected interior and non-vanishing boundary gradient, up to isometry and multiplication of the potential by a positive constant.

Conclusion: The paper provides a rigorous construction of static, positive-cosmological-constant vacuum solutions with two small (comparable) black-hole horizons inside one outer horizon, parameterized by two masses, using weighted Hölder estimates and a contraction/obstruction-removal scheme together with a Bianchi-identity energy argument. It also gives a boundary-rigidity result showing that one boundary component's intrinsic metric determines the static solution up to isometry and constant rescaling of the potential.

Abstract: We construct smooth Riemannian metrics and scalar potentials that are invariant under a circle action and satisfy the static Einstein equations with positive cosmological constant. The underlying compact three-manifold is a ball with two disjoint open balls removed. The potential (or lapse function) is positive in the interior and vanishes at the boundary, consisting of three non-degenerate horizons. The areas of the two inner boundary components are controlled by two arbitrary parameters, namely the masses of the black holes, which are taken to be sufficiently small and comparable. Each inner horizon radius scales linearly with the individual mass parameters, while the separation of their reference centers scales as the cube root of the total mass. These two scales are assumed to be sufficiently small at different rates, whereas the cosmological length remains fixed. The main analytical challenge is to control the linearized equation and the nonlinear remainder simultaneously near the shrinking horizons, between the centers and in the outer region. Weighted Hölder estimates quantify the growth of the inverse and show that the error of an approximation joining Kottler collars to de Sitter space is small enough to close a contraction argument. Namely, we solve a reduced elliptic Einstein equation that is endowed with two obstruction tensors; varying the two axial positions removes their coefficients. The contracted Bianchi identity and an energy estimate for an auxiliary vector field (Killing field) introduced by the projection to vanish. Finally, for a fixed cosmological constant, the intrinsic metric of one of the two boundary components is sufficient to determine a static solution with simply connected interior and non-vanishing boundary gradient, up to isometry and multiplication of the potential by a positive constant.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [173] [Greedy Orthogonal Polynomial Manifolds with Lipschitz Control for Dimensionality Reduction and Reduced Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33784)
*Tomoki Koike,Benjamin Peherstorfer,Elizabeth Qian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Greedy-OPM, a greedy algorithm that builds polynomial manifolds by jointly selecting encoder modes and orthonormal polynomial features, under a Lipschitz bound on the decoder, to improve generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Polynomial decoders give nonlinear low-dimensional representations, but unconstrained decoders can generalize poorly away from the training data. The authors want a construction that keeps high reconstruction accuracy while controlling the decoder's smoothness (Lipschitz constant).

Method: Represent the decoder's polynomial terms with features that are orthonormal on the training data (empirical orthogonality). Exploit this to design Greedy-OPM, which greedily/iteratively selects encoder modes and polynomial features jointly, minimizing reconstruction error subject to a prescribed bound on the decoder's Lipschitz constant.

Result: Numerical experiments show that enforcing the Lipschitz constraint improves generalization away from the training data while still retaining high reconstruction accuracy.

Conclusion: Orthonormal (on-data) polynomial features plus a Lipschitz-bounded greedy selection yield polynomial manifolds that are both accurate and better generalizing outside the training set.

Abstract: Polynomial manifolds provide nonlinear low-dimensional representations through polynomial decoders. We propose to represent the polynomial terms of the decoder using features that are orthonormal on the training data. This empirical orthogonality enables a greedy algorithm, Greedy-OPM, which jointly selects encoder modes and polynomial features while minimizing the reconstruction error under a prescribed bound on the decoder's Lipschitz constant. Numerical experiments show that this Lipschitz constraint improves generalization away from the training data while retaining high reconstruction accuracy.

</details>


### [174] [Model reduction in time: Learning flow maps for reducing stochastic dynamical systems in time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35504)
*Shreya Jha,Benjamin Peherstorfer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FM-FTM is a nonintrusive model reduction method that learns flow maps to replace many sequential time-integration steps, accelerating ensemble predictions of stochastic dynamical systems by up to two orders of magnitude while preserving accuracy of time marginals and ensemble statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting with dynamical systems is computationally expensive because reaching future times requires many sequential integration steps, even with reduced models. Ensemble predictions of stochastic systems compound this cost.

Method: The approach uses first-order trajectory matching (FTM) to learn a deterministic representation of the stochastic probability-current dynamics, then reduces its temporal evolution into a flow map whose evaluation directly yields long-horizon predictions, skipping multiple integration steps.

Result: Numerical experiments on evolution problems with stochastic forcing show up to two orders of magnitude acceleration of ensemble predictions, with accurate prediction of time marginals and ensemble statistics.

Conclusion: FM-FTM provides a nonintrusive, accurate, and substantially faster alternative for ensemble prediction of stochastic dynamical systems.

Abstract: Predictions with dynamical systems can be computationally expensive---even with reduced models---because reaching future prediction times can require many sequential time integration steps. We propose Flow-Map First-Order Trajectory Matching (FM-FTM), a nonintrusive model reduction approach that accelerates ensemble predictions of stochastic dynamical systems by learning flow maps that replace multiple time-integration steps. Our method uses first-order trajectory matching (FTM) to learn a deterministic representation of the stochastic probability-current dynamics and then reduces its evolution over time into a flow map whose evaluation directly yields predictions over long time horizons. Numerical experiments on evolution problems with stochastic forcing demonstrate that FM-FTM accelerates ensemble predictions by up to two orders of magnitude while accurately predicting time marginals and ensemble statistics.

</details>


<div id='math.ST'></div>

# math.ST [[Back]](#toc)

### [175] [Optimal tradeoffs between variance and sensitivity, via Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35858)
*Adam Quinn Jaffe,Congwei Yang,Nicolás García Trillos,Bodhisattva Sen*

Main category: math.ST

TL;DR: The paper studies the multiobjective tradeoff between variance and "sensitivity" (expected estimator change under infinitesimal input perturbations) for unbiased estimators, showing the Pareto front is characterized by Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry interpolating Hellinger and Wasserstein geometries, and develops a Z-estimation method achieving asymptotically Pareto-optimal estimators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical statistics minimizes variance of unbiased estimators, but recent work seeks to control other notions of estimator instability. The authors previously introduced "sensitivity"—the expected change in an estimator under infinitesimal perturbations of inputs—and here aim to understand how variance and sensitivity trade off, rather than optimizing either alone.

Method: They formulate a multiobjective problem minimizing variance and sensitivity over unbiased estimators and characterize Pareto optimal tradeoffs. Their main result identifies the Pareto front via the Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry of the statistical model, which interpolates the Hellinger geometry (variance) and Wasserstein geometry (sensitivity). They also develop a Z-estimation technique to construct estimators asymptotically attaining Pareto optimal variance-sensitivity pairs, and solve the required continuum of PDEs in some parametric examples.

Result: The Pareto front for variance-sensitivity tradeoffs is precisely determined by the Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry, interpolating between Hellinger (variance) and Wasserstein (sensitivity) geometries. The Z-estimation technique yields estimators that asymptotically achieve Pareto optimal pairs. The program is fully executed in several non-trivial parametric examples. As a corollary, the MLE attains the smallest asymptotic sensitivity among all estimators achieving optimal asymptotic variance.

Conclusion: Variance and sensitivity tradeoffs for unbiased estimators are governed by a unifying Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry, and asymptotically Pareto optimal estimators can be constructed via Z-estimation. The framework also clarifies classical problems, notably showing the MLE is optimal in sensitivity among asymptotically efficient estimators.

Abstract: While the goal of classical statistical theory is to minimize the variance of unbiased estimators, an additional goal of some recent research is to minimize or constrain other notions of the instability of estimators. In particular, a subset of the authors recently introduced a notion of instability termed "sensitivity", which represents the expected amount that an estimator would change if its inputs were infinitesimally perturbed. In this work, we study the multiobjective problem of minimizing both the variance and sensitivity over unbiased estimators in a given statistical model, and we aim to determine Pareto optimal tradeoffs between these two objectives. Our main results show that the Pareto front for these tradeoffs is precisely determined by endowing the statistical model with the Hellinger-Kantorovich geometry, which interpolates between the Hellinger and Wasserstein geometries that are known to be associated, respectively, with the separate minimization of variance and sensitivity. We also develop a $Z$-estimation technique which constructs estimators that asymptotically achieve Pareto optimal pairs of variance and sensitivity. While computing the Pareto front and constructing the resulting estimators in practice requires solving a continuum of partial differential equations, we fully execute this program in some parametric examples of interest where the analysis is already non-trivial. Our results also shed light on some classical statistical problems, for example by showing that the maximum likelihood estimator achieves the smallest asymptotic sensitivity among all estimators achieving the optimal asymptotic variance.

</details>


<div id='math.CV'></div>

# math.CV [[Back]](#toc)

### [176] [Integrable distortion II: the p-Weil-Petersson variational problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34007)
*Gaven Martin,Cong Yao*

Main category: math.CV

TL;DR: The paper studies hyperbolically weighted distortion energies for finite-distortion disk homeomorphisms with prescribed boundary data, and the associated monotone Sobolev inverse problem, establishing a polyconvexity threshold p_c=1+1/√2 that separates regularity of minimisers from rank-one comparison failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterise admissible boundary data and understand existence, uniqueness and regularity of minimisers for weighted distortion energies and their inverse monotone-Sobolev formulation, controlling distortion below ellipticity cutoffs.

Method: Polyconvexity analysis of the inverse density; selected barrier approximation with uniform scalar gradient estimates and a weighted Reich–Strebel comparison; finite rank-one comparison arguments for low p; variational and energy-dependent quasiconformal estimates.

Result: For 1<p≤2 the inverse density is polyconvex iff p≥p_c; then quasisymmetric data with finite-energy monotone barriers yield minimisers with holomorphic Ahlfors–Hopf differentials and strong local Sobolev convergence. For p_c<p<2 a finite-energy finite-distortion barrier gives a unique absolute monotone minimiser that is an asymptotically conformal quasiconformal diffeomorphism, characterising admissible boundary data as T^p. For 1<p<p_c, finite rank-one comparisons bound distortion on the positive-Jacobian set; attained minima are quasiconformal diffeomorphisms or have rank-one derivatives on positive area. For 0<p≤1, finite-distortion or locally Sobolev-invertible minimisers are Möbius. Energy-dependent quasiconformal bounds hold for regular minimisers when p>2.

Conclusion: The critical exponent p_c=1+1/√2 governs the regularity theory: above it minimisers are well-behaved (unique, quasiconformal, asymptotically conformal, with Ahlfors–Hopf structure), while below it rank-one convexity only gives partial constraints; spanning the full range of p yields a complete boundary-data characterisation and quasiconformal bounds.

Abstract: We study the hyperbolically weighted distortion energies \[
  \mathcal{I}_p(f)=\int_\mathbb{D}
  \frac{|μ_f|^p}{(1-|μ_f|^2)^{\max\{2,p\}/2}}\,dA_η\] for finite-distortion disk homeomorphisms with prescribed boundary values, together with the associated inverse problem in the monotone Sobolev class. For $1<p\leq2$, the inverse density is polyconvex precisely when $p\geq p_{\mathrm c}=1+1/\sqrt2$. In this range, quasisymmetric data with finite-energy monotone barriers yield minimisers with holomorphic Ahlfors--Hopf differentials and strong local Sobolev convergence of minimising subsequences.
  For $p_{\mathrm c}<p<2$, a finite-energy finite-distortion barrier yields a unique absolute monotone minimiser, which is a quasiconformal diffeomorphism and is asymptotically conformal. Together with the boundary-class theorem of Part~I, this characterises the admissible boundary data as $T^p$. The proof combines a selected barrier approximation, uniform scalar gradient estimates and a weighted Reich--Strebel comparison.
  For $1<p<p_{\mathrm c}$, finite rank-one comparisons bound distortion on the positive-Jacobian set by a cutoff strictly below the ellipticity cutoff. An attained monotone minimum is either a quasiconformal diffeomorphism or has nonzero rank-one derivatives on a set of positive area. For $0<p\leq1$, minimisers with finite distortion, or homeomorphic minimisers with a locally Sobolev inverse, are Möbius maps. We also obtain energy-dependent quasiconformal bounds for regular minimisers when $p>2$. \

</details>

### [177] [Interaction of Strong X-mode Waves with Magnetized Pair Plasmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35849)
*Navin Sridhar*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: Strong superluminal X-mode waves in magnetized pair plasma behave in two regimes determined by ε_B = a₀ω_B/ω₀. Below 1, magnetic field adds a restoring frequency that extends the unattenuated propagation length; above 1 (with a₀²>ε_B), nonlinear plasma current damps the wave.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how strong superluminal electromagnetic X-mode waves propagate and get disrupted in magnetized pair plasmas, and what this implies for fast radio burst durations and their escape from surrounding plasma.

Method: Analytical theory combined with kinetic simulations of induced (nonlinear) scattering, introducing a restoring frequency Ω_B from the background magnetic field, characterized by the regime parameter ε_B = a₀ω_B/ω₀ alongside ε_p = a₀ω_p/ω₀.

Result: For ε_B<1 the wave propagates until induced scattering disrupts it; when the restoring frequency Ω_B exceeds the scattering rate, the unattenuated length grows from ~ε_p^(-2/3) to ~ε_B^(1/2)/ε_p wavelengths. For a₀²>ε_B>1, the plasma current becomes nonlinear and the wave is damped while riding a motional electric field, so one half-cycle appears attenuated.

Conclusion: The magnetic field and its induced restoring frequency govern wave propagation regimes, which have implications for the duration of fast radio bursts and whether they can escape their surroundings.

Abstract: The interaction of strong ($a_0>1$), superluminal X-mode electromagnetic waves with magnetized pair plasma falls into two regimes set by $\varepsilon_{\rm B}\equiv a_0ω_{\rm B}/ω_0$, the ratio of the wave strength parameter to its frequency, in units of cyclotron frequency. For $\varepsilon_{\rm B}<1$ the wave propagates until induced scattering disrupts it. Using analytical theory and kinetic simulations, we show that the background magnetic field introduces a restoring frequency $Ω_{\rm B}$ to scattering; when $Ω_{\rm B}$ exceeds the scattering rate, the unattenuated length grows from $\simeq\varepsilon_{\rm p}^{-2/3}$ to $\simeq\varepsilon_{\rm B}^{1/2}/\varepsilon_{\rm p}$ wavelengths ($\varepsilon_{\rm p}\equiv a_0ω_{\rm p}/ω_0$; $ω_{\rm p}$ is the plasma frequency). When $a_0^2>\varepsilon_{\rm B}>1$, the plasma current is nonlinear, and the wave is damped while riding a motional electric field, so one half-cycle appears attenuated. This framework has implications for the duration of fast radio bursts and whether they can escape their surroundings.

</details>

### [178] [At last, an explicit singular value decomposition for electrical impedance tomography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35217)
*William R B Lionheart,Michael Pidcock,Cristiana Sebu*

Main category: physics.med-ph

TL;DR: The normal operator of the linearized EIT problem on a disk (radially symmetric Schrödinger potential, Dirichlet-to-Neumann data) is diagonalized via the finite Hilbert transform on an interval; since the operator is non-compact, its spectrum is continuous.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the spectral structure of the linearized EIT normal operator is useful for analyzing the related inverse problem, but existing treatments typically assume compactness or ignore the non-compact, continuous-spectrum case.

Method: Transform the normal operator into the adjacent-interval finite Hilbert transform, exploiting its known spectral theory, which includes a commuting differential operator, to obtain an explicit diagonalization.

Result: A diagonalization of the normal operator is obtained for radially symmetric Schrödinger potentials on the disk. The operator is shown to be non-compact with continuous spectrum.

Conclusion: The linearized EIT normal operator with radial symmetry admits a Hilbert-transform-based diagonalization, and its non-compact, continuous spectrum must be accounted for in the analysis.

Abstract: We derive a diagonalization of the normal operator of the linearized electrical impedance tomography (EIT) problem on the disk in the case of a radially symmetric Schrödinger potential with Dirichlet-to-Neumann data. The diagonalization is achieved by transforming the normal operator to the adjacent interval finite Hilbert transform which has a known spectral theory including a commuting differential operator. The normal operator in this case is not compact and the spectrum continuous.

</details>

### [179] [Graph-Convolutional Reinforcement Learning for Finite-Time Stabilization of a Chaotic Chua Circuit via Unified Flexible Terminal Sliding-Mode Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35613)
*Hooman Fatoorehchi,Majid Roohi,Rasmus Kold,Matthias Ehrhardt,Andreas Basse-O'Connor,Sebastian Nis Bay Villadsen,Philip L. Fosbøl*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: A graph convolutional actor-critic reinforcement learning framework tunes a terminal sliding-mode controller for finite-time stabilization of chaotic dynamics in a physical Chua circuit, improving error metrics by ~56-65% over a reference method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual tuning of sliding-mode controllers for chaotic circuits is difficult and labor-intensive; the authors aim to reduce reliance on hand tuning while preserving the robustness and analytical stability structure of the controller.

Method: The Chua circuit is modeled physically (capacitor voltages, inductor current, component parameters). A unified, flexible terminal sliding-mode controller with state-dependent nonlinear exponents (avoiding singularities near equilibrium) achieves finite-time stabilization under uncertainties, disturbances, and input saturation. Controller parameters are optimized via an actor-critic RL scheme using a graph convolutional network that exploits the circuit's coupling structure, with learned parameters constrained to theoretically admissible stability ranges.

Result: Numerical experiments show rapid suppression of chaotic oscillations and finite-time convergence of state and sliding variables under input-saturation limits. Compared to a reference method, IAE, ISE, ITAE, and ITSE are reduced by 56.27%, 56.40%, 61.69%, and 64.70%, respectively.

Conclusion: Graph-based reinforcement learning can effectively tune parameters while retaining the robustness and analytical structure of the underlying sliding-mode controller.

Abstract: This paper introduces a graph convolutional reinforcement learning framework for the finite-time stabilization of chaotic dynamics in a physical Chua circuit. The circuit is modeled directly in terms of capacitor voltages, inductor current, and physical component parameters. This preserves the connection between the mathematical model and the underlying electrical system. A unified, flexible terminal sliding-mode controller is developed to achieve stabilization within a finite time in the presence of uncertainties in the lumped model, external disturbances, and bounded control inputs. The controller incorporates state-dependent nonlinear exponents to provide flexible convergence characteristics while avoiding singular terms near equilibrium. To minimize dependence on manual controller tuning, the admissible controller parameters are optimized using an actor-critic reinforcement learning scheme with a graph convolutional network that leverages the circuit dynamics' coupling structure. The learned parameters are constrained to the ranges required by the theoretical stability conditions. Numerical experiments demonstrate the rapid suppression of chaotic oscillations and the finite-time convergence of the state and sliding variables while respecting the prescribed input-saturation limits. Compared to a reference control method, the proposed approach reduces the IAE, ISE, ITAE, and ITSE by 56.27\,\%, 56.40\,\%, 61.69\,\%, and 64.70\,\%, respectively. These results suggest that graph-based reinforcement learning can effectively tune parameters while retaining the robustness and analytical structure of the underlying sliding-mode controller.

</details>

### [180] [Scattering for the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and the Steklov spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34588)
*Jeffrey Galkowski,Marcello Malagutti,Ruoyu P. T. Wang*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: Develops scattering theory for Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps on asymptotically conic domains with Lipschitz interior, and uses it to approximate Steklov-type eigenvalues on curvilinear polygons via a Dirac operator with corner scattering transmission coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Steklov-type eigenvalue problems on domains with corners need a way to capture corner-local behavior; existing local asymptotics lack a scattering framework for DtN maps on unbounded, asymptotically conic domains that are only Lipschitz inside a compact set.

Method: Proves a limiting absorption principle and existence of the scattering matrix for DtN maps on asymptotically conic, unbounded domains that are Lipschitz inside a compact set and extend to infinity; applies this to build a Dirac operator on the boundary of a curvilinear polygon whose edge-to-edge transmission coefficients come from the corner scattering matrices.

Result: Eigenvalues of Steklov-type problems on curvilinear polygons are well approximated by eigenvalues of this boundary Dirac operator, with error optimal (up to an arbitrary loss in the power of the eigenvalue) among all asymptotic formulas using only local boundary information.

Conclusion: Corner scattering data from DtN maps provides the sharp local model for Steklov eigenasymptotics on curvilinear polygons.

Abstract: We develop scattering theory for Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) maps on asymptotically conic, unbounded domains $Ω\subset \mathbb{R}^d$, that are only Lipschitz inside a compact set and whose boundary extends to infinity. Specifically, we prove a limiting absorption principle and demonstrate existence of the scattering matrix.
  As an application of the theory, we show that the eigenvalues of Steklov-type problems on curvilinear polygons, $\mathcal{P}$, are well approximated by the eigenvalues of a Dirac operator on $\partial\mathcal{P}$ whose transmission coefficients between edges meeting at a corner are given by the scattering matrix for DtN maps on asymptotically conic domains associated to the corners. Up to an arbitrary loss in the power of the eigenvalue, the error in the approximation is optimal among all possible asymptotics that use only local information about $\partial\mathcal{P}$.

</details>
